  • 😎Extra Concepts

    • Resolution, Bill, Motion

      1. Bill - Legislative Proposal
      2. Motion - Not Necessarily Put to Vote
      3. Resolution - It is necessarily Put to Vote

    • Timing of No of Parliamentary Sessions in a Year

      • Summary

      1. No Mention of How Many Minimum No of Session in a Year in the Constitution
      2. The Constitution Only Says that there shall be no TIME GAP OF NOT MORE THAN 6 Months
      3. As a Conventional Practise 3 Sessions are Done. There is no Mention in the Constitution itself
        1. Budget Session
        2. Monsoon Session
        3. Winter Sessions
      4. Recess : Break between Two Sessions
      5. Adjournment :
        1. Temporary Breaks in Between the Session
        2. Temporary Break : Definite Break
          1. Hour
          2. Days
          3. Week
        3. Done by the Chairman of the House
      6. Adjournment Sine Di :
        1. Temporary Breaks in Between the Session
        2. Temporary Break : Indefinite Break
        3. Presiding Officer will pass adjournment Sine Die, when all the tasks are over
        4. This Serves as an Indication to the President that work of parliament is over, taking cue from Adjournment Sine Di President calls for Prorogation of House

    • Art 100 Article → When there is No Specific Majority Mentioned ?

      Whenever there is no Specific Majority is Mentioned, it has to considered as a Simple Majority

    • Analogy

      1. Batch of Students on 5 Aug = 100 - ABSOLUTE ATTENDANCE - Which Subject to Start with ?
      2. Withdrawn on 15 Aug = 20
      3. Effective Students = 80 - EFFECTIVE ATTENDANCE - Whether Teacher is Good or Not ?
      4. on 15 Jan 22 = Present in Class = 50 & ask u the question - Can we take 15 minutes Extra
        1. 20 - Didn't wanna Answer
        2. 30 - Wanted to Answer the Question - SIMPLE - PRESENT & VOTING -

    • Parliamentary Majority

      • Basic Rules

        1. Tougher Decision Requires Tougher Majority
        2. Parliamentary Majority is Who to Ask What Questions ?

      • Usage of Parliamentary Majority in ⇒ Part + Legislature

        1. State - 28 - SLA + SLC
        2. UT -3 - UT LA

      • Types of Votes

        • Yes
        • No
        • Abstain - Present & Not Vote
          1. Why Allowed to Not Vote ? Explained by Example
          2. Ex - Voting of Farm Laws in LS
          3. You are a BJP MP from Punjab - You have Objection to Farm Laws
            1. Vote Yes - Proves Loyalty to BJP but Betray the Constituency
            2. Vote No - will uphold the trust of Constituency but BJP will be Betrayed
          4. Hence Solution is - Silence i.e Freedom of Speech is Freedom of Silence
          5. Example : China abstains from many votes in UNSC against Pakistan
          6. Example : Russia abstains from Many votes in UNSC against Iran/Iraq

      • Imp Note

        All these Terms haven’t been used in the Constitution, it only puts down the Procedure

        The above terms are our Innovation

      • Tough 💪 to Easy 😌 - Use More than 1/2 of Figure

        Note : Simple is Sub Set of Effective is Sub Set of Absolute Majority

        • Absolute Majority - Political Majority

          1. Used Only & Only to Establish Majority in Lower Houses, Hence used in Lok Sabha & Vidhan Sabha By Extension being used in Local Self Government

          2. Figure : > 1/2 of Total Strength of That Specific House

          3. Used at time of Govt Formation

            LOK SABHA

            1. LS : > 1/2 * 545 = 273
            2. 545 → 543 ( Anglo Indian Nomination Removed )
            3. Whichever Party gets Majority in LS decide who gets to be the Minister ; that doesn't mean minister will be necessarily from LS it can be from RS
            4. Current Lok Sabha - 17th Lok Sabha 2019-2024
            5. BJP Coalition - NDA National Democratic Alliance but useless due to BJP having absolute majority of 303
        • Effective Majority - To Remove Presiding Officers of Houses
          • Presiding Officers
            1. LS - Sp + Dy Sp
            2. SLA - SP + Dy Sp
            3. UT - Sp + Dy Sp
            4. SLC - Sp + Dy Sp
            5. RS
              1. RS - Vice Chaiperson
              2. PARTIALLY REMOVE ONLY → RS - Chairperson ( VP of India
                1. Here Both Houses Participate
                2. First in RS Removed then LS Removed
          • Effective Majority depends on Effective Strength
            1. Effective Strength = Total Strength - 3 ( Death+Disqualified+ Resigned )
          • Feature : > 1/2 of ES(House)
          • Example
            1. To Remove Lok Sabha Speaker
            2. Current Strenghth of LS - 543
            3. Dead MP - 20
            4. Resigned MP - 3
            5. Disqualified MP - 20
            6. Total DDR - 43
            7. Effective Strength ⇒ LS-DDR ⇒ 543-43 = 500
            8. Effective Majority = > 1/2 * 500 ; Hence Effective Majority is 251
        • Simple Majority - Jitne Aaye Hain Unse Poocho, Simple
          1. Used in All Houses - LS, RS, SLA, SLC, UT LA
          2. Purposes
            1. Pass Laws which are NOT Constitutional Amendments
            2. For all Parliamentary Motion
              1. No Confidence Motion - Koi Bhi Emotion Motion - Bas Notion Rahe Naam ke Saamne
          3. Hence, Simple Majority - Default Majority - If not mentioned then Default Majority it is
          4. Figure : > 1/2 of Present & Voting

      • Easy 😄 to Tough 🤯 - Use More than 2/3 of Figure

        • using a Higher Fraction cause it is Symbolic that it will affect the
          1. Federal Structure
          2. Institutions
          3. CS Impact
          4. Functionaries
        • Special Majority 1 - Resolution/Permission Majority
          1. Only in RS/SLA
          2. Resolution / Permission Majorities : colloquial term
            1. RS has some special powers like
              1. authorise the parliament to make a law on something that is state list
              2. add or subtract an all india service
              3. certain emergencies to approve
              4. Requesting SLC by State Govt
          3. Figure : > 2/3 of Present & Voting
        • Special Majority 2 - Only in LS & RS

          1. Only used in LS & RS ; not in State/UT

          2. Usage :

            1. To Pass a Certain Type of Amendment
            2. To Remove Certain Functionaries
              1. SC Judge, HC Judge, CAG etc

          3. Basic Funda : कम से कम इतना वोट तो आना ही चहिये

          4. Figure :

          5. Example to Remove a SC Judge

            • Example 1 - Judge Not Removed

              1. Only Par LS/RS ( Separately will sit and do ) by Spl Maj 2
              2. LS Total Strength 545
              3. on 15 Jan 2022 : 360 MP Present
              4. Only and Only 270 MP - Have decided to Vote
              5. Other 90 - Abstain
              6. Hence P&V : 270

              Special Majority 2 is :

              1. 2/3 of P&V = 2/3* 270 = 180
              2. this 180 SHOULD BE > 1/2 of TS of HS (LS) 545
              3. 1/2 of 545 - 273
              4. Hence 180 is not greater than 270
              5. Hence it will not pass

            • Example 2 - Judge Removed

              1. TS LS : 545
              2. MP Present : 500
              3. P&V : 480
              4. Hence > 2/3 of 480 = 320
              5. 320 > 1/2 of TS LS
              6. 320 > 273
              7. Hence Judge will be removed
        • Special Majority 3 - Extension to Special Majority 2
          1. Only used for ONE PURPOSE - For a Specific Type of Constitutional Amendment
          • Step 1
            1. Only & Only in LS & RS
              1. In LS - like Special Majority 2
              2. In RS - like Special Majority 2
              3. Once LS & RS SEPARATELY pass the Bill
              4. This Bill will be sent to all 31 Legislatures
          • Step 2 - Sate Ratification Process
            1. Across All SLA (28) inc UT W.L (3) = 31 Legislatures
            2. Out of these 31 Legislatures → 1/2 of these 31 i.e 16 Legislatures → will have to pass the same law using simple majority
        • Special Majority 4 - Only in LS & RS
          1. used in LS & RS Separately ; not in State Legislatures
          2. Usage : Only & Only to remove Hon. President of India
          3. Figure : > 2/3 of that Specific House
            1. LS : > 2/3 of LS 545 = 273
            2. RS : > 2/3 of RS 245 = 123

  • Majorities in Indian Constitution 2

    | Majorities | Remarks | Keep in Mind | | --- | --- | --- | | Simple | 1/2 of Present & Voting | How Many Pre & How Many Voting | | Absolute | 1/2 of Total of House | Remember Total Strength of RS & LS | | Effective | 1/2 of Effective (Total - Death Disqualify Resign) | How Many Death Disqualify & Reisgned | | Amend Constitution | Federal Strcuture NOT Altered NOT <2/3 of P&V) && (1/2+1 of Total Strength of Each HouseFederal Strcuture AlteredNOT <2/3 of P&V) && (1/2+1 of Total Strength of Each House&&>1/2 of State Ratification by Simple Majority | | | Impeachment of Prez | NOT <2/3 of P&V | |

    Type of Majorities in India

    • Simple Majority

      1. Definition : It is the Majority of Members, Present as well as Voting
      2. You need to Know : How Many PRESENT ? && How Many VOTING ?

    • Absolute Majority

      1. Definition : It is the Majority of Total Membership of House
      2. Just Half of Total Strength + 1

    • Effective Majority

      1. Definition : It is the Majority of Effective Membership of the House.
      2. Effective Membership = Total Membership ( Strength of House ) - Vacancies ( Death Disqualify Resign )
      3. Total Membership = Total Strength of House
      4. Vacancies = Death + Disqualification + Resignation
      💡 ***Absolute = Effective*** → when ***Vacancy = 0***

    • Special Majority (4)

      1. Apart from above mentioned Majorities i.e Simple, Absolute & Effective Majorities. All other Majorities are denoted by a Common Name i.e Special Majority
      2. However, There are Different Types of Special Majority under Constitution for Different Purposes
      • Constitutional Amendment - Has Two Conditions
        1. Condition 1 - There Should be a Yes by - NOT LESS than 2/3rd of Members Present & Voting
        2. Condition 2 - Absolute Majority of the House
        3. Example : Satisfying Both Conditions
          1. Total Members = 543
          2. Present = 500
          3. P&V = 450
          4. in Favour = 300
          5. in Against = 150
        4. Example : Not Satisfying Both Conditions
          1. Total Members = 543
          2. Present = 500
          3. P&V = 300
          4. in Favour = 200
          5. in Against = 100
        5. State Ratification Needed in Amendments which alter the Federal Structures of the Constitution
      • Impeachment of President - Has One Condition
        1. Condition 1 - Yes by - NOT LESS than 2/3rd of Total Membership of House
        2. Example :
          1. LS : 2/3 of 543
          2. RS : 2/3 of 245

      1. Placed in Back of Constitution
      2. To Maintain Continuity & Organisation
      3. It is like a Appendix

    • Schedule 07 - Union State & Concurrent List

      1. It Provides for 3 Lists
      2. It is example of Division of Powers

      The Lists are

      1. Union List - 100 - Power of Parliament

        Defence, Home Affairs, Foreign Affairs, Insurance, Banking

      2. State List - 61 - Normally within the Power of State Legislature

        Police, Law & Order, Health

      3. Concurrent List - 52 - Concurrent Power of Both Parliament & State Legislature

        Forest, Wild Life

      Total Subject Matters : 213

  • Part 20 (Art 368) - Amendment of the Constitution

    Power & Procedure of Parliament to amend the Constitution

    • Some Important Questions

      • Who can Introduce a Bill ?

        • Either a Minister
        • Private Member

      • Do they have to take Prior Permission from President ?

        No Not under Art 368

      • Do they have to take Permission from Chairman of the House ?

        Yes, a Mere Formality

        in LS : Speaker

        in RS : VP

      • Can it be Introduced in any house ?

        Yes, in either LS or RS

    • Types of Constitutional Amendments

      • Bill tries to Amend - Non Federal Provisions - Doesn’t Change Power Balance b/w Centre & State
        • Procedure
          1. Bill has to be Introduced (by any MP) - Subject Matter is to Amend the Constitution
          2. Introduced in & Passes to → LS/RS || RS/LS
          3. It should gain Special Majority
            1. </ 2/3rd Members Present & Voting
            2. Absolute Majority of House Needed
          4. President has to Give the Assent ( No Veto ) - It was made mandatory for the President to give assent to the bill by 24th CAA, 1971
      • Bill tries to Amend - Federal Provisions - Changes Power Balance b/w Centre & State
        • Procedure
          1. Bill has to be Introduced (by any MP) - Subject Matter is to Amend the Constitution
          2. Introduced in & Passes to → LS/RS || RS/LS
          3. It should gain Special Majority in both the Houses Separately
            1. </ 2/3rd Members Present & Voting
            2. Absolute Majority of House Needed
          4. Then it is Sent for Ratification by the State Legislature
            1. <1/2 of State Legislatures Accept it, its Enough
            2. i.e 28 States - 14 State Legislatures have to Ratify them
              1. State Legislature - Simple Majority
              2. State Legislative - Simple Majority
          5. President has to Give the Assent ( No Veto ) - It was made mandatory for the President to give assent to the bill by 24th CAA, 1971
        • Example
          • Art 246
          • Schedule 7

  • Basics of Polity & Indian Constitution

    • 4 Golden Rules of Indian Polity

      • 1 Democratic Mandate

        1. Mandate : Authority given to a Person to do something
        2. Analogy - Eligibility of Atish Sir to Teach Polity, is the Mandate of Atish Sir
        3. Rule Says
          1. in Order to Perform any advanced or higher democratic function one must have some form (direct or indirect) of a Democratic Mandate - Corollary also true.
          2. D(f) → D(m) && Corollary 0> XD(f)→ XD(m)
        • Examples
          1. to be a Minister you have to be a MP - Simplest Application D(m)
            1. Either at time of becoming MP
            2. but within 6 months you have to be a MP either e/n && either in LS (IDD DE) / RS (IDD IDE)
            3. Example : Manmohan Singh, Arun Jaitley, S Jayshankar
          2. Rajya Sabha MP → elected by respective SLA → that SLA - SLA(MLA)e must be elected && SLA(MLA)n nominated not allowed
          3. Prez Elected by → (LS + RS + SLA)e → Nominated Members of RS & SLA & LS → DON’T Participate in a Presidential Election
          4. Ordinance Powers of Centre(Art 123) and State(Art 213)
            1. Ordinance is a Temporary Law drafted by Council of Minister Directly Sent to President for Signature when Parliament is not in Session
            2. The Term “Temporary Law” is the Application of Democratic Mandate
            3. CoM - Draft the Ordinance - in Absence of Parliament, CoM is the best Next Mandate
            4. President - because he has been democratically elected by LS & RS & SLA
          5. Advice & Suggestion given by Bodies like CAG etc → are NOT Binding → cuz they are appointed authority and not elected → Hence don’t have the mandate
          6. Even Recommendation of Parliamentary Committee → is NOT Binding → cuz it will violate the democratic mandate of Parliament

      • 2 Federal Balance

        1. About Centre and State
        • Analogy
          1. Family Dynamics → Younger and Elder Sibling → Indian Family Scenarios ELDER SIBLING (CENTRE) have the FR to Dominate YOUNGER SIBLINGS (State)
          2. Make a Face && Threaten to Parents → Toffee to make feel good → Superficial Condolence → Toffee Wrapper has Stones 😂
        • Steps
          • Step 1 : C > S ( Centre will do something to overpower the States )
          • Step 2 : Candy to States to Compensate the States
          • Step 3 : Hollow Candy (Hollow Appeasement)
        • Examples - States ka Katna Tay Hai
          • Territory
            1. C>S → All Territorial Rules power are with Centre and State has no say in the matter whatsoever
            2. Candy → President refers the Law to concerned State Legislature for their opinion
            3. Hollow Candy → Centre has to ask, but not bind to agree or implement their view
          • State Legislative Council
            1. C>S → Parliament authorises the creation of SLC in the state
            2. Candy → Parliament can only create SLC, if State SLA has requested for a SLC (Special Majority 2)
            3. Hollow Candy → Just because SLA has asked, C will not give SLC to S.
              1. Ex : Sikkim asking for SLC since last 2 decades
              2. Ex : WB Bengal asking for SLC for safe passage for key functionaries
          • All India Services - IAS + IPS + IFoS
            1. C>S
              1. Recruited and Removed by Centre but work for States & States pay for your training
              2. States didn't want this because they had their PSC with them
              3. Recruiting a Bunch of Officers and compulsory sending them to the states ; states don’t have the choice
            2. Candy → Increase / Decrease of AIS, LS can do it, but RS has to authorise LS for the same ( as RS is representative of states) & this will affect States as well
            3. Hollow Candy → Just because RS has said yes, its not sure if LS will accept it or pass it.
          • Appointment of Governor
            1. C>S : Recruited by (C) && Removed by (C) but Governor HAS to work at (S)
            2. Candy → as per constitution Governor has more discretionary powers than President
            3. Hollow Candy → Governor has been appointed by the president on advice of CoM but whenever governor uses his discretionary power against the Centre ; centre by means of president can remove him.

      • Prelude to 3 & 4

        1. Everybody believes that they are powerful - Origin of Separation of Powers (SOP)

      • 3 Separation of Powers (2)

        • Inter SOP - Between L ↔ E ↔ J
          1. Conduct of Supreme Court Judge cannot be discussed on the floor of parliament
        • Intra SOP - Inside LEJ → L (RS/LS) && E (EE-m && PE-b) && J (SC HC LC)
          1. Except for Ministers, LS & RS MP can only sit in the houses they belong

      • 4 Checks & Balances (2)

        • Inter C&B - Between L ↔ E ↔ J
          1. Supreme Court Judge can be removed only by Parliament
        • Intra C&B - Inside LEJ → L (RS/LS) && E (EE-m && PE-b) && J (SC HC LC)
          1. Judgement of SC is binding on all Lower Courts

    • What is a Constitution

      Countries, Political Parties, Corporates have Constitution

      Constituion is Necessary but not Sufficient

      A Constitution is the most basic but highest Law of the Land. All the Other Laws in the Country in order to be valid must be as per constitution.

      A Constitution provides a framework for the Governance of the Country. It gives the structure of State, Its organs of state, their Functions, Powers and Limitation on these powers.

      👨‍🏫 Organs of State Are
      1. Legislature → Legislate Make Laws
      2. Executive → Execute & Implement
      3. Judiciary → Interpret Judiciary & Deliever Justice

      • A Constitution Establishes a Legal Relationship called Citizenship between State and Its People. A Democractic Constitution protects the Rights of Minorities. A Constitution for countries Like India is not just a Legal Document but also a Socio Economic Document because it contains the Values, Dreams and Aspiration of People of India

      A Constitution Ensures that the basic values of a country are not threatened in moments of temporary passion.

      False - A & D && True - B & C

      True - A B D E && False - C

    • Does having a Constitution Ensure Constitutionalism ?

      It is more difficult to run a constitution than to frame one. Constitutionalism refers to the degree or extent to which a country is governed according to its constitution.

      A country may have a written constitution but little constitutionlaism where as a country with unwritten constitution may have high constitutionalism

    • Written Constitution & Unwritten Constitution

      An Unwritten Constitution is one whose provisions have not been codified into a Single Legal Document.

      Example : UK, Israel, Newzeland

      In India SC and HC by their power of Judicial Review Ensure that India has high levels of constitutioanlism

    • Judicial Review (from USA)

      In India the Power of Judicical Review is Availaible Implicitly to SC in Art 32 and to HC under Art 226 but not to Sub Ordinate Courts

      Power of Judicial Review Means the Power of Court (SC&HC) to declare any law policy or order (whether central or state) as null and void if it contravenes any provision of constitution to the extent of such contravention ( Jis Had Tak Galat Hai Sirf us had tak, not poora)

      SC & HC → Can Declare Null and Void of both Parliament and State. Can be done Simultaneously in Both Together like one in CG and One in Delhi in SC.

      When the court declares a Law as Null and Void it does not suggest any alternative or improvement

      While Exersicing Power of Judicial Review the Courts Follow Following Principals.

      👨‍🏫 Importance of Comprehension

      Woman without her man, is nothing. Woman without her, man is nothing.

      1. Principal 1

        If a Law or Policy has Two Interpretations such that First Interpretation makes it constitutionally valid and the other makes it invalid, Judiciary Shall Consider first Interpretation and declare the Law or Policy as Valid

        Thus the Presumption is in the Favour of Constitutionality

      2. Principal 2

        👨‍🏫 1. A Proposed Law is a Bill 2. A Passed and Assented(Prez) Bill is a Act 3. Acts come into force in India by Three Ways (Direct Assent, After “x” no of days, on the date specified in the Gazette of India)

        Manmohan Singh in power passed a Act called Whistle Blowers Protection Act, it read that it wil be implemented as in the date specified by Govt of India. Modi hasnt implemented yet

        👨‍🏫 **Gazette** of Govt of India is like a Magazine of Govt of India

        Judicial Review is not applied on the Laws and Rules which are not Operational

      3. Principal 3

        👨‍🏫 Suo Motu - Khud Se - By Self

        Example: In IPC, 1860 && Sec 375, Sec 376

        Generally SC or HC examine a Law or Policy for its constitutionality only on a petition or reference filed by someone and not Suo Motu

        The Power of Judicial Review is Considered to be most Significant Power of Supreme Court w/o which court will not Enjoy Status and Prestige that it currently enjoys

        Considering its importance SC decalred Power of Judicial Review as Part of Basic Structure of Constitution

    • What is a State ?

      A State is a Poiticial Legal Term which refers to a defines Territory, Population, Govt and is Sovereign

      The Terms Govt and State Should not be used Interchangebly. Govt is one of the components of State. because Govt is the Instrument to Govern the State. State is Permanent while Govt is Sub to Change.

      Difference Between Nation and State from International Relatiosn

    • Democracy

      1. Cracy - Rule & Demo - People or Population
      2. It refers to a Political System in which people practise self rule. In Modern times, such a direct democracy is not practically feasible and therefore indirect or Representative Democracy is practised
      3. In this system, People elect their reprensetatives after every few years and these representatives then govern the country
      4. Merits of Democracy
        1. Citizens elect their Government.
        2. Citizens can change governments if unhappy with gocts policies, actions or inactions.
        3. Govt is accountable to the people.
        4. Govt works for welfare of people and hence a democratic govt is also called as a Welfare Govts
        5. Citizens Enjoy Basic Rights and Liberties
      5. Demerits of Democracy

        1. Political & Policy Instability

        2. Good and Bold but Unpopular Decision may not be taken. Ex: Reforming Reservation Policy is needed in India

        3. Slow Decision Making

        4. Minorities May be Ignored

        5. In Democracy, there is no assurance that best persons come to power because citizens do not caste vote only on the grounds of merit

          Democracy often goves Birth to Demagogues (A Political Leader is not Necessarily Leader but is good in Mobilising Emotions)

    • Theory of Separation of Power

      1. Was Given by a French Philosophers Montesquieu

      2. This Theory Proposes that the Powers of State should be divided into three distincy and separte organs

        The Objective of the Theory is to Prevent Concentration of Powers and to prevent the state from working in Authortitarian or Dictatorial Manner

      3. US was the first constitution to adopt this Theory

      4. Constitution of India has also adopted this theory. However India being a Parliamentary Democracy this theory has not been adopted completely like that of US Constitution (Presidential System)

      5. Art 50 (DPSP), which requires the state to separate Judicial Functions from Executive Office. Accordingly Parliament Enacted CrPC (Criminal Procedure Code), 1973 to separate Judicial Functions from District Officers.

        However, District Officers still enjoy certain executive magisterial powers like

        1. Insepction of Jail
        2. Issuing Prhobitoty Orders under Sec 144 of CrPC
        3. Granting Parole to Prisoners (Parole is Temporary Leave from Jail)

    • Doctrine of Checks and Balances

      This concept is a Natural Extension of Concept of Spearation of Powers. It says that every organ of state must exersice, some checks and balances over other two organs of state

      This ensures a Balance of Powers amongst three organs and that none of the organ oversteps its constitutional limits

    • Federalism

      It is a Political System in which the powers of state are divided into two levels of govt.

      First Level - Union / Central / Federal / National

      Second Level - State / Region / Province / Canton (Switezerland)

      Federalism is necessary in countries which are large and diverse, it allows people of different communities to stay together as a nation without the fear of one culture being imposed on others

      Features of Federalism Are

      1. Govt at Atleast Two Levels

      2. Division of Powers and Functions between Levels of Government (Union List, State List, Concurrent List)

      3. Division of Revenue and Taxation Powers

      4. Independent Judiciary for the 29 states to resolve dispute between (State and State or State and Centre)

      5. Apart from Lower House or Lok Sabha. There should be a Bi Cameralism → Upper House - Rajya Sabha (Ind) && Senate (USA) → For States Voice

      6. A Written and Rigid Constitution

      7. Federal Provisions cannot be amended by one level of government unilaterally

        Constittuion of India does not use the word Federalism in Constitution, it is because of Centrifugal Tendencies Prevailing in Some parts of India at the Time of Independence

        Art 1 Describes India i.e Bharat as a Union of States rather than Federation of States

  • Historical Development to Constitution

    Link to the Summary Sheet

    • Regulating Act 1773 - COD & Central Administration

      Philosophical

      1. The first step towards Central administration
      2. Political and administrative functions of East India Company was regulated, British Crown interference started
      3. To regulate the administration of the East India Company

      Features

      1. Governor of Bengal (Warren Hastings) was made governor-general of Bengal + 4 members executive Council
      2. The Governor of Bombay and governor of Madras were made subordinate to him
      3. Supreme Court established in 1774 in Calcutta = 1 Chief Judge + 3 Judges
      4. The Court of Directors looked after revenue, civil, military affairs and reported to British Parliament/crown/government
      5. Ban on bribe and personal trade by East India Company officials

    • Amending Act of 1781 - SC Power

      • Supreme Court Jurisdiction

        1. Supreme Court jurisdiction was removed

          ON

          1. Governor general + his Council (in official capacity)
          2. Servants of East India Company

          FROM

          1. Revenue matters
          2. Collection of revenue

        2. Supreme Court jurisdiction remained in

          1. Inhabitants of Calcutta
          2. Muslims Sharia law
          3. Hindu Hindu law

      • Governor General Council Function Changes

        Gov Gen of Ben + Council (4) - GG Council → make laws Hear Appeal of provincial courts

    • Pitt's India Act 1784 - Dual Government

      • Dual Government was formed
        • Commercial matters - Court of directors (COD)
        • Political matters - Board of Control (BOC) - supervise and direct CMR affairs
      • First time Sovereign authority of crown over EIC

    • Act of 1786

      1. Governor general had the power to override decision of counsel in special cases with his responsibility
      2. He was made the commander in chief of East India Company Army

    • Charter Act of 1793 - First Charter && GG Structure

      1. Governor generals overriding Council power extended
        1. Future governor general
        2. Governor of other Presidency
      2. Governor general of Bengal was given more power over governor of Bombay and Madras
      3. Trade Monopoly was extended for 20 years
      4. Commander in chief was not from governor general Council
      5. Board of Control fees and salary was to be given from Indian revenue

    • Charter Act of 1813 - Transitionary Act && Ban on Monopoly

      1. Trade Monopoly in India ended Tea and Trade Monopoly with China remained
      2. Sovereignty of British Crown over EIC Territory
      3. Christian Missionary promoted to educate and awaken locals
      4. Western education was introduced
      5. Local government could authorise
        1. Impose tax
        2. Punish for non collection of taxes

    • Charter Act of 1833

      1. It was the first step towards centralisation
      2. Governor general of Bengal was made
        1. Governor General of India - William Bentick
        2. Was given
          1. Civil military power
          2. Executive legislative power
      3. Of legislative power of Governor General of Bombay and Madras was reduced
      4. Before and after charter act 1833
        1. Before - regulation
        2. After - ATS
      5. Company's commercial operation was ceased company was only administrative body now
      6. Civil services was made open to Indians but COD negative

    • Charter act of 1853

      1. Governor general Council function was separated into
        1. Legislative
          1. 6 Legislative Councillors Appointed
            1. 4 Indians From - Madras Bombay Agra Calcutta
            2. 2 Foreigners
          2. Indian Central Legislative Council similar to British parliament in London was made
        2. Executive
          1. Civil services was made open to Indians by free and fair competition by Recommendation of Macaulay committee of 1854
      2. Companies rule extended by British Crown until further notice

    • Government of India Act 1858

      1. Important points to note
        1. Government act
        2. Start of crown rule
        3. Just after the revolt of 1857
      2. Companies power over (civil military revenue) was removed, now it would be in the name of Her Majesty
      3. Governor general of India was made viceroy of India → Lord Canning
      4. Secretary of state was sent to India as
        1. Indian minister of parliament of London
        2. He was a member of British cabinet
        3. He was answerable to British Parliament
        4. SOS had his own 15 member Council
      5. Dual government was ended

    • Sequence of GOI Act 1858, 1919, 1935,

    • Indian Council Act 1861

      1. Viceroy nominated Indians to viceroys Legislative Council, Indian inclusion was intended after revolt of 1857
        1. 1862
          1. Raja of Banaras
          2. Raja of Patiala
          3. Sir Dinkar Rao
      2. Decentralisation took place
        1. Legislative power of Bombay Madras was restored
        2. Provincial Legislative Council was created
          1. Bengal
          2. Punjab
          3. NWFP
      3. Portfolio system was introduced in Viceroy council in 1859
      4. Ordinance power by Viceroy without concurrence of counsel was allowed

    • Sequence of ICA 1861 of, 1892, 1909

    • Indian Council Act 1892

      1. Increased non official members in in Central and provincial legislative councils but not in majority
      2. Rights of Legislative Council was increased like budget decision they could ask question but not reject it
      3. Nomination to Legislative Council was introduced
        1. At Centre - by viceroy of India by recommendation of Bombay Chamber of Commerce provincial Legislative Council of state governments
        2. At state level by district board University and trade Association Zamindar noncommercial Chambers
      4. Indirect election was introduced to fill seats of centre and state legislative council

    • Indian Council Act 1909 - Morley Minto reforms

      1. Background
        1. Morley was Secretary of state
        2. Minto was viceroy of India
      2. Size of Legislative Council was increased
        1. Centre 16 to 60
        2. State - vary from state to state
      3. Changes in Legislative Council
        1. Central - retained official majority
        2. Provincial - non official majority was allowed
      4. Changes in Powers of Legislative Council
        1. Allowed supplementary question
        2. Allowed to move resolution on budget
      5. Indians in viceroy's executive Council was held at first person to be added was Satyendra Prasad Sinha as law member
      6. Separate electorate was introduced, this had two features
        1. Communal electorate
          1. Hence Minto is called father of communal electorate
          2. Communal electorate meant Muslim member of Council by Muslim voters
        2. This legalised communalism

    • Government of India Act 1919 - Montague Chelmsford reform

      1. Background
        1. SOS - Montague
        2. Viceroy - Transport
      2. It was introduced on 20 August 1917
      3. Relaxed Central Control over Provinces Separate and Demarcated Subjects and Budget DYARCHY - Centre & Province could make laws on their subjects
        1. Central
          1. Central List - GG + Executive Council
        2. Provincial
          1. Transferred → Gov + Minister of LC
          2. Reserved → Gov + Executive Council of Governor
      4. Bi-Cameralism was introduced - Majority Elected + Some Nominated
        1. Upper House - Legislative Assembly
        2. Lower House - Council of States
      5. Limited Franchise on Tax Education & Property
      6. In viceroy's executive Council 26 Indian compulsory other than commander-in-chief
      7. Communal electorate was extended to 6 Indian Christians anglo-indians and Europeans
      8. Office of high Commissioner of India in London was to share responsibility of SOS
      9. Public Service Commission in 1926 was established to recruit civil servants
      10. Statutory Commission to review working of government every 10 years was introduced

    • Simon Comission 1927

      1. Background
        1. 7 member committee under Sir John Simon came to India with no Indian representation
        2. Submitted report in 1930
        3. it was by quoted by all the parties in India
      2. Recommendations of Simon Commission are
        1. Abolition of dyarchy
        2. Extension of and establishment of responsible government in provinces
        3. Establishment of Federation of British government and state (to appease kings and Queens)
        4. Continuation of separate and communal electorate
      3. Also called
        1. It is called white paper on constitutional reforms - which is a joint select committee of British Parliament
        2. Lead to Round table conference between
          1. British Government
          2. British India
          3. princely states
      4. Most of its recommendation were implemented in Government of India Act 1935

    • Communal award 1932

      1. At this time ransom McDonald was the British pm
      2. It was a scheme for representation of minorities
      3. Provided for separate electorate for
        1. Muslim Sikh Indian Christian
        2. Anglo Indian and European
        3. Depressed classes ( SC / ST )
          1. Gandhi was against it
          2. Took a Fast in Yerwada jail Pune
          3. Led to Poona pact between Gandhi and Ambedkar
            1. Poona pact allowed to retain Hindu electorate reserved not separated seats to depressed class
            2. This was the starting of reservation in India

    • Government of India Act 1935

      1. Background
        1. It was just after World War 2 in 1939
        2. Mini constitution of India
        3. 321 section and tenth scandals
      2. It provided for
        1. All India Federation = provinces && princely state
        2. Power between centre and units (provinces && princely state) was divided into three part
          1. Federal 59
          2. Provincial 54
          3. Concurrent 36
          4. Power only with Viceroy
      3. Federation was abolished the princely states didn't agree
      4. Provincial autonomy was given by abolishment of dyarchy
        1. State would be autonomous units of Administration
        2. Governor woodwork with the advice of ministers of state legislature this was the responsible government suggested in Simon Commission of 1927
      5. Bi Cameralism in States
      1. Dyarchy at centre was launched but never started Federal subject for to be divided into reserved subjects and transferred subjects
      2. Communal representation extended from all minorities and SC to women labour workers
      3. SOS’s Council of India abolished which was established by government of India Act 1858 SOS was provided with team of Advisors
      4. UAF extended to 10% of total population
      5. RBI was given control over currency and credit
      6. 3 tier civil services was established
        1. Centre - Federal Public Service Commission
        2. 2 or More States - joint PSC
        3. 1 State - Provincial PSC
      7. Federal Court was established in 1937

    • Indian Independence Act of 1947

      • Background
        1. Constant Assembly was established in 1946 saying that constitution kind applied to area not willing to accept
          1. On 20th February 1947 British pm Clement attlee said, British rule would end in India by June 30 1948 Muslim League protested in favour of partition
        2. Viceroy of India Lord Mountbatten resolve this by Mountbatten plan which was implemented by independence of India Act 1947

      Features

      1. British rule ends in India India Pakistan would be independent sovereign countries from 15 August 1947
      2. Dominion status was given that is right to secede or stay from Commonwealth India was divided into two Dominions India and Pakistan
      3. Office of Viceroy was abolished governor general was appointed by British king on advice of dominance cabinet Mountbatten was first Governor General of India
      4. Secretary of state of India abolished secretary of state of Commonwealth affairs started They both had same work
      5. Constituent assembly of both the Nation was established to
        1. Enact any Constitution that they make
        2. Repeal any act of British including Indian Independence Act of 1947
      6. New Constitution was formed in India, constant assembly would do to work
        1. Legislate themselves for territories
          1. Headed by GV Mavalankar
        2. Make constitution
          1. Headed by Rajendra Prasad
      7. British rule ended in India → lapse of British Paramountcy, Britishers drop the title of king or emperor of India from Royal titles
      8. Princely states could join any
        1. India
        2. Pak
        3. or be Independent

  • Indian Political System

    • Meaning of Political System

      1. India being a democracy is an aspect of our political system
      2. Political Systems - A Formal / Legal / Constitutional Arrangement that constitutes the Government
      3. It also deals with Relationship of Government with Organs of the Country

    • How is Government Formed in India ?

      • What are the Parts of Indian Parliament ?
        1. Lok Sabha - House of People - Direct Election @ UAF - 543 Seats
        2. Rajya Sabha - Council of States - 243 Seats
      • Entire Country is divided into ?
        1. Entire Country i.e 28 States & 7 UT have been divided into No of Territorial States
      • What is UAF ?
        1. Universal Adult Franchise
        2. Anyone who has attained the age of 18 has the right to vote
      • Which is the Latets Parliament and Its Details ?
        1. 17th Lok Sabha Election (Popular Choice) : 2019 Elections
          1. Congress - Coalition - UPA - United Progressive Alliance
          2. BJP - Coalition - NDA - National Democratic Alliance

    • Key Features of Indian Political System ? (6)

      💡 De PD SOP DOP FSUB IIJ

      • 1 Democracy

        • Meaning
          1. Democracy is a Government which is elected for the people, of the people, by the people
          2. Means Rule of People by Representative
          3. Indian Democracy is Not Direct but Indirect Democracy
          4. India was chosen to be a Indirect Democracy because
            1. Huge Size of Nation
            2. Administrative Convenience
        • Three Important Features for a Country to be called a Democracy ?
          1. UAF
            1. Universal Adult Franchise
            2. Every Citizen on Attaining 18 years of age shall have the right to vote without any discrimination
          2. Free & Fair Elections
            1. Process in which conduct of election is free from any from of corrupt practise & also provides an equal platform for all the candidates contesting the elections
            2. Ensured by Independent Authority to Conduct Elections i.e Election Commission of India
          3. Periodic & Regular Elections
            1. It also means that the elections happen at a regular interval as prescribed in the constitution
            2. In India normally the elections to both Parliament ( Lok Sabha ) & State Legislative Assembly happens every 5 Years

      • 2 Parliamentary Democracy

        CRUX - PARLIAMENTARY DEMOCRACY

        1. Legitimacy of Executive (CoM + PM) is constantly & regularly accountable to Legislature (Lok Sabha && State Assembly)
        2. Continuous Accountability of Executive(PM/CM && CoM) to Legislature - They should always have Majority in Lok Sabha / State Assembly
        3. Example : India

        CRUX - PRESIDENTIAL DEMOCRACY

        • Process of Election in USA
          1. Electoral Collage : 100 Members will be elected by the People
          2. This Electoral Collage will elect the President
        • USA Parliament is called as Congress
          1. House of Representatives - Lower House or the Lok Sabha
          2. Senate - Upper House or the Rajya Sabha
        1. Executive (President) is NOT accountable (meaning : to not have majority) to Legislature
        2. Once President is Elected, President has no Accountability to the Legislature
        3. Example : USA
        💡 Meaning of being “Accountable” = To Have Majority In the House

        DEBATE IN CONSTITUENT ASSEMBLY

        1. Majority Chose Parliamentary Democracy because it is more accountable to the People

      • 3 Separation of Powers - Inter Body - { Division between LEJ }

        • Prelude

          1. Essential Feature of Indian Political System
          2. Maxim : Power Corrupts but Absolute Power Corrupts Absolutely

        • Separation of Powers

          Implemented by Checks & Balance of System

          Three Important Systems

          1. Legislature - Making of Law
            1. Centre : Parliament
            2. State : State Legislature
            3. Union Territory
              1. With & w/o Legislature - President
          2. Executive - Implementation of Law
            1. Centre : Union Executive
              1. Political / Temporary Executive : Pres + VP + PM + CoM
              2. Permanent Executive : Central Deputed Bureaucrats
            2. State : State Executive
              1. Political / Temporary Executive : Gov + CM + CoM
              2. Permanent Executive : State Deputed Bureaucrats
          3. Judiciary - Interpretation of Law
            1. Centre : Supreme Court
            2. State : High Court & Sub Ordinate Courts
            3. Power of Judicial Review & Interpretation
            4. If Judges Make Mistake : Parliament can remove Judges

      • SOP & DOP Mind Map

      • 4 Division of Powers - Intra Body - { Division within LEJ }

        • Prelude
          1. Also Called - Federal Political System or Decentralisation ;
          2. First Time in Government of India Act 1935
          3. Any Federal System in the World Follows : Division of Powers
          4. Federal Political System : DOP b/w C & S
          5. Multiple Levels of Government
            1. Union Government
            2. State Government
            3. Panchayati Raj
        • Constitution DOP b/w C&S
          • Central Govt - Unity Needed
            1. Defence
            2. Insurance
            3. Banking
            4. Citizenship
          • State Govt - Regional Diversity Needed
            1. Agriculture
            2. Health
            3. Sanitation
            4. Police

      • 5 Federal System with Unitary Bias

        • What is Federalism ?

          1. Powers of the Government are divided at multiple levels- Centre, States
          2. Union has its Legislature (Parliament), Executive(Centre & State)(Permanenet & Temporary)
          3. Federal Political System is one which has a division of Power between Centre & State

        • What are the Essential Features of Federalism ?

          1. Dual Government
          2. Distribution of Powers
          3. Supremacy of Constitution
          4. Rigidity of Constitution
          5. Independent Judiciary

        • Where is Federalism Suitable for ?

          1. Federalism is suitable for a plural society with multiple cultures and multiple language speaking populations.
          2. It is more democratic in nature and facilitates people participation
          3. Checks the powers of the government—checks abuse of power.
          4. Federal Government can absorb some of the costs of new technology or programs that would have to be absorbed completely by member units in a unitary system.

        • Is India a Federal Polity ?

        • Why India Chose a Federalism with Unitary Bias ?

          1. Federal System - To Accommodate Diversity
          2. Unitary Bias - To Prevent Balkanisation of India

        • Federalism with Unitary Bias is Called ?

          1. Federal System with Unitary Bias
          2. Federation with Centralising Tendency
          3. Quasi Federal System - by K C Wheare

        • Ambedkars View on Indian Federalism

          https://app.notion.com

      • 6 Independent & Integrated Judiciary

        1. Independent Judiciary
          1. Enough Mechanism in Constitution that insulate Judiciary from Influence of Legislature & Executive
          2. Once Judges are appointed it becomes extremely difficult for legislature & executive to remove a supreme court judge
          3. Since Constitution has been made, no judge has been removed
        2. Integrated Judiciary
          1. Judgement Given by SC is binding upon all HC & Sub Ordinate Courts
          2. Judgement Given by HC is binding upon all Sub Ordinate Courts of that state
          3. Judgement by a HC is not binding on other HC, It does not have a Binding Effect but a Persuasive Effect and a Precedence Value
        3. Extra Judicial & Quasi Judicial Bodies
          1. Tribunals with some role of Executive

  • Indian Constitution

    • Constitution

      • Why do we need to have a Constitution ? - BR, SoG, Lim of Pow, Obj Ach

        • Prelude
          1. To Accommodate Diversity - Different Aspirations & Demands
          2. To Solve the Conflict of Interests
          3. Basic Rules by Constitution - to MINIMISE the conflicts in Society
        1. Basic Rules Facilitating Co Ordination amongst People
          1. Basic Rules : Doesn’t Have minute Information of All Small Rules
          2. Example : Constitution Provides for Safeguarding rights of Minority & oppressed class, each and every sections of society is protected
        2. Define Structure of Government
          1. by Democracy implemented by Elections
          2. India is a Parliamentary Democracy
          3. Not a Presidential Democracy
        3. Limitation of Powers
          1. to prevent the Government to become Arbitrary
          2. Meaning of Arbitrary : not seeming to be based on any reason or plan and sometimes seeming unfair
          3. How ? : by Conferring rights on the people → Especially Fundamental Rights
        4. Enables the State to Achieve its objectives

      • How do we define the Constitution ?

        • Definition

          Constitution is the Fundamental Law of the Country which lays down the Basic Structure of the Political System having a special sanctity under which its people are to be governed.

        • Image of Constitution

      • Why is it called a Fundamental Law or Supreme Law ?

        Other Laws in the Country have to be in Congruence or Consonance with the Fundamental Laws

        Other Laws - Ordinary Laws - can be made by

        1. Parliament
        2. State Legislature

      • Why Constitution Chose Democracy ?

        1. The Welfare of People is Supreme
        2. People Should be Centric in the Governance

    • What is the Definition of State ?

      An Independent political entity occupying a defined territory,  the members of which are united together for the purpose of resisting external force and preservation of internal order and the state needs certain instruments or organs to carry out its function

    • What are the Major Functions of Indian Constitution ?

      1. To provide a set of basic rules that allow for minimal coordination amongst members of a society
      2. To define who has powers to make laws and enforce them i.e organizational framework
      3. To set some limits on what the State can impose on its citizens
      4. Reflects the ideology and philosophy of a Nation-State
      5. Provide for its own amendment provisions

    • How the success of the constitution could be ensured?

      1. A Constitution Should be drawn by those people who have credibility, are popular, and represent various sections of society
      2. A law that has something for everyone
      3. Should have constitutionalism
      4. Should be followed and respected
      5. Should be rigid yet flexible

    • What is Constitutionalism ?

      1. Constitutionalism is specific limitations on general State powers to prevent the exercise of arbitrary decision-making e.g. Gaddaffi constitution did not have constitutionalism.
      2. To ensure the success of democracy it is important that there is a limitation on the powers of the state
      3. Limitation on the Arbitrary Action of State & Tyranny of Executive

    • How does Rule of Law Help in Ensuring Constitutionalism ?

      1. For constitutionalism, the Constitution should be adhered to and followed
      2. The primary meaning of the term rule of law is that everything must be done according to law
      3. Two principles
        1. Everyone should be equal in the eyes of law
        2. No one is above the law

    • Salient Features of Indian Constitution (9)

      • 1 Written Constitution

        1. Written Constitution
          • What are the Characteristics of a Written Constitution ?
            1. Codified into a Single Document ( Can be in more than one Document )
            2. But whatever has been codified, only that will be called and used as a Constitution ; nothing else than that
            3. there is a clear distinction between constitutional text and ordinary laws ( IPC, CrPC, Consumer Protection, Motor Vehicles Act)
            4. There is a Specific time of Enactment and Adoption & Changes Organically with time but not so frequently ( Combination of Rigidity & Flexibility )
          • Example : India
            1. Indian constitution is the lengthiest constitution
            2. Commencement : 395 Art ; 22 Parts ; 8 Schedules
            3. Currently : 465 Art ; 25 Parts ; 12 Schedules
          • Example : USA
        2. Un Written
          • What are the Characteristics of a UnWritten Constitution ?
            1. It is codified and written but there is no separate document as a constitution
            2. All the Laws Passed by Legislature, Conventions Followed by Country & Judgements Passed by Supreme Court becomes a part of constitution
            3. There is no distinction between Constitution & Ordinary Laws
            4. There is no Specific Time of Enactment and Adoption and It involves Changes but Very frequent ones
          • Example : British, Israel & Newzeland

      • 2 Drawn from Multiple Sources

        • Summary

        • Details

          1. Indian constitution was made after deep study of various existing constitutions of the world.

          2. Criticism that it is a borrowed constitution. But Ambedkar refuted this statement. becasue Indian constitution was made at a time when allmost many countried had their own constitution

          3. USA

            Fundamental Rights, Concept of federalism, Independent Judicial system, bicameralism

          4. British

            Parliamentary democracy, Westminster model of Government, the idea of single citizenship, the institution of the speaker, the concept of rule of law.

          5. German constitution

            Emergency provisions.

          6. South Africa

            Procedure for amendment of Indian constitution, Election of Rajya Sabha members

          7. Irish Constitution

            DPSP, Nomination of members to Rajya Sabha.

          8. Soviet Union

            Fundamental Duties

          9. Australia

            Free commerce and trade between the states, Article 253- the power of the Union to legislate on state subject in order to keep up India’s commitment at the international level.

          10. France

            values of liberty, equality & fraternity.

          11. Canada

            A form of federalism(strong centre), the concept of residuary powers with the centre.

      • 3 Lengthiest Constitution of the World

        • Indias Constitution is the Lenghtiest Constitution of the World

          1. Single constitution for the centre and states
          2. Huge diversity is prevalent
          3. Principles of administration have also been provided in the Indian constitution.
          4. Positive directions have been given to the state

          Facts

          1. Indian constitution is the lengthiest constitution
          2. Commencement : 395 Art ; 22 Parts ; 8 Schedules
          3. Currently : 465 Art ; 25 Parts ; 12 Schedules

      • 3 Rigid & Flexible

        1. How Easy / Difficult is it to change/amend a constitution
        2. To Change / Amend : Article 368
        3. Latest Amendment : 105th CAA occurred in 2022
        4. Contrary to this USA Constitution has been Amended 33 times only

      • 4 Fundamental Rights Part 3 12-35

        • Whats is the Meaning of the Word Rights ?

          Rights are Entitlement, Privileges or Normative Fundamental Rules of what is allowed of people

        • Why are the Rights Given ?

          1. Prevents Arbitrary Actions by State - Majorly Against the State
          2. Prevents Arbitrary Action by Citizen as well - Very Less are Against the State

        • What is the Top Most Rights ?

          1. Fundamental Right is the top most right of India under Part 3 ( Art12 - Art35) of the constitution

        • How are they Justiciable ?

          They are Justiciable in

          1. Supreme Court under Art 32
          2. Difference : 194
          3. High Court under Art 226

      • 5 DPSP Part 4 36-51

        1. Directive means Guidelines
        2. Present Under Part - IV (Art 36 - Art 51 )
        3. These Guidelines will lead to Welfare of State
        4. It has been Purposefully made Non Justiciable
        5. Moral Obligation - Duties of State

      • 6 Fundamental Duties Part 4 A 51 A

        1. Mentioned in Part 4 A of the Constitution
        2. Art 51 A deals with it
        3. There are in total 11 Duties
        4. 10th FD were added by 42nd CAA, 1976 under PM Indira Gandhi ; It was time of Emergency in India
        5. 11th FD was added by 86th CAA of 2003
        6. These FD are Not Punishable
        7. These are moral obligations to citizens i.e., Duties of Citizens

      • 7 Secular State

        1. In India, the State will never Identify itself with any one particular region
        2. The State will promote & provide equal opportunity for all religion
        3. State is Non Religious & State Promotes All Religions Equally & there shall be no Discrimination on the basis of Religion
        4. Concept of Positive Secularism

      • 8 Single Citizenship

        • Which are the Countries that Allow Dual Citizenship

        1. Constituent Assembly Debated, Dual Citizenship will go against Fraternity & Brotherhood
        2. Local Identity of State would prevail over National Identity
        3. Idea of Single citizenship is to promote Unity and Integrity of Nation

      • 9 UAF

        • Whats is the Meaning of Franchise ?

          Franchise Means : the right to vote in elections

        1. It is Essence of Democracy, There shall be no discrimination on any basis
        2. Every Citizen above the Age of 18 has the Right to Vote
        3. Right to Vote was mentioned in Articles - 325 & 326
        4. At the commencement of Constitution, the age for UAF was 21 years
        5. With 61st CAA, 1988 , the age was reduced to 18 years

      • 10 Federal Government

        • Whats is Federalism ?
          1. Two or more levels of Government- Central level and state level
          2. Both the Union and State derive power from constitution
          3. Presidential form of government- Eg: USA
          4. Parliamentary government- Westminster Model- Eg: UK & India
        • Essential Features
          • Dual Government
          • Distribution of Powers- Separate spheres of functioning. Eg: 7 Schedule-Union List, State List, Concurrent list.
          • Residuary subject-In India more power to the centre
          • The supremacy of the constitution
          • The rigidity of the constitution
          • Independent judiciary
        • Benefits of federalism
          1. Federalism is suitable for a plural society with multiple cultures and multiple language speaking populations.
          2. It is more democratic in nature and facilitates people participation
          3. Checks the powers of the government—checks abuse of power.
          4. Federal Government can absorb some of the costs of new technology or programs that would have to be absorbed completely by member units in a unitary system.
        • Is India a federal polity?
          • India has a dual government(division of powers), Supremacy of the constitution, Independent judiciary, etc.
          • In matters of federalism Indian constitution is rigid, but flexible in most others.
        • Reasons for criticizing India's federal setup:
          • The powers of the union are more than the state in the legislative domain.
          • In certain exceptional instances, the Union can legislate on state subjects as well.
          • Article 356- Gives power to the centre to dismiss the state government
          • Finance commission- Appointment made by the Central government
        • Why choose a federal setup with strong unitary bias?
          • Secessionist tendencies at the time of independence made it imperative to have a strong centre to maintain the unity and integrity of the country.
          • External threats. Eg: Pakistan
          • We require uniformity in policies to address major issues facing the country. Eg: Poverty, literacy, etc.

      • 11 Responsible Government

        Executives are responsible to the legislature

      • 12 Independent & Integrated Judiciary

      India is a Unique Example of : ”Reconciliation of written constitution with parliamentary sovereignty”

    • Characteristics of State / Nature of Indian State

      • Sovereign
        1. Full Control over the people, property & territory and also free to conduct internal & external affairs
        2. India can chose its friends
        3. Is full sovereignty possible in a Globalised World ?
          1. Theoretically Yes
          2. Practically No
      • Socialist
        • Whats is Socialism ?
          1. Social Ownership of Property & against private ownership
          2. Ownership of Production by the Government, so that the redistribution is easy
        • What type of Socialism is followed by India ?
          1. India follows Democratic Socialism i.e Mid Way
            1. Essential for majority of masses especially poor State Holds & Provides for Example : Railways
            2. Not Essential or may be used by the Rich State Privatises & Promotes Public Sphere of PPE Example : Air India
        • What are the Pro Socialist provision in Constitution & Indian Polity ?
          1. Public Sector Union
          2. Nationalisation of Banks
          3. Social Sector Schemes
          4. Abolition of Privy Purse by Indira
        • Why was a Socialistic Nature Adopted for the Constitution ?
          1. Economic Inequality
          2. Land Ownership Inequality
          3. to promote Egalitarianism
        • What was the view of Ambedkar on putting the word Socialist in the Preamble ?
          1. DR B R Ambedkar opposed the introduction of this word in the constitution
          2. He said so because
            1. It is today in 1947 that due to inequality socialism appears good
            2. but tomorrow when society develops socialists ideology would become obsolete
            3. by putting it in constitution, it makes it permanent & thus takes away from the masses their choice of type of social, economic & political system they want
      • Secular

        • What is the Meaning of Secular

          1. State is Non Religious in Character.
          2. State has No Official Religion

        • Differences between Indian & Western Secularism

          Link to Summary Sheet

        • What was the view of JLN on Secularism

          1. Unlike west Indian State can and will be involved with religion but will not promote one religion over another
          2. We will have our own kind of Secualrism which will be accomodated according to India
          3. The Indian Idea of Secularism doesnt match specifically with the current definition of secularism. Hence it is better to not put Secular as a Word in the Preamble but Indian Constitution inherently is a Secular State
      • Democratic
        1. Govt Elected by the People, for the People, of the People
        2. Things that prove that India is a Democratic State
          1. Regular Elections 5 Years
          2. Rt To Vote under Art 326
          3. Free & Fair Election ( ECI under Art 324 )
      • Republic
        1. Head of State will be Elected ( by Elections )
        2. Two Cases - Both are Democracies, Both have F&F Elections, Both have UAF but
          1. UK : Head of State is a Constitutional Monarchy (Heredetary Succession)
          2. India : Head of State is a Elected Person ( Direct Election - India && Inidrect-USA )

    • Objectives of Constitution

      • Justice ( SEP )

        • Details about Justice

          • Does Equality Mean Justice ?

          • What is the Meaning of Justice ?

            1. Just & Fair Treatment of People
            2. Equality Doesnt Mean Justice
            3. India Doesnt Follow Equality Before Law but Equality by the Law

        • Why this Specific Order has been followed

          1. According to Ambedkar, S E P are in line of, Toughness to Achieve a Particular Justice Type
          2. As in Social Justice is the Most Toughest to Achieve
          3. Then Economic Justice is Tough
          4. Easiest to Achieve is Political Justice.
            1. For Example : in a Stroke of Enactment of Constitution, Everyone will be Equal Politcially

        • Social

          • What is a Social Justice ?
            1. It is a fair and just tretament of all citizens from their social standing
            2. Determined by their : Educational Level, Caste Acceptance etc
          • What are the Examples of Social Justice ?
            1. Examples of Social Justice
              1. Reservation in the Educational Institutions
              2. Reservation in Public Employment
          • What is concept of Postitive Discrimination
            1. Positive Discrimination is the Affirmative Action Taken by the State for the upliftment of a particular group

        • Economic

          • What is a Economic Justice ?
            1. Equitable Distribution of Wealth amongst Citizens
          • Examples of Economic Justice ?

            1. Land Reforms & Land Ceiling

            2. Direct Taxes - Progressive Taxation System — Differential Tax Slabs in Income Tax - People who Earn More, Pay More

        • Political

          • What is the Meaning of Political Justice
            1. State cam commit discrimination but cannot be a Arbitray Distinction
            2. Absence of any arbitray disntinction between man and man
          • Examples of Political Justice in India ?
            1. UAF
            2. Political Resrvation

      • Liberty (TE BF W)

        • Relation Between Liberty & Freedom ?

          1. Liberty comes with some Reasonable Restrictions
          2. Liberty is not an absolute concept where as Freedom is more of a absolute restriction
          3. Freedom of One, might hamper the right of another
          4. Freedom Restrained to a Particular Level is Liberty

        • What are the Examples of Liberty with Restrictions ?

          1. Example :
            1. Loudspeaker in Religious Institutions ; Govt Circular on Db Level of Speakers
            2. Hate Speech

        • Meaning of Liberty ?

          Minimal Rights Essential for free and civilised existence

        • Types of Liberty in Indian Constitution are ?

          Art 19 - with RR

          1. Thought - Thought Process
          2. Expression - To Express Thoughts in Various Forms

          Art 25 - with RR

          1. Belief - Lower in Degree, It can be Changed
          2. Faith - Higher in Degree, It cannot be Changed
          3. Worship - Act of practising a Religion

          A Belief System Repeated over time becomes Faith and Its Expression is Worship

      • Equality ( S&O) - Pending

        • Difference between Equality & Equity ?

        • Equality of Status

        • Equality of Oppurtunity

      • Fraternity ( Dign. & UI )

        1. Assuring Dignity of the Individual
          1. Unless All the Previously Mentioned Objectives are met, one cannot feel dignity
        2. Unity & Integrity of Nation (2)
          1. Unity ( Brotherhood ) - Single Citizenship & Fundamental Duties
          2. Territorial Integrity

        • Keshavanand Bharti Case, 1973 - 13 Judge Bench
          1. Yes it is an Integral Part of Constitution & can be Amended
          2. Is Not Justiciable & Non Enforceable
          3. Why is Preamble a Part of Constitution Now ?
            1. cuz it was debated, discussed & voted by the constituent assembly as and like any other part of constitution
      • Is Preamble Integral Part of the Constitution ?
        1. Yes it is ; under Keshavananda Bharthi Case
      • Can the Preamble be Amended under Art 368 ?
        1. Yes, But the Basic Structure cant be Amended
      • Can the Preamble be Amended in Totality ?
        1. No, Basic Structure Doctrine cant be Amended
