    • Current Affairs

Constitution - Part 3, Part 4, Part 4A

  • Part 3 (Art 12 - Art 35) - Fundamental Rights

    • ⭐️Important Questions & Concepts of Fundamental Rights

      • What is a Right ?

        An Entitlement by the State

      • What is the Difference between Rights & Fundamental Rights ?

        1. These are Fundamental Rights is because they are essential for a “Dignified Human Survival & Realize Fullest Potential of thyself”
        2. Dignified Human Survival
          1. To have sense of Respect for ThySelf
          2. This makes you feel like “Life is Worth Living”
        3. Fullest Potential, in terms of
          1. Intellectual
          2. Spiritual
          3. Physical

      • How is the Top Priority Given to Fundamental Rights (FR) ?

        1. One can go directly to the Supreme Court, w/o going to Lower Courts
        2. It is Protected & Guaranteed by the Constitution

      • What is the Purpose of Giving Fundamental Rights ?

        1. Constituent Assembly wanted the FR to be
          1. Protective
            1. Citizens be Protected from any Arbitrary & Tyranny of State
          2. Promotive
            1. Promotes Individual Well Being & Allied Aspects of Social Life
            2. Example : Art

      • Who can Exercise this Privilege or Who is Holder of the Right ?

        By Citizen or Foreigners (Alien)

      • Against Whom can we Assert this Right ?

        1. Against State
        2. Against Individuals

      • With Right comes Remedy - How & Against Whom ?

        Art 32 Provides for Rt to Constitutional Remedy

      • What is the Summary of Fundamental Rights ?

        Equality, Freedom, Exploitation, Religion. Culture Education, Remedies

      • We have Borrowed Fundamental Rights from ?

        Bill of Rights from American Constitution

      • How Many Number of Fundamental Rights were Originally & at Present ?

        Originally - 7

        Currently - 6

      • At the time of Independence we had how many Right ?

        1. we had a total of 7 themes of FR
        2. Art 31 - Right to Property has been Removed from Part 3 by 44th CAA in 1978 and sent to 300 A

      • How is Fundamental Right a Limitation on State ?

        If by the Actions of State ( above Defined ) → FR is Violated → Individual can Assert & Seek (Remedy & Justice) this in Court of Law → Which is a Limitation on Actions of State

    • ⭐️Features of Fundamental Rights

      • 1 Rigidity v/s Flexibility

        Not as Rigid as US

        Not as Flexible as UK

      • 2 Citizen v/s Alien

        Only for Citizens → Art - 15,16,19,29,30

        Other All Are for both → Citizen and Aliens

      • 3 Qualified v/s Absolute

        1. These FR are only Qualified meaning they can be reasonably restricted
        2. Absolute Rights can cause harm to Other Individuals of Society

      • 4 Against State v/s Against Individual

        1. Some are Against the State Protection Against Arbitrary Action & Tyranny of State
        2. Some are Against the Individuals

      • 5 Negative v/s Positive

        1. Negative - Restrict the State
        2. Positive - Confer Privileges on Person

      • 6 Interaction with Court of Law

        1. Justiciable - Can Move to Court of Law
        2. Defended & Guaranteed by Supreme Court

      • 7 Not Sacrosanct & Permanent

        1. Can curtail or repeal them
        2. Only without affecting the Basic Structure of Constitution

      • 8 Can be Suspended in Case of

        1. National Emergency - Except Art 2० & Art 21
        2. 6 Rights of Art 19 Suspended
          1. on basis of : External Emergency → Emergency on Grounds of War & External Aggression
          2. NOT on basis of : Internal Emergency → Armed Rebellion

      • 9 Scope of Operation is Limited

        1. Art 31 A - Acquisition of States
        2. Art 31 B - Validation of Acts & Regulations in 9th Schedule
        3. Art 31 C - Giving Effect to Certain DPSP
        4. Art 33 - Application to Forces Restricted
        5. Art 34 - Martial Law

      • 10 Mode of Enforcement

        1. By Means of Law (Art 35) - Only By Parliament, Not by State Leg
        2. Self Executory

    • ⭐️Parts of Basic Structure

      • 1 Possibility of Amendment to Basic Structure
        1. Cannot be Deleted - Whatever is Given cannot be taken Away
        2. Value Can be Added - to Existing
      • 2 Basic Structure According to KNB Case is
        1. Supremacy of the Constitution
        2. Unity and Sovereignty of India
        3. Democratic and Republican form of government
        4. Federal character of the Constitution
        5. Secular character of the Constitution
        6. Separation of Power
        7. Individual Freedom
      • 3 Over time, many other features have also been added to this list of basic structure features. Some of them are:
        1. Rule of law
        2. Judicial Review
        3. Parliamentary system
        4. Rule of equality
        5. Harmony and balance between the Fundamental Rights and DPSP
        6. Free and fair elections
        7. Limited power of the parliament to amend the Constitution
        8. Power of the Supreme Court of India under Articles 32, 136, 142 and 147
        9. Power of the High Court under Articles 226 and 227

    • ⭐️Art 12 - Art 13 → Basics of Part (III)

      • ⭐️Art 12 - Defines the Term State for the Purpose of Part 3 of the Constitution ⭐️

        • Article 12 Says

          Definition In this Part, unless the context otherwise requires, the State includes the Government and Parliament of India and the Government and the Legislature of each of the States and all local or other authorities within the territory of India or under the control of the Government of India

        • Why do we need to define State ?

          1. This helps us in deciding that “Against whom to Assert these Rights” ?
          2. This helps us in Defining “The Arbitrary Action of” State**.** Hence Definition Necessary
          3. Most FR are against State. Hence Definition Necessary

        • What is a State Acc to Art 12 ?

          1. Government & Parliament of India
            1. P VP PM CoM + Civil Servant
          2. Government & Legislature of States
            1. G CM CoM + Leg Assembly + Leg Council
          3. Local Authorities
            1. Panchayats, Municipalities etc
          4. Other Authorities within the Territory of India, under the Control of the Government of India
            1. Other : LIC, ONGC, SAIL, MTNL, BSNL, HAL, DRDO

        • What is Other Authorities ?

          • Electricity Board of Rajasthan v/s Mohan Lal, 1967

            1. other authorities would include

              all the authorities created by the constitution or statute on which powers are conferred by law

            2. The court held that an electricity board would also be a state overruling a previous judgment where the court had ruled otherwise

            3. In another important judgment of SC, it was held that statutory corporations such as ONGC and LIC are also a part of the state under article 12

          • Ajay Hasiya v/s Khalid Mujeeb, 1981 Case - Doctrine of Instrumentality & Agency

            1. Share Capital
              1. If Govt is a Majority Share Holder
            2. Financial Assistance
              1. If the financial assistance provided to a body by the govt. is so much that it meets the entire expenditure of the company
            3. Monopoly Status
              1. If company enjoys a State Conferred Monopoly
              2. Ex MTNL, BSNL, HAL, DRDO
            4. Nature of Functions
              1. Related to Govt Functions or Important Functions
              2. if an entity performs public functions or functions closely related to govt. functions
            5. State Control
              1. Appointments of Important Positions in the Company/Organisation

            Note : All these Principles are not needed to be Applied at Once, Even if one or some apply, it will also be considered as a Satte

        • Is Judiciary a Part of State ?

          1. Judiciary falls under the definition of state in some ways, and still, in some ways, it does not
          2. Judiciary carries out:
            1. Judicial functions - Not Considered as State
            2. Administrative functions - Considered as State
            3. Rulemaking functions - Considered as State
      • Art 13 - Laws Inconsistent with Fundamental Rights be Void
        • Important Questions

          • What is Judicial Review ?

          • What are the Laws ?

            1. Pre Constitutional Law
              1. Entire Bulk of IPC & CrPC
              2. Example :
                1. Sec 124 A - Sedition
                2. Sec 377 - Rape
            2. Post Constitutional Law
              1. IT Act, 2000
              2. Motor Vehicle Act, 1987 → Amended in 2016
              3. Right to Information Act, 2005
              4. Right to Education Act, 2009
            3. Laws Made by
              1. Parliament
              2. State Legislature

          • What is Inconsistent ?

            Not in Sync

            Out of Line

            Not in Congruence

            Not in Concurrence

          • What are the Article of Fundamental Rights ?

            Directly Confers the Rights - Art 14 to 32

          • What is a Void ?

            It cannot be Implemented, There will be no Legal Affect

          • How much of Law will be Stuck Down ?

            1. Not the Full Law but
            2. to the Extent of Inconsistency, the Laws will be Struck Down

          • Who can strike down the Law ?

            1. Judiciary Interprets and can decalre the Laws to be Void
            2. Courts use Power of Judicial Review
            3. Under Art 13 - Power of Judicial Review under Art 13

          • What is a Judicial Review - Suo Moto Cognisance

            1. It is the Power of Courts to Review Actions of Legislature & Executive and declare them null & void if its actions are found to be violative of provisions of constitution
            2. Under Art 13, it is the power of the courts to Judicially Review, the actions of Legislature & Executive. If they are found to be violative of fundamental rights they can be decalred as void
            3. The Power of Judicial Review is Implicit under Art 13 it is not Explicitly Mentioned under Art 13

          • What is the Idea of Art 13

            1. To Give Primacy & Supremacy to Fundamental Rights
            2. Checks the powers of the legislature
            3. Shows significance/sacrosanctity of FRs
            4. It's like a safety valve for FRs
        • Extra Self

          • Does the Word Law Include Ordinary Laws & Constitutional Amendment ?

          • Doctrine

            • Doctrine of Severablity
            • Doctrine of Eclipse

          • Can a Constitutional Amendment (Article 368) abrogate/reduce the effect of the FR?

            Article 368 v/s Article 13

            • Shankari Prasad v/s Union of India, 1950
              1. Whether the courts can look into CAA, ie., whether CAA is a law or not. (If a law, then subject to Article 13)
              2. 1 CAA added 9th schedule among others
              3. Laws kept under 9 Schedule, even if they violate FRs(Fundamental Rights), they cannot be struck down for violating Article 13
              4. SC(Supreme Court) majority opinion was that CAA is not a law, hence not subject to judicial review.
            • Sajjan Singh v/s State of Punjab, 1965
              1. Whether CAA is a law was the question;
              2. Similar judgement-CAA is not a law;
              3. In this case, the minority judges(2 judges out of 3 judges) dissented- Justice Hidayatullah & Justice Mudholkar;
              4. Justice Hidayatullah opined that FRs are sacrosanct & they cannot be amended;
              5. Justice Mudholkar opined that every constitution has certain parts that can be amended and that sections which cannot be amended. If the entire constitution can be amended then what was the need of writing a constitution by the CA(Constituent assembly)
            • Golaknath Case v.s State of Punjab, 1967
              1. 7th CAA was challenged before a larger bench;
              2. Whether CAA is a law or not;
              3. CAA is equal to law & hence subject to Article 13;
              4. Hence the effect of FRs cannot be amended; FRs are sacrosanct;
            • 24th CAA, 1971
              1. introduced Article 13(4)& A*rticle 368(3)*to the Indian constitution;
              2. Nothing in this article shall apply to an amendment to the constitution made under Article 368-->Thus beyond the judicial powers of the court now;
              3. FRs can now be amended under Article 368;
              4. Thus via a CAA any part of constitution can be changed & cannot be challenged under Article 13;
            • Keshavananda Bharti Case v/s State of Kerala, 1973
            • 42nd CAA, 1976
            • Minerva Mills v/s Union of India, 1980

          • Criticism of Basic Structure Doctrine

            1. No constitutional basis
            2. Tyranny of the unelected—Judges
            3. Violates Separation of powers
            4. Subjective and vague Doctrine
        • Evolution of Basic Structure Doctrine - Parliament (Art 368) v/s Judiciary (Art 13)

          • The Chronology

          • Socio Economic Reforms after Independence

            • Like Zamindari Abolition Acts → Led to Resistance Amongst People

          • Kameshwar Singh v/s State of Bihar, 1950

            1. He didn't want to sell the Property, he thought it was the Violation
              • of Right under Art 19
                1. Art 19(1)(f) - Acquire Hold Dispose Property
                2. Grounds of Restriction
                  1. Interests of General Welfare of Society
                  2. Interests of Protecting Rights of SC/ST
              • of Right under Art 31
                1. Govt can take away property only by a law Passed by the Government ; and not only by a Executive Action
                2. That Compensation under Law under Art 31 is not a Just & Fair Compensation
              • of Right under Art 14
                1. Govt had given Various Discretion of Value of Land for Various People
              • Supreme Court Said
                1. Just & Fair Compensation is different from Equal Compensation
                2. Just & Fair Compensation is Must

          • 1st CAA - Art 31 B, 1951

            1. Art 31 B Says
              1. Saves those Laws from Judicial Review which are placed in 9th Schedule

          • Addition of 9th Schedule

            1. Originally Constitution had 8 Schedule
            2. First Law which was added : Bihar Land Reforms Act 1950
            3. It now has 284 Laws Now

          • Shankari Prasad v/s Union of India, 1951

            • Supreme Court has to decide
              1. What is the Power of Parliament to Amend the Constitution ?
                1. Insertion of Art 31 B is in Part 3 of the Constitution - Parliament is Taking Away Fundamental Rights by Amending the Fundamental Rights Part
              2. Whether Constitutional Amendments are Considered to be a Law under Art 13 ?
                1. Under Art 13 - He Said Addition of Art 31 B under 1 CAA is a Law in Itself and hence can be Declared Null and Void
            • SC Said that
              1. Parliament has 2 Types of Powers
                1. Constituent Powers under Art 368
                  1. Constituent Powers similar to that of a Constituent Assembly
                  2. This power was given to Parliament because CA wanted the Constitution to be a Living Document
                  3. Hence, Parliament can Amend the Fundamental Rights under Art 368
                2. Ordinary Law making powers
              2. SC said that according to CA debates, CA never wanted to consider CAA as a Ordinary Law

          • Sajjan Singh Case v/s State of Rajasthan, 1965

            • 17th CAA was challenged in SC
            • Rajasthan came up with Land Reforms Act and requested the Parliament to put up the Law in 9th Schedule
            • Same Result as that of Shankari Prasad Case
            • If CAA is taking away FR Parliament can Still Amend it, only a Ordinary Law cant take away your FR

          • IC Golaknath ORS v/s State of Punjab, 1967

            1. Basic Line of Argument by SC : Parliament is having Constituent Power under Art 368
            2. Golaknath Brother Argued that there is no Constituent Powers to the Parliament as
            3. Originally Art 368 says : Procedure to Amend the Constitution
            4. There is no mention of Power of Parliament → Hence they don’t have a Constituent Assembly like Power.
            5. Hence Now SC Stance was
              1. Earlier Until Now Parliament had two Laws :
                1. Constituent Law Powers : not under Art 13
                2. Ordinary Law Making Powers : under Art 13
              2. Now Parliament gad
                1. Constituent Law Powers : is under Art 13
                2. Ordinary Law Making Powers : is under Art 13

          • 24th Constitutional Amendment Act, 1971

            1. Amended Art 368 → Added [Power] → Power & Procedure to Amend the Constitution
            2. Amended Art 13 →
              1. Earlier → Laws Inconsistent with Fundamental Rights be Void
              2. Now → Constitutional Amendments under Art 368 would not be Law under Art 13
            3. What was given by SC as Judgement in Shankari Prasad & Sajjan Singh Case - was added by the Parliament to the CAA as an Amendment

          • Keshavananda Bharti Case v/s State of Kerala, 24 April 1973

            1. While Shankari Prasad and Sajan Singh Case gave unlimited Powers to the Parliament, KBC gave limited Powers

            2. SC upheld that there is a Constituent Power and 24th CAA is valid and Constitution can amend the Fundamental Rights but it should not destroy the Basic Structure

            3. Basic Structure

              1. Sovereignty
              2. Democratic
              3. Republic

            4. Hence, Now merely for Amending the FR you cannot challenge the CAA in Court of Law

              it can only be challenged only on the Ground that it violates Basic Structure

            • The Sacrifise of Justice Khanna

          • Indira / Raj Narayan Case, 1975

            • Free & Fair Elections Added to Basic Structure

          • 42nd CAA, 1976

            1. Amended Art 368 of Constitution → Constitutional Amendements will be complete Immune to Judicial Review

          • Minerva Mills Case, 1980

            Power of Judicial Review of Courts is a Part of Basic Structure and Hence even by a CAA it cannot be changed. Hence 42nd CAA’s was struck down

          • Indira Sahani Case, 1992

            1. Rule of Law Added to Basic Structure

          • SR Bommai Case, 1994

            • Secularism Added to Basic Structure

          • NJAC Case, 2015

            • Independence of Judiciary Added to the Basic Structure

          • Final Answers

            • Can Parliament Amend the Constitution
              • Yes
            • Is it Unlimited ?
              • No, it is Limited
            • How much is it limited
              • It cannot destroy the basic structure
            • Can parliament amend FR
              • Can Amend but not to the extent to destroy the basic structure
            • Can amendment to the FR be declared null and void by the courts
              • Yes it can be declared to be Null and Void, only if it violates the Basic Structure
            • Who determines the Basic Structure
              • Supreme Courts
        • Summary of Cases of Basic Structure Doctrine - Dynamic

    • Art 14 - Art 18 → Right to Equality (5)

      • Art 14 - Equality Before Law & Equal Protection of Law (1)

        • Art 14 in Constitution

          The State shall not deny any person equality before Law or the equal protection of Laws within territory of India

        • To Whom & Against Whom

          1. Persons
            1. Natural Persons
            2. Artificial Entities
          2. Who Shall Not Discriminate
            1. State

        • Why these 2 types of Application of Law came into existence

          • Absolute Function of Equality is not Possible

          • Treating Equals Equally is Actual Law

          • Before Law & Protection of Law

        • Equality Before Law

          • Concept
          • Exception
            • Art 361 - President & Governor
              1. Criminal Cases
                1. Can’t be Convicted in Criminal Cases
                2. Can be trial after the Leaving of Posts
              2. Civil Cases
                1. Can be tried while in Office
                2. Notice of 2 months to be Given
            • Art 105 & Art 194 - Parliamentary Privileges
              • Art 105 -
              • Art 194 -
            • Diplomatic Immunity to a Foreign Sovereign

        • Equal Protection of Law

          • Background
            1. Talks about Equals being Treated Equally
            2. Equal Protection of Law is not Against Equality before Law but helps in Achieving it.
            3. Equality Before Law includes Equal Protection of Law under Art 14
            4. Equal Protection of Law is applied in the form of Different Groups can be Created and Different Privileges can be conferred upon certain Groups to Empower them
            5. Can be called as an Affirmative Action or Positive Discrimination
            6. Reservation is Example of Positive Discrimination
            7. Individuals who are considered to be Backward (Primarily Socially)
              1. Scheduled Caste
              2. Scheduled Tribe
              3. Other Back Ward Class
          • Summary
            1. Similar Treatment of Individuals whoa are similarly placed
            2. Positive Discrimination or Affirmative Action
            3. Reasonable Classification - Classification Should not be Arbitrary
            4. Reasonable Nexus between Classification & Object
            5. Examples
              • Reservation Policy
                1. Schedule Caste : 15% Reservation in Educational Institution and Govt Jobs
                2. Schedule Tribe :
                3. Other Backward Class :
              • Progressive Taxation Policy
                1. Slab Based Taxation Policy
              • Citizenship Amendment Act, 2019
                1. This is Violative of Equality Before Law
                2. This is Application of Equal Protection of Law with Reasonable Classification of 3 Countries and 6 Religions
          • Most Important Concept
            1. There should be a Reasonable Nexus in the Objective and Grouping of the Beneficiaries
            2. The
              1. The Positive Discrimination
              2. The Affirmative Action
              3. of the State
              4. Should not become Discrimination in totality for the Unreserved

      • Art 15 - Prohibition of Discrimination on Certain Grounds (6)

        • What is Art 15

          Prohibition of Discrimination on Grounds of Religion, Race, Caste, Sex or Place of Birth - ReRa CaSe PoB

        • Art 15(1)

          • Summary No Discrimination ReRa CaSe PoB

          • What is Art 15(1)

            State Shall not Discriminate against any citizen on Grounds only of Religion, Race, Caste, Sex or Place of Birth or Any of them.

          • Shall not Discriminate Against

            1. Religion - Belief Faith
            2. Race - Physical Attributes
            3. Caste - Varna System
            4. Sex - Biological Factors
            5. Place of Birth - Birth Place
            6. Any of Them - or a Combination of them

        • Art 15(2)

          • Summary No Discrimination ReRa CaSe PoB Disability, Liability, Restrcition (a) Access - Shop Pub Res Hot & Place of Pub Ent (b) Use - Well Tank Ghats Road Pub Resort - Wholly Partly by State Fund

          • What is Art 15(2)

            No Citizen shall be discriminated on grounds only of Religion, race, caste, sex, place of birth or any of them, be subject to any disability, liability or restriction with regard to

            (a) access to Shops, Public Restaurants, Hotels & Places of Public Entertainment

            (b) the use of wells, tanks, bathing ghats, roads and places of public resort maintained wholly or partly out of state funds

          • Access - Can be Private Owned

            • Public Entertainment (Parks, Malls, Exhibition Centre) - Can be Private Hotel - Can be Held by Private Individual

          • Use - Has to be Partially / Fully by State

        • Art 15(3)

          • Summary Special Provision for Women & Children

          • What is Art 15(3)

            Confers Power upon the State for making any special provisions for women and children

          • Background

            1. is a Enabling Provision for Women and Children
            2. It is Dependent on the Will and Wish of State
            3. Women and Children cannot Demand the Right

          • Example

            1. 73rd 74th CAA - Special Provision for Women
              1. Gave Constitutional Status to Panchayats and Municipalities
              2. Reservation for Women - 33% to Women
            2. Odd and Even Policy in Delhi - Special Provision for Women
              1. Men have to follow Odd & Even
              2. Women can drive any day
            3. Juvenile Justice - Care & Protection of Children Act, 2015 - Special Provision for Children
              1. Juvenile is someone below 18 Years of Age
              2. Criminal Offence Punishment for Adult & Children is not Same

        • Art 15(4)

          • Summary Special Provision for SEBC/SC/ST Institute : Govt Institution

          • What is Art 15(3)

            Confers Powers upon the State to Make Special Provisions for the Advancement of any socially and educationally backward classes or the Schedule caste and Schedule Tribes

          • Background

            1. For SEBC / SC / ST
            2. Added by First Constitutional Amendment Act, 1951
            3. and the History for it is

          • Champakam Dorairajan v/s State of Madras Case, 1951

        • Art 15(5) - Confers power upon the state to make special provisions by law for the advancement of any socially educationally backward classes or the for the schedule caste or for the scheduled tribes in so far as such special provisions relating to their admission to educational institutions including private educational institutions whether aided or unaided by the state except minority educational institutions

          Why Article 15(50 when there is Article 15 (4) ?

          1. Supreme Court Judgement in Inamdar Case 2005
            1. Powers Conferred to state doesn't extend to giving reservation in Private Educational Institutions
            2. Added by 93rd CAA
          2. Special Provision for SEBC/SC/ST
          3. Exception : Minority Educational Institution

        • Art 15(6) - Confers Power Upon the State for the Advancement of any EWS of citizens through reservation in Educational Institutions not exceeding 10 Percent

          1. Based on Economically Weaker Section
          2. Added by 103rd Constitutional Amendment Act, 2019
          3. This 103rd CAA was challenged in court of Law. Given that CAA can only be challenged if it affects the Basic Structure Doctrine
          4. Challenged on the basis of
            1. in Indira Sawhney Case, 1992 SC said that

              1. Reservation is Approved but

              2. The Reservation Should not be above 50%

                1. OBC - 27
                2. SC - 15
                3. ST - 7.5

                Total : 49.5

                EWS : 10

              3. SC maintained that any reservation above 50% is violative of rule of law

          • Condition for being a EWS

      • Art 16 - Equality of Opportunity in Public Employment (7)

        • Art 16

          • Article is

            Equality of opportunity in Matters of Public Employment

          • Exceptions to Art 16

            • Presidential Guards

              • Only People from 3 Caste can be in Presidential Guards

                1. Rajputs
                2. Sikhs
                3. Jaat Sikhs

              • Petition was filed against this in Delhi High Court

              • Govt Replied that

                People from these 3 Castes → They have uniform body structure

              • This case is Sub Judice Right Now

            • Women in Army for Permanent Commission
              1. SSC - Short Service Commission
              2. Indian Army SSC - 14 Years of Service
              3. Permanent Commission from SSC → can serve upto Retirement Age
              4. But Women weren't allowed to get into Permanent Commission earlier
              5. Now, in some fields permanent commission has been allowed for women ; Like in
                1. Recruitment Division
                2. Ordinance
                3. Traffic
                4. Non Combat Divisions
                5. Not in all fields
              6. Reasons Given by Army
                1. Biological Factors
                  1. May have to go on Long Leave and it will be strategically bad for army
                2. Socio Cultural Factors
                  1. Most of the Jawans are from Rural Background, They look upto leaders
                    1. Who are More Stronger
                    2. Women Might have difficulties in commanding leadership
                3. Operational Constraints
              7. in 2021, SC didn't accept these factors

        • Art 16 (1)

          • Article is

            There Shall be Equality of Oppurtunity for all citizens in Employment to any office under the state

        • Art 16 (2)

          • Article is

            There shall be no discrimination among any citizen only on the grounds of religions, race, caste, sex, descent, place of birth, residence, or any of them for employment under office of the state

        • Art 16(3)

          • Article is

            Parliament shall have the power to make laws regarding residence as a requirement for certain employment or offices under the states

          • Its an Enabling Provision → To make a Law → for Local Residents only can Apply for the Jobs

          • The Services can be Reserved in case of Sub Ordinate Services only i.e → Group C & Group D Services

          • Example : Public Employment (Requirement as to Residence) Act, 1957

            1. Certain Jobs in : Manipur, Himachal Pradesh, Andhra Pradesh, Tripura
            2. in all these places : Reserved only for Local Residents

        • Art 16(4)

          • Article is

            Confers Power upon the State to make provisions for reservation of post in favour of backward classes of citizens

          • First Backward Classes Commission, 1953 (KaKa Kalelkar Commission)

          • Second Backward Classes Commission, 1979 (Mandal Commission)

            1. Appointed by Morar Ji Desai Govt after Emergency, Govt Collapsed in 1980
            2. Implemented by V P Singh Government in 1990
            3. Recommendation of Mandal Commission 27 % Reservation to OBC in Central Government Jobs to elevate their position

          • Final Recommendations

            1. 27% OBC Reservation in Centre & State Govt Jobs - Added by Mandal Commission
            2. 27% OBC Reservation in Successive Promotion - Added by 77th CAA

          • Indira Sawhney Case, 1992

            1. 27% Reservation of OBC in Central Government Jobs was challenged in Court
            2. SC upheld 27% Reservation of OBC in Central Government Jobs
            3. Conditions Put Forth by Government
              • Concept of Creamy Layer
                1. SC & ST is Homogeneous Community
                2. OBC is Not
                3. OBC Creamy Layer - are Socially & Educationally Advanced
                4. Hence Creamy Layer should be excluded from Reservation
                5. and Non Creamy Layer OBC should be Given Reservation
                6. Reservation only should be given to Deserving People
              • No Reservation in Promotion
              • 50% Ceiling Limit in Reservation
                1. If this Limit is surpassed, rule of law for general will be violated
                2. in Both Centre & State Government

          • Current Reservation Status

            1. OBC - 27%
            2. SC - 15%
            3. ST - 7.5%
            4. EWS - 10%

            Total : 59.5%

          • How is Reservation of more than 50% been Given in some states

            • Maharashtra Case - Reservation of 50% - Struck Down by SC
              1. Old Reservation was 46-48% Reservation
              2. Backward Commission was Appointed by State Govt to study that Maratha Community will fall under OBC or not
              3. Backward Commission said that Maratha Community is in Backward Classes
              4. Hence Maharashtra Government, Gave Reservation and total Reservation was 64%
              5. First to HC → Then to SC → SC Struck Down the Reservation citing
                1. It goes over and above 50% Rule
                2. Maratha Community is Advanced Community both Socially & Educationally
            • Tamil Nadu Case - Reservation of 69% - Sub Judice in SC

              • Prelude & Backdrop

                1. 50% Rule Violated by Giving 69% Violation
                2. Tamil Nadu Reservation & Educational Institutions & Public Employment Act, 1994
                3. Actions
                  1. State Govt can pass the Law
                  2. Only Parliament add to 9th Schedule by means of CAA
                4. TN Govt Requested the Parliament to Place this Law under 9th Schedule - Hence it becomes Immune to Judicial Review
                5. Hence 69 Percent Reservation Continued

              • IR Coelho Case, 2007

                1. Until Now 9th Schedule was Immune to Judicial Review
                2. IR Coelho argued that the By Putting any Laws in 9th Schedule of Constitution & Giving them Blanket Immunity It Violated Basic Structure Doctrines Part = Judicial Review
                3. Hence SC upheld that Laws Under 9th Schedule which violate Basic Structure Doctrine can be subject to Judicial Review
                4. Doctrine of Basic Structure came into Existence 24 April 1973
                  1. After 24 April 1973 && Violates Basic Structure - Can be Challenged
                  2. Before 24 April 1973 && Violates Basic Structure - Cannot be Challenged

              • TN Reservation 69% Case

                FOR REMOVING 69% RESERVATION

                1. 69% Reservation was given by a Act in 1995
                2. Which is After 1973
                3. Also it violates Rule of Law && Rule of Law is Part of Basic Structure Doctrine

                AGAINST REMOVING 69% RESERVATION

                1. Mandal Commission Whole India OBC was 52% Reservation was 27%
                2. Where as TN Has 90% OBC Hence More Affirmative Action is needed Hence More Reservation is Needed

        • Art 16(4)(A)

          • Article is

            Reservation in Promotion by State in favour of SC & ST

          • How Much Reservation ?

            Varies in Centre and in State to State

          • Reservation in ?

            Centre & State

        • Art 16(5)

          • Article is

            Confers power upon the state to make appointment to any religious denominations belonging to a particular religion

          • Example : Tamil Nadu Hindu Religious Charitable & Endowment Act, 1959

            1. Approximately 30,000 Temples are under control of Management of State of Tamil Nadu
            2. State can appoint : Manager & Priests

        • Art 16(6)

          • Article is

            Confers Power upon the State to make reservation to public offices for categories of persons belonging to economically weaker section (EWS) not exceeding 10%

          • Inserted by 103rd CAA

            Inserted both 15(6) - EWS in Educational Institutions

            Inserted both 16(6) - EWS in Matters of Public Employment

          • This Amendment is Still Sub Judice

      • Art 17 - Abolition of Untouchability (1)

        • Art 17

          Abolition of Untouchability

        • Untouchability

          1. is a Form of Social Disability
          2. is a Social Evil
          3. is against the concept of & Denies Equality
          4. Still Prevalent in Some Rural Parts of India

        • Art 17 Says that

          Disability arising out of practise of untouchability is a punishable offence

        • But

          It doest define untouchability

        • Laws Passed by Government

          • Untouchability Offences Act, 1955
            1. Defines Untouchability & Defines Punishment of Offence
            2. Made Untouchability
              1. Non Cognizable & Bailable Offence
              2. Non Cognizable Offence
                1. Police cannot arrest without a Warrant
              3. Bailable Offences
                1. Is a Matter if Right
                2. Only a Charge Stands now
                3. He is Not Guilty Yet
                4. Can be Given by Police or Magistrate
          • Protection of Civil Right's Act, 1976
            1. Defines Untouchability & Defines Punishment of Offence
            2. Made Untouchability
              1. Cognizable & Non Bailable
              2. Cognizable
                1. Police Can Arrest w/o a Warrant
              3. Non Bailable Offence
                1. Is not a Matter of Right

      • Art 18 - Abolition of Titles (1)

        1. Titles are hereditary in Character like : Raja, Maharaja, Nawabs
        2. This Created a Lot of
          1. Social Inequality in Society
          2. Artificial Distinction in Society
        3. CA felt that this goes against Equality of Status
        4. Balaji Raghavan v/s Union of India Case, 1996
          1. Courts Allowed Presentation of Awards by Indian State
          2. Indian Citizen cannot received Awards but not Titles
        5. Except - Academic & Military Titles
        6. Objectives
          1. to avoid social equality & artificial distinction

    • Art 19 - Art 22 → Right to Freedom (5)

      • Art 19 - Protection of Certain Rights regarding Freedom of Speech (6)

        • Articles 19 & Its Allied Restrictions - A Summary

          Art 19 1 A - FoSE Art 19 2 - 8 RR

          Art 19 A B - Assemble w/o Arms Art 19 3 - 2 RR

          Art 19 A C - Association Union Co Operatives Art 19 4 - 3 RR

          Art 19 A D - Move Freely Territory of India Art 19 5 - 2 RR

          Art 19 A E - Reside & Settle Art 19 5 - 2 RR

        • Art 19(1)(a) - Right to Freedom of Speech and Expression

          • What is Freedom of Speech & Expression ?
            1. Expression of Ideas, Beliefs, thought process, Faith and Ideologies subject to Reasonable Restrictions
          • How can it be Expressed ?
            1. In the form of Picture, Writing, Speaking, Audio Video & Other Modes
          • Is it Absolute ?
            1. It is not Absolute & is subject to Reasonable Restriction
          • What is the Definition of Right to Freedom of Speech and Expression
            1. Definition : It is the Fundamental Right of the Citizens to express their ideas, views, beliefs, thoughts in the form of word of mouth, writing, painting, picturing, etc
          • How is Democracy Affected by FoSE ?
            1. It is the Essence of Participatory Democracy in the World
          • What are the Important Dimensions of Rt to FoSE ?
            • Right to Freedom of Press
              1. In Romesh Thapar v/s Union of India, 1950, Supreme Court declared the Right to Freedom of Press is Implied under RFoSE
              2. Right to Freedom of Press
                1. Protects Pre Censorship
                2. Right to Propagate
                3. Right to Publish
                4. Press Cant be Subjected to Arbitrary Taxation
            • Right to Criticise
              1. Kedarnath v/s State of Bihar,
            • Right to Remain Silent
              1. Bijoe Immanuel v/s State of Kerala,
              2. Students not singing national anthem, not disturbing anyone but not singing
              3. SC Held that staying silent is not a sign of non patriotism and is the right under FoSE
            • Right to Know the Criminal Antecedents of Candidates Contesting Election
              1. ADR - NGO to being Electoral Reforms in Country
              2. Association for Democratic Reforms (ADR) v/s Union of India Case
              3. SC Held that - People contesting election should declare their Criminal Antecedents

          • Can the Rt to FoSE be Restricted ?

            Yes, as it is subject top Reasonable Restriction

          • How many grounds on which FoSE can be restricted ?

            in total 8.

          • Who can Restrict it ?

            Only State can by means of Reasonable Restriction

          • How can the FoSE be Restricted ?

            It can only be restricted by a way of a Law

          • What are the Grounds ?

            • Sovereignty & Integrity of India - Law Pending

              Example : anybody who would write or say something that affects the Sovereignty of India

              Example : a Separatist Leader of Kashmir

              Example : Anti India Comments on Social Media can be Removed

            • Security of the State

              Law : Unlawful Prevention of Activities Act, 1967

            • Friendly Relation with Foreign State - Law Pending

              Example : Someone is Authoring a Book, in which it consists some information which can strain the relation of India and some country

              Hence, India on the basis of Reasonable Restriction can either completely ban or partly remove

            • 4 Public Order

              Law :

              Sec 153 A - Hate Speech → Incitement of Offence → Disrupted Public Orders

            • 5 Decency or Morality

              Decency or Morality : Means of Norms of Society

              Law : Indian Cinematographic Act, 1952

              Body : Central Board for Film Certification (CBFC) - Does Pre Censorship of Movies

              • Why is Pre Censorship only to movies and not Press ?

                Context : the content of motion pictures can affect the minds of society and frame narration

                1. Not Pre Censored - Books, Press (Online & Offline), Newspaper
                2. Pre Censored - Movies
                  1. It has huge impact on audience
                  2. something that people may like unlike book
                  3. you can easily convey message
                  4. actors are looked upon as role models

            • 6 Contempt of Court

              1. Contempt of Court : Showing disrespect to the court including criticising the judges and their decisions
              2. Example : Prashant Bhushan
              3. Law : Contempt of Court Act, 1971

            • 7 Defamation

              Reasonable Restriction to : Defamation Law : Section 499 of IPC

            • 8 Incitement to an Offence

              Reasonable Restriction to : Incitement to an Offence or Hate Speech either Libel (written) or Slander (Speaking) to defame an Individual or a section of Society Law : Sec 153 A of CrPC

          • Some Controversial Restrictions

            • Sedition

              • What is the Definition of Sedition ?

                1. the use of words or actions that are intended to encourage people to be or act against a government
                2. Any Action (words, signs or visible representation) that attempts to bring hatred or contempt or dissatisfaction or disloyalty towards the government established by Lae in India

              • What is the Law for it ?

                Under Section 124 A of IPC Sedition is an Punishable Offence

                Punishment Range : 3 Yrs to 14 Yrs

              • What is the current Petition in SC ?

                to have a Sedition or to do away with it

              • What is the Background of this Law in India ?

                1. IPC came into existence in 1860
                2. 1870 Amendment brought in Sec 124 A under IPC
                3. It was brought in to control the actions of freedom fighters
                4. Some Freedom Fighters who were found guilty of Sedition in British Times - Tilak & Gandhi
                5. Gandhi Ji even pleaded guilty of this Offence

              • Will Criticism Amount to Sedition ?

                In Kedarnath v/s State of Bihar Case, SC held that Mere Criticism of Government will not amount to Sedition

              • How many Sedition Charges are actually convicted ?

                According to NCRB, in 97% cases the case put forth by govt is nullified and convicts are acquitted

                Hence Conviction Rate is only 3%

                NCRB : National Crime Records Bureau

              • What are the Criticism of Sedition Law ?

                1. Sedition Law is a Colonial Legacy and in a democratic country sedition law should not exist

                2. It has chilling affect on the rights of FoSE, especially on the Art 19(1)(a)

                  Its a Paradox that on one side scope & interpretation of FoSE is being widened and on the other hand Sedition Law is restricting it.

                3. Even U K doesn't have such Law.

                4. JLN personally remarked Sedition Law to be : “Highly Objectionable & Obnoxious”

              • Why Should Sedition Law be Continued ? - PENDING TO ADD

              • Kedarnath v/s State of Bihar, 1962

                • 1 Kedarnath : Member of CPI Party in Bihar

                • 2 Essence of Statement

                  India has gained freedom from Britishers but India will actually be free when India will be free of Congress and we will work towards it

                • 3 Congress Applied Sedition Law on Kedarnath

                • 4 He Challenged this in Court saying

                  1. Application of Sedition doesn't Apply here
                  2. The Sedition Law Sec 124 A in itself is unconstitutional as Sec 124 A directly violates Art 19(1)(a)

                • 5 Supreme Court has to decide that ?

                  • Whether Sec 124 A is constitutional or not ?

                    1. SC held that Sec 124 A is Constitutional
                    2. Because FoSE is not Absolute
                    3. cuz of Diversity there may actually be instances of Sedition
                    4. But it cannot be used Arbitaraily

                  • If it is constitutional, actions of Kedarnath is sedition or not ?

                    Kedarnath was not found guilty of Sedition

                • 6 Supreme Courts Guideline in application of Sedition Law

                  • What Does Not Amount to Sedition ?
                    1. Mere Criticism of Government, no Matter how stringent will and shall not amount to Sedition
                    2. Mere Discussion & Advocacy cant be Seditious
                  • What Does Amount to Sedition ?
                    1. By Any Action of an Individual leading to clear & immediate violence against the State will constitute Sedition

              • Balwant Singh Case v/s State of Punjab, 1995

                1. A Group of Sikh Individuals shouted “Khalistan Zindabad” after assassination of Indira Gandhi
                2. SC Said that what they said is not Patriotic
                3. but their actions didn’t lead to any violence and hence they were Acquitted

              • Aseem Trivedi v/s State of Maharashtra, 2011

                Cartoonist Criticised Congress, Arrested for Cartoon, Acquitted

              • Kanhaiya Kumar v/s State of Union, 2016

              • Jignesh Mevani v/s State of Gujarat, 2019

              • Should Sedition be continued

                1. Matter was Referred to Law Commission of India

                2. Law Commission of India

                  Law Commission of India is a non-statutory body and is constituted by a notification of the Government of India

                3. Law Commission Suggested in 2011 that Sedition is not Favourable.

              • Current Status of Sedition

                1. It is Currently Sub Judice in the Supreme Court
                2. SC said that until the decision is made, all sedition charges should be put at hold

              • Question : Mains

                • Should India do away with the Sedition Law ? Critically Examine.
                • Critically Examine : Both Pros and Cons
                • Finally Take a Rational Stand

              • The Mid Way - Way Forward

                Dont Scrap ; Dont Current

                1. The Laws Definition should be made more clear
                2. It is currently cognisable, this can be altered a bit
                3. More Procedural Safeguards in the Application of Law
                4. Change in Semantics will reduce the Social Degradation and Media Trial of the Blamed
                5. Most Sedition Law are applied on Journalists, the Journalists made requests that, before arresting people, HC should hear the conviction plea
                6. Mala fide Intention of Application of Law can be defined
                7. Ambiguity should be reduced. Rather than 'govt', it should only be contempt against the country/ 'India'…
            • Hate Speech

              • What is Hate Speech ?

                Hate Speech is an Incitement to hatred Against a Particular group of persons such as a Particular race, religion, caste residence, sexual orientation etc especially in circumstances in which the communication is likely to provoke violence

              • Current Law Status ?

                There is no Separate Law but a No of Law

                • Sec 153 A CrPC

                  Offence of Promoting Enmity or Feelings of Hatred on the Grounds of Religion, race, place of birth, language, residence etc

                • Sec 505 CrPC

                  Promotion of Fake News

                  Publication of Circulation of material aimed at causing public mischief or enmity of ill will

                • Sec 123(3) of Representation of Peoples Act, 1951

                  Regulates hate speech by election candidate

              • Case of Religious Parliament in Uttarakhand ?

                Religious Leaders passed open resolutions against minority

                Court Summoned Chief Secretary and asked can it be considered a Hate Speech

              • Mains Question

                FoSE

                Hate Speech - Art 19(2)

                Films in India - Pre Censorship

        • Art 19(1)(b) - Right to assemble peaceably & without arms

          • Conditions of Peace

            It Confers Fundamental Rights on Citizens to assemble peacefully without arms in public places

            Conditions Are :

            Peaceful Manner

            In Public Place

            Without Arms

          • Grounds on which it can be restricted

            Protecting Sovereignty and Integrity of India

            • AFSPA → Armed Forces Special Powers Act, 1958
              1. Invoked in Places considered to be a disturbed area
              2. Disturbed Area : Insurgency Activities, Separatist Activities, Terrorist Activities
              3. Example : J&K & North East States
              4. With Application of AFSPA, Many Rights are Curtailed
                1. Assembly of People even if its a peaceful protest is NOT ALLOWED

            Public Order

            • Section 144
              1. Prevents Assembly & Movement of People, Despite Order if people assemble it can be considered illegal

        • Art 19(1)(c) - Right to form Association or Union or Co Operative Societies

          • Article Says ?

            Article 19(1)(c) confers rights upon the Citizen to form Association, Unions and Co - Operatives

          • When was the word Co Operatives Added ?

            Co Operatives was Added by : 97th CAA, 2011

          • Meaning of Association ?

            Group of People coming together for a common cause Can be a Cultural Political Sports Caste Individuals have the Right to form Association To be a Part of Association is NOT the Right

          • Meaning of Cooperatives ?

            Cooperative is a form of association but on economic terms

          • Meaning of Union ?

            1. Union is also a form of association but the differences are
            2. Forming an Union is a Fundamental Right but the Union has to be Registered under Trade Union Act, 1926
            3. With Registration comes a Legal Obligation for Collective Bargain of Union and Employer or Opposite Party Allowed

          • Difference Between Union & Association

          • Examples of Unions

            • AITUC - All India Trade Union Congress
            • INTUC - Indian National Trade Union Congress
            • BMS - Bhartiya Mazdoor Sangh
            • BKU - Bhartiya Kisaan Union

          All Unions are Association but not all Associations are Union

          • Reasonable Restrictions
            • Grounds of
              1. Sovereignty & Integrity of India
              2. Public Order
              3. Morality
            • Banned Unions
              • ULFA - United Liberation front of Asom
              • PLA - Peoples Liberation Army
              • LTTE - Liberation Tigers of Tamil Elum
              • IM - Indian Mujahideen
              • Al Qaida

        • Art 19(1)(d) - Right to Freedom of Movement

          • Article Says

            To Move Freely Throughout Territory of India

          • Grounds of Reasonable Restrictions

            1. Interest of General Public
              1. Epidemic Act, 1897 - Legality of Quarantine
              2. Sec 144 CrPC - Partying and Movement is Curtailed
            2. Protection of Interests of Any Schedule Tribes
              1. To Prevent the Contamination of Way of Living of Indigenous People
              2. Bengal Eastern Frontier Restriction Act - Inner Line Permit Areas
              3. To Enter into this Area, you have to take permission from State - Also called Quasi Visa System
              4. States covered under ILP are : MINA + Ma
                1. Mizoram
                2. Imphal
                3. Nagaland
                4. Arunachal Pradesh
                5. Manipur
              5. Britishers also had this Inner Line Permit System but there motive was to protect there vested economic interests

        • Art 19(1)(e) - Right to Reside & Settle in any part of territory of India

          • Article Says
          • Grounds of Reasonable Restrictions
            1. Interests of General Public
              1. Disasters Management Act - authorities can order leaving an area
              2. Coastal Regulation Zone
              3. Nuclear Power Plant Buffer Zone
              4. Inner Line Permit System is for a Fixed Period
            2. Protection of Interests of any Schedule Tribe

        • Art 19(1)(g) - Right to Profession, Occupation, Trade, Business

          • Article - Important for Economic Livelihood of Citizens

            Right to Practise any profession or to carry any occupation, tade or business

          • Ground of Reasonable Restriction

            • Technical Qualification Restriction

              1. Lawyer - Law Degree
              2. Doctor - MBBS Degree

            • Interest of General Public - Public Safety | Health | General Welfare → of People

              Public Safety

              1. Nuclear Power Plant Right not given to Private Individuals

              General Welfare

      • Art 20 - Protection in respect of conviction for offences (3)

        • Basics
          1. Presence of this right prevents India from becoming a Police State
          2. Conviction : Someone has been found Guilty of Some Offence
        • Protection Given Under Art 20

          • Art 20(1) → No Ex Post Facto Criminal Legislation

            • Types of Legislations are ?

              • Civil Legislation
                1. Property Disputes
                2. Family Disputes
                3. Marriage
                4. Divorce
              • Criminal Legislations
                1. Murder
                2. Domestic Violence
              • What is the Point of Difference for Civil and Criminal Legislation
                1. Criminal Offences are considered to be a Crime Against the Society Hence, State is the Prosecuting Authority
                2. Civil Offence are often Interpersonal in Nature Hence, Two Parties Involved can either be State or Individuals

            • Which type of Legislation is it applied to

              It is applied only to Criminal Legislation

            • What is - No Ex Post Facto Criminal Legislations

              • Case 1 - Making a New Law All together
                1. No Criminal Legislations CAN NOT be given effect from BACK DATE
                2. All Criminal Legislation CAN be Implemented from either TODAY or from a FUTURE DATE
                3. Hence, Criminal Laws in Country can be Prospective but not Retrospectively
              • Case 2 - Changing the Law
                1. No Increase in Punishment for any criminal offence retrospectively
              • Can Punishment Decrease from a Back Date in Criminal Offence ?
                1. Yes, it is not prohibited by constitution

            • Can State give effect to Civil Legislation Retrospectively ?

              1. Yes

          • Art 20(2) → No Double Jeopardy

            Meaning of Double Jeopardy

            1. Double Jeopardy → Double Prosecution & Double Punishment
            2. For a Same Offence at a Single Point in time, a person cannot be punished twice

          • Art 20(3) → No Self Incriminating Evidence

            • Article Says

              Accused Immunity from being Compelled to Give Evidence Against Himself

            • The Procedure

              • First of All : Police Should Not Torture Someone to Give Evidence Against Self
              • Second of All : Even if the accused is forced to give evidence against themselves (oral or document)
              • Now if Person Says in front of Magistrate that he gave evidence against himself by force, the current evidence will be considered null & void

            • Also Provides Protection from

              Narco Test & Polygraph Test can only be done by the permission of the convicted

            • But SC said that it is not an Immunity Against Cooperation with police in investigation

      • Art 21 - Protection of Life & Personal Liberty

        • SC View on Art 21

          Has been given the Widest Interpretation

        • Article Says

          No Person shall be deprived of his life or personal liberty except according to the procedure established by Law

        • Keywords

          • No Person - Rt to both Citizen and Alien
          • Except According to Procedure Established by Law - by Following Procedure Established by Law

        • Meaning of Procedure Established by Law

          1. SC said that meaning of Procedure Established by Law is same as Due Process of Law as in the USA Constitution

        Right to Life

        • Meaning of Right to Life
          1. SC Said : Rt to Life means Rt to Dignified Life
            1. First time said by SC in Menaka Gandhi Case
          2. Rt to Life is something more than mere animal existence or physical act of breathing
        • Rt Implied under Art 21 - Rt to Life
          • Rt Against Sexual Harassment - Vishakha Case
          • Rt to Livelihood
          • Rt to have a clean environment
            1. Against Pollution
          • Rt to Die with Dignity

        Right to Personal Liberty

        • Liberty Means

          A set of actions that a man can undertake in pursuit of happiness by a free man which are subject to certain restrictions

          Not just Rt Against Bodily Restraints rather all those rights & liberties that are essential for the orderly pursuit of happiness of a free man

        • Right Implied under Art 21 - Rt to Personal Liberty

          • Rt to Privacy

          • Rt against Telephone Tapping

          • Rt against Hand Cuffing

            If Ind cooperates in arrest, he shouldn’t be handcuffed

          • Rt against Solitary Confinement

          • Rt to Speedy Justice

          • Rt to Travel Abroad

        Case Studies

        • Two Concepts

          • Procedure Established by Law
            1. There Should be a Law
            2. The Law should prescribe a Procedure & that should be followed
            3. Not Concerned whether the procedure mentioned in the Law is just, fair or reasonable
          • Due Process of Law
            1. Procedure Established by Law (First 2 Points) +
            2. Whether the Procedure Mentioned in the Law is Just Fair and Reasonable

        • What is the Difference between Procedure Established by Law & Due Process of Law ?

          Due Process of Law focuses on the Procedure Mentioned in the Law is Just, Fair, Reasonable

        • A K Gopalan v/s State of Madras, 1950

          • The Case

            1. A K Gopalan was a member of Communist Party
            2. There was intel of a mass protest
            3. Police arrested him on a Preventive Detention
            4. he challenged it in Supreme Court

          • Details of Preventive Detention

            1. Before committing the crime, on anticipation of police, an individual can be arrested
            2. Preventive Detention can happen by a Law
            3. Preventive Detention Act, 1950

          • Gopalan Argued

            Gopalan Argued that his Rt to Life & Liberty is violated

            He challenged Preventive Detention Act also

          • SC said

            1. Rt to Life is not absolute
            2. It checked for Procedure Established by Law
              1. Is there a Law - Yes
              2. Was the Procedure Followed - Yes
            3. Hence SC is only concerned until here

          • Criticism

            The Narrowness of Procedure Established by Law

            The Procedure’s Just, Fair, Arbitrariness is not considered

        • Menaka Gandhi v/s Union of India, 1978 - Watch Part of Lecture 22/10

          • The Case

            1. Menaka Gandhi had to travel foreign
            2. Regional Passport Authority asked her to surrender the passport by means Indian Passport Act
            3. She Surrendered the Passport & asked the reason for it
            4. Regional Passport Officer Said : In Public Interests we cannot disclose the reason
            5. Regional Passport Officer had the right to do so under Indian Passport Act

          • Filling of Case in Supreme Court

            1. Menaka Gandhi with this reply filed a petition in SC and argued that this violated her
            2. Art 14
              1. Discretion of Presiding Officer - in which case to give reasons and in which case to not give reason
              2. This Discretion leads to Arbitrariness of officer and thus Leads to Arbitrariness of State
            3. Art 19
            4. Art 21
              1. Passport has been Seized - Cannot Travel Abroad & Cannot Return back
              2. Violates Rt to Life & Liberty
            5. Hence She argued that
              1. Procedure Established by Law was unfair - Rt to Life

          • SC Upheld that

            Only if we apply the Due Process of Law

            Individuals Rt to Life & Liberty can be maintained

        Case Studies Related to Interpretation of Article 21

        • Rt Against Sexual Harassment at Workplace - Watch Last Part of Lecture on 22/10
          1. Declared to be a Fundamental Right by SC in Vishakha Case
          2. Principle of Locus Standi - What your interests and how are u affected by this case
          3. Traditional Way - Only People who are affected can go to court
          4. Any one exposed to Sexual Harassment will not have a dignified life
          5. Outcome
            1. Training and Awareness has to be given
            2. Creation of Sexual Harassment Compliance Committee
            3. It will be headed by a women
            4. will have representation from Local NGO concerned with this things to prebtn pressure from company
            5. Committe will recommend Action
              1. Legal Action
              2. Action According to Conduct Rules of Service Conditions of Employees
          6. in 2013 - Prevention of Sexual Harassment Act was made in 2013, hence for a long time Sc guide,ines acted as a tool to prevent sexual harrasment
        • Rt to Livelihood
          1. It became a Fundamental Right after Pavement Dwellers Case in Mumbai Municipal Corporation.
          2. SC said that Municipal Corporation was correct in doing it but at the same time Rt to Livelihood is a implied Rt in Rt to Life
          3. Hence Suggestion by Supreme Court was of making Hawking and Non Hawking Zone
        • Rt to Clean Environment
          • M C Mehta Case - Polluting of River Wanter Ganga
          1. Features
            1. Polluter’s Pay
            2. Cost of Implementation of Filtering Plants by the company
          2. Banning of Crackers - Enforcement is Difficult, but it is Regulated
            1. To Ensure Noise Pollution
            2. To Ensure Air Pollution
        • Rt to Privacy
          • M P Sharma v/s Satish Chandra, 1954
          • Kharak Singh v/s State of Uttar Pradesh, 1962
          • International Bodies which Recognise 2011

            2. European Union
            3. Universal Declaration of Human Rights

              UIDAI would collect

              1. Biometric
              2. Demographic
          • Dimensions of Rt to Privacy
            • Physical Privacy
              1. Home
              2. Even State needs Search Warrant
              3. Already Existed but not as a Fundamental Right
            • Informational Privacy
              1. use of personal data of individual related to Personal Data Protection
              2. Other Should not use personal data of individual
              3. Personal data is something from which one can be recognised
            • Decisional Privacy
              1. Whatever decisions an individual makes can have no interference by any individual
              • Decision on Sexual Orientation
                1. Sec 377 - Criminalises Sex Against Order of Nature LGBT - Lesbian, Gay, Bisexual, Trans LGBTQ+ → Q Questioning & Queer
                2. Delhi HC gave some remedy in 2010
                3. but SC turned it down in 2013, SC declared in 2013 that Sec 377 is correct
                4. SC has read down some part of 377
                5. Current Laws is :
                  1. Consenting Adults
                  2. Any Form of Sexual Orientation
                  3. in Private Space

            1. Right to be Left Alone
            2. Right to be Forgotten
            3. Right to have any Sexual Orientation

          • Reasonable Restriction
            1. Medical Records

        • Euthanasia (Rt to Die) & Rt to Execute a Living Will

          • What is Euthanasia

            1. Procedure through which you medically terminate the life of an individual

          • Whom Does it Apply to ?

            Applicable to Only those people who are in

            1. Permanent Vegetative State
            2. Permanent State of Coma
            3. Brain Dead Patients

          • Is there a Law on Euthanasia ?

            There is no Law in India on Euthanasia as such, it only works on SC Guidelines until now

          • How is Euthanasia different from Suicide

            1. Sec 309 of IPC gives punishment of 2 Years if suicide fails
            2. Natural Death of Ind is already commenced, he is in the process of dying, his life is not dignified in manner and hence dignified death be given to him/her
            3. in Suicide, the Natural Death has not yet commenced

          • What are the Types of Euthanasia ?

            Mere Procedural Difference of Providing Euthanasia

            • Active Euthanasia - Not Legal in India
              1. Something is Actively Administered by Doctor to Terminate the Life of Patients
              2. Ex : Lethal Poisonous Injection Administered
            • Passive Euthanasia - India Allows this
              1. Medical Practitioner remains passive to the needs of patient
              2. Ex : He doesn't replace the Medical Support System

          • Aruna Ramchandra Shanbag v/s Union of India, 2011

            1. Implemented the Execution of Living Will
            2. Background
              1. Working as Nurse in Mumbai Hospital
              2. Didnt had good relationship with Ward Boy
              3. Ward Boy Strangled and Left her in Room
              4. She wasted a Lot of Blood, She got into coma
              5. Until Present She didn't gain any consciousness

          • SC Guidelines

            1. Doctors Team will give a report
            2. Kith & Kin has to be informed
            3. Family has to be taken permission from High Court
            4. Ask Immediate Family
            5. If Not, Ask Relatives
            6. If Not, Ask Care Takers

          • NGO Common Cause, 2018

            1. Rt to Execute a Living Will

            2. What is a Living Will ?

          • Consent of Patients

            • Voluntary
            • Non Voluntary

        Important Point

        Due Process of Law will be Applied to Cases of Art 21 only

        But, Supreme Court has devised its own mechanism to keep a check on the Just & Fair Nature of Procedure

      • Art 21 A - Right to Education

        • Article Says

          Free & Compulsory Rt to Education

        • Background Development

          • Added by 86th CAA, 2002
          • Before it was made a Fundamental Right it was considered implied under Art 21 i.e Rt to Life and Liberty

        • Mohini Jain v/s State of Karnataka, 1992

          1. in 1992, Mohini Jain v/s State of Karnataka declared that Rt to Education
          2. upto Higher Education (Upto Tertiary Education)

        • Unnikrishnan v/s State of Andhra Pradesh, 1993

          1. Rt to Education will be considered a FR under Art 21 upto Primary Education

        • 86th CAA, 2002

        • Rt to Education Act, 2009

          1. Any Child upto 14 Years of Age
          2. has right to get Quality Education
          3. with Access to Free Books & Dress

          No Detention Clause

          1. This Act has a No Detention Clause
            1. with Enrolment, Retaining of Individual is also a must
            2. They will be promoted upto 8th Std
          2. But because of this Clause
            1. The quality of education had been degraded
            2. Math Skill of a Class 5 is of Class 1
            3. Language Skills worst
          3. Now they cannot be failed until Class 5
            1. After Class 5 there will be purview of District Education Officer

          Reservation

          1. In Govt School, No Once Can Deny Admission
          2. 25% Reservation of Seats in Private School for Disadvantaged Section (SC ST & BPL)
          3. Disadvantaged Section will be notified by the State Govt
          4. Operational Implementation of this Reservation due to Stigmatisation is Difficult
          5. Exception is : Minority Schools

      • Art 22 - Protection Against Arrest & Detention in Some Cases

        • Article Says

          Protection Against Arrest & Detention in Certain Cases

          Detention - Preventive & Punitive

        • Scheme of Things - Preventive & Punitive Detention

          • Punitive Detention

            • Defintion

            • Process

              A → Complains Against → B → Police Officer Acts → Cognisable Offence → w/o Warrant Arrest

              1. Judge if wants to extend the custody, will give the Judicial Custody - ie in Jail and not in police station
              2. After Investigation Report is submitted, and then he is bailed until judgement is given
              3. Result
                1. Found Guilty → Sent to Jail → Punitive Detention
                2. Not Found Guilty → Released

            • Rights of Person who is Arrested

              • Written

                1. Should be Informed the Reasons of his Arrest
                2. Has to be given a Legal Right of Lawyer
                3. Has to be produced in front of nearest Magistrate in front of a Judge within 24 Hours
                  1. Judge will give permission for extending the custody or giving the bail

              • Image

              • D K Basu v/s State of West Bengal

                1. Rt to see the Identity Card of Police Officer
                2. There has to be a Arrest Witness of a Person in locality
                3. Women has to be Arrested
                  1. in presence of a women police officer
                  2. after sunrise and before sunset

            • To Note

              1. The Trial Will Take Place
              2. Offence has already taken Place
          • Preventive Detention

            • Defintion

            • Example

              1. All Local Leaders were kept under house arrest
              2. A K Gopalan Case

            • To Note

              1. It is done in preventive basis
              2. The actual commission of offence has NOT been done
              3. Preventive Detention can happen only by way of Law
                1. Both Central & State Can Make Law
                  1. Centre - Applies in India
                  2. State - Applies to State
                2. Examples of Laws of Preventive Detention
                  1. PDA - Preventive Detention Act, 1950
                  2. MISA - Maintainence of Internal Security Act, 1971
                  3. NSA - National Security Act, 1980

            • Constitutional Provisions

              1. PD not beyond 3 Months w/o the approval of advisory board

                1. Advisory Board Consists of Judge of HC & Other Members

              2. Detention can not be beyond the period prescribed in the Law

                1. Ex : NSA Max Detention is 1 Year Detention

              3. Detainees are entitled to know the grounds of the detention

                Except : Public Interests

      • Art 24 - Prohibition of Employment of Children in Factories

          2. Factories Act, 1948
          3. Child Labour Prohibition Act, 1986
