    • Art 25 - Art 28 → Right to Freedom of Religion(4)

      • Background
        • Questions to Ask ?
          1. Positive Secularism has a Interventionist Approach
          2. To What Extent Can State Intervene in the Religion
        • Article in totality Says
          1. Profess, Practise, Propagate : The Freedom of his Choice
      • Art 25 - Individual Religious Rights

        • Article Says

          Propagate - Disseminate Information

          Practises - Rituals or Religious Practises

          Profess - Openly using his/her faith/belief

        • Important Points

          1. State Intervention is Possible in Matters of Religion → Positive Secularism

        • Reasonable Restriction

          1. Public Order

            1. Use of Loudspeakers

          2. Morality & Health

            Health

            1. Practise of Sallekhana & Santhara

            Morality

            1. Practise of Performing of Nudity by Monks of Digambar's

          3. Other Provisions of this Part

            1. If Rt to Freedom of Religion is violative of others FR
            • Forceful Conversion
              1. a Person wilfully can convert from religion to another religions by his faith and clarity of conscience
              2. but cannot be by means of force, fraud, inducement or fear

        • Doctrine of Essentiality

          Also called the Doctrine of Essential Religious Practise

          • Religious Practises can be divided into 2 Parts

            • Essentiality Religious Practise

              According to Supreme Court there are certain practises which are integral to the religion w/o which the practise of the religion is not possible

            • Non Essential Religious Practise

              However there are certain practises which are not integral to the religion. Such Practises doesn't affect the essence of the religion

          • Sirur Math Case, 1954

            1. Innovated by SC for the first time in this case

          • Santhara Case - Jains

            1. HC Said it is not
            2. SC it is Essentially Religious Practise
            3. Evidence Taken as
              1. Religious Texts
              2. Testimony Given by Religious Experts

          • Hijab Case - Muslims

            • One View - No

              Against Secularism & Equality

            • One View - Yes

              Rt to Life - 21

              Rt to Liberty - 19

              There is no Question to enter into Doctrine of Essentiality

            • Evidence Taken was

              1. Empirical Evidence
              2. Behavioural Issues

          • Sabrimala Case - Hindus

            • Why Not Entered

              God Placed in sacrosanctum practises Celibacy

              Hence entry of Active Menstruation Ladies is not allowed

            • SC Said

              Evidence - Scriptures & Religious Experts

              Violative of Art 17 & Art 25

          • Criticism of Doctrine of Essentiality

            1. Judges may be Legal Experts but they are not Religious Experts
            2. Methodology Used by Judges is Different in Different Cases
            3. In Totality, Why is Supreme Court Venturing into a domain, which is not its domain
      • Art 26 -Freedom to Manage Religious Affairs & Religious Denominations

        • Article Says

        • Meaning of Denominations

          1. Denomination means the Group of People
          2. Within a Religion there can be many Denomination
          3. Religious Denomination has not been defined in the constitution
          4. Definition by SC : It consists of a Religious Sect or a Body having common faith and organisation and designated by a distinct name

        • Rights Included within are

          • Rt to Establish Religious Denominations
          • Rt to Manage their Religious Affairs
          • Rt to do Charitable Activities
          • Rt to Acquire & Own ; Movable and Non Movable Property

        • Subject to Reasonable Restriction

      • Note : Art 25 Individual Art 26 Group
      • Art 27 - Religion cannot be a basis of Taxation Policy in Country
        1. No Tax on the Name of Religion ; Whatsoever
        2. Usage of Tax on Promotion of Religion
          1. Subsidy for People for Mansarowar Temple
          2. Building of Ram Temple, Contribution by States
          3. Hajj Subsidy Earlier there, Now not there
        3. Question of Fees collected in Various Temples in India
          1. collected only for secular activity and not for promoting religion
      • Art 28 - Can there be Religious Instructions

        • Article Says

          Religious Worships - Religious Practises cannot be forced upon students

          Religious Instructions - Preaching of Religious Texts and Scriptures

        • Three Types of Institutions

          • Educational institution wholly maintained by State
            1. No Religious Instruction - 100% Ban
          • Educational Institution Administered by State but Established under the Religious Endowment or Trust
            1. Religious Instructions are allowed
            2. Religious Endowment or Trust Act
              1. Estab of Madarsa
              2. Estab of Convent Schools
          • Private Educational Institutions recognised or aided by the State
            1. Religious Instruction or Worship can be Imparted only on Voluntary Basis
            2. Cannot be Forced to do it

        • What is the Exact Meaning of Religious Instructions

          1. Mere Teaching of Religious Peoples Lives, Like teachings and values of Guru Nanak is not a Religious Instruction

    • Art 29 - Art 31 → Culture & Educational Rights

      • Art 29 - Protection of Interest of Minorities - FOR ANY SECTION OF CITIZEN

        • Article Says

          1. Section of Citizens

            Can be a Minority or Majority - Any Section of Citizen

          2. Residing in Any Part

          3. Distinct Language, Script, Culture

          4. Shall Preserve by Any Means

            1. Like the Game of Jallikattu
            2. Initially banned
            3. Protest in Tamil Nadu
            4. Again Court Hearing and Finally Allowed with Slight Modification in Tamil Nadu
            • Jallikattu
              1. Taken up by PETA
                1. Asked for Ban of Jallikattu
                2. Cruelty of Animals to be Prevented
                3. Prevention of Cruelty to Animals Act
              2. What Does Jallikattu Do ?
                1. Practise leads to hurting of these animals
                2. usage of chilli powder on eyes and nose of animals
                3. physical abuse of animals to make it more violent
      • Art 30 - Right of Minority to Establish & Administer Educational Institutions - ONLY FOR MINORITY
        • in Art 29 - by any means
        • in Art 30 - by means of establishing educational institutions

          • Conditions

            1. Only if they(minority person or group) establish and then they will have right to administer
            2. No Unnecessary Interference from State

          • Art 30(1)

            • Article Says

              All Minorities whether based on religion or language shall have the right to establish and administer educational institutions of their choice

          • Grounds on Which Minority can be Identified

            1. Religion
            2. Language

          • Amongst these 2 who will be minority

            that is not defined in the constitution

          • What is the Basis of Identifying Minorities

            • TMA PAI Foundation v/s State of Karnataka, 2003

              wherever the educational institution has to be established ; the state should be the unit

              Whoever is less than 50% in numbers on grounds of language or religion → it will be considered as minority

          • Examples of a Minority Educational Institutions

            1. AMU
            2. St Stephens
            3. Jamia Milia Islamia

          • Privileges to Minority Educational Institutions

            1. They will be regulated but with minimal interference
            2. they can setup their own governing body
            3. reasonable fee structure, state can just set a boundary
            4. they will recruit their own teaching or non teaching staff
              1. method of appointment - upto institute
            5. Admission should be in fair manner - but how do u do that is your outlook

          • Why is the Minority Educational Institutions

            1. To Give Impetus to education in that community
            2. Free to Reserve 50% of Seats in the Institution
            3. Reservation Doesn't apply here

          • Can the Land be compulsory be acquired by state ?

            Normal Institution

            Yes it can be acquired by state compulsorily with proper remuneration

            Fair & Just Compensation < Market Value

            Minority Institution

            Can be Compulsorily Acquired

            But the Compensation here should be completely on Market Value and NOT a Just & Fair Compensation

      • Art 31 - Rt to Property && Art 19(1)(f) - Currently Removed from Part 3

        By 44th CAA, 1978

        1. Both Clauses were present in the original constitution
        2. 19(1)(f) - removed from Part 3 of the Constitution
          1. Article Says Guarantees to Every Citizen the Right to Acquire, Hold & Dispose Property
          2. Not an Absolute Right
          3. RR for Art 19(1)(f) are
            1. Public Welfare
            2. Interest of Scheduled Tribe
        3. Art 31 - Removed from Part 3 and Placed in 300A
          1. Article Says No Person shall be deprived of his property except by an authority of Law
          2. Law : Land Acquisition Act
          3. Meaning : The Action of the state has to be backed by a Law
          4. Earlier it was a FR now its a Legal Right
          5. Kameshwar Singh v/s State of Bihar, 1950
            1. He Said Land Acquisition Act is violative of Art 14 Art 19 Art 31
            2. SC Said land can be taken away by law
            3. He Said that it should be by Fair & Just Compensation - Hence Violative of Art 19 & Art 31
            4. Different Place Different Compensation - Violative of Art 14
            5. SC accepted this Premise
            6. Was a Blow to the Govt. Hence Series of Changes were brought to Indian Constitution
          6. as a Reaction to Kameshwar Singh v/s State of Bihar
            1. Art 31 A was inserted

    • Art 31 A - Art 31 D → Saving of Certain Laws

      • Art 31 A - Saves Certain Categories of Laws related to Agrarian Reforms

        1. Inserted by First CAA, 1951
        2. Saves Certain Categories of Laws related to Agrarian Reforms, (Specifically 5 Categories of Law) specifically for Socio Economic Reforms
        3. Even if Law of Land Acquisition violates Art 14 19 31 is violated, it cannot be challenged in court of law
        4. Art 31 A takes away the power of Judicial Review from Courts on the cases of Art 14 19 31

      • Art 31 B - Saves Laws Placed in the Ninth Schedule

        1. Inserted by First CAA, 1951
        2. Saves Laws Placed in the Ninth Schedule
        3. Provides for Complete or Blanket Immunity
        4. Provides Protection Against All the Fundamental Rights

        • Why Need 31 A when there is 31 B

          31 B requires a CAA && only when it is placed in the Ninth Schedule

          Currently there are 284 Laws in the Ninth Schedule

          Where as a CAA is not required in case of Art 31 A

      • Art 31 C - Save Any Law Trying to Implement Art 39(b) & 39(c) on grounds of violation of Art 14 19 31

        1. Inserted by 25th CAA, 1971
        2. Indira Gandhi Wanted to Specifically Implement Two Important Directives i.e Art 39(b) & Art 39 (c)
          1. Art 39(b) → Prevention of Concentration of Wealth in the hands of few individuals of society
          2. Art 39(c) → Redistribution of Wealth
        3. by 42nd CAA, 1976 she tried to enlarge scope of Art 31 C
          1. Protection of 39(b) 39(c) was extended to all the DPSP over Art 14 19 31
          2. Now the Art 31 C read - Saves Any Law Trying to Implement DPSP over Art 14 19 31
        4. Minerva Mills Case, 1980
          1. This was taking Judicial Review of Supreme Court. Hence, the Extended Scope of 31 C was made null and void
          2. SC declared - These 2 to be part of Basic Structure
            1. Judicial Review
            2. Harmonious Balance between DPSP & FR
          3. Sometimes 39 (b) 39 (c) can be given supremacy over Art 14 19 31
          4. Here DPSP - Collective Need - Art 39(b) && Art 39(c)
          5. Here FR - Individual Need - Art 14 19 31
        5. 44th CAA, 1978
          1. 19 (1)(f) was repealed
          2. Art 31 deleted & inserted in Art 300 A

    • Art 32 - Art 35 → Right to Constitutional Remedies - Heart & Soul of Constitution

      • Art 32 - The 5 Writs

        • Writ Summary

        • Background

          1. According to Ambedkar its the Heart & Soul of the Constitution
          2. Talks About Writ Jurisdiction
          3. Enforcement of Rights comes from this Right
          4. Rt to Seek Remedy in case of violation of FR is itself a FR

        • Process of getting Constitutional Remedies

          1. Individual will file a petition that SC has been violated
          2. Court will issue Writs - Directive by Court

        • Powers of Issuing Writs

          1. Power to Issue Writs is a Concurrent Powers(SC & HC Both) and Not Exclusive Power
          2. Affected Individuals can knock doors of
            1. SC under : Art 32
            2. HC under : Art 226

        • Difference in Jurisdiction of HC & SC

          1. Difference Between Knocking Doors of SC & HC

            Territorial Jurisdiction

            1. But SC has got wider Territorial Jurisdiction
            2. whereas HC can issue writs only if right has been violated within the state

            Nature of Cases

            1. SC can issue writs only in case of violation of FR
            2. where as HC can issue writs for both FR & Legal Rights

          2. Can any other court other than SC & HC issue writs

            1. Art 32(3) provides for the Parliament to Give power to Other Courts to Issue Writs as well
            2. Not done until now

        • When is a Writ Issued

          When the violated BRINGS TO THE NOTICE OF COURT, that his FR has been violated

        • Meaning of Locus Standi ?

          1. Whenever a case is brought in front of court, the court asks the person, what is your standing or what is your relation to this case
          2. Traditionally Judiciary has been following the rule that, the person who is affected should come to the court
          3. the affected person might not have resources to file the case, this would prevent justice

        • Public Interest Litigation - PIL

          1. hence SC innovated PIL - Public Interest Litigation
          2. Where the another person having no connection to the case, can apply to the court for justice
          3. Examples are
            1. Aruna Shaunbague Case
            2. Vishaka Case

        • Habeas Corpus - To Have the Body

          • Meaning
            1. Literally Means to Have a Body
            2. It means, to produce the body in front of the authorities which has issued the order
          • Does Principle of Locus Standi apply in this case ?
            1. No it does not apply in case of Writ of Habeas Corpus
            2. Meaning not necessarily the Affected Ind but any other person can also file for petition
          • Hussainara Khatoon & ORS v/s Home Secretary, State of Bihar, 1979 - PIL
            1. It was in case of prolonged Undertrials
            2. in Bihar No of Undertrials were increasing, even if convicted jail would be for 2 Years but they have spent 5 to 6 Yrs in Jail
            3. SC Said
              1. Rt to Speedy Justice is Rt under Art 21
              2. Initially 14 Undertrials were released
              3. Taking Precedence later 40,000 Undertrials were released

        • Mandamus - We Command

          1. Principal of Locus Standi Doesnt Apply here
          2. Its a command given by the Courts to the Public Authorities (PA) in case of Violation of Rights cuz PA failed to Perform the Duty
          3. This is only in case of PUBLIC Authority
          4. Failing of PA could be
            1. Omission - Missed
            2. Commission
          5. Examples of Public Authority not doing its work ; for the court to issue mandamus
            1. Police Station not Filling FIR
            2. Municipal Corporation Not taking care of Pollution Free Environment

        • Prohibition - To Forbid (HC→LC)

          1. Direction for Higher Court to Lower Court
          2. Preventive in Character ; Negative Injunction
          3. Principle of Locus Standi Applies in Case of Prohibition
          4. This is a Direction from Higher Court to Lower Court ; to tell the lower court that this is not the jurisdiction of the lower court in the first place
          5. Prevents Lower Court from Exceeding Jurisdiction
          6. Negative Injunction which directs Inactivity of Lower Court
          7. Applies to Only Judicial and Quasi Judicial Authority
          8. Examples of Quasi Judicial Bodies
            1. Parliament
            2. Finance Commmission
            3. Election Commission
            4. Arbitration Board
            5. Tribunals

          What if the Lower Court has already passed the boundary of Jurisdiction

        • Certiorari - To Certify

          1. Grounds on Which Higher Court can certify Lower Courts Judgement
            1. Error of Law
            2. Excess of Jurisdiction
            3. Violation of Natural Justice
          2. Certiorari is CURATIVE in NATURE
          3. Principle of Locus Standi Applies
          4. Can be Issued against
            1. Against Judicial
            2. Quasi Judicial
            3. Administrative Authorities - by Departmental Enquiry based on the Conduct Rules

        • Quo Warranto - By What Authority

          1. To Check of an Eligible Person is holding on to the Public Office (which is Significant & Substantive)
          2. Examples : PM, CM, CoM, CEC, CVC
          3. These Offices can only be held with Suitable Qualifications
          4. Whenever the Writ of Quo Warranto is filed, the person has to prove in court of law that he fulfils the eligibility
          1. by What Authority or Warrant
          2. Enquire into Legality of claim of a Person to a Public Office
          3. Against Public Authority holding office of substantive importance
          4. Principle of Locus Standi does not apply
          • Under Representation of People Act, 1951
            1. If they are convicted for a minimum of 2 Years or More. Then they will be immediately disqualified to be the member of state legislature
            2. Further, They cannot contest election for another 6 Years
          • Examples
            1. Jayalalita was convicted for 4 years for Prevention of Corruption Act
              1. Her Party won the Elections
              2. she didn't fight elections
              3. She was appointed the CM
              4. Quo Warranto Applied
              5. 6 Months time for Jaylalitha
              6. SC said within 6 months also she is not eligible to contest elections
            2. Azam Khan convicted for 3 years for Hate Speech

      • Art 33 - Power of the Parliament to Modify the Rights Conferred by this Part (Part 3)

        1. Art 33 empowers the parliament to restrict or abrogate the FR of the members of the
          1. armed forces - Army Navy Air Force
          2. para military forces CISF, BSF, NSG, ITBP
          3. police forces - CBI
          4. intelligence agencies - R&AW, IB
          5. analogus forces - NIA
        2. Examples of Law
          1. Army Act, 1950
          2. Navy Act, 1950
          3. Airforce Act, 1950
          4. Police Force (Restriction of Rights), 1965
        3. Rights Restricted are :
          1. Rt to Form Union
          2. Rt to Speech and Expression
        4. Rights Restricted due to
          1. National Security Concerns

      • Art 34 - Restriction on Rights conferred by this part while Martial Law is in Force in any area

        1. This Part is Part 3
        2. Sec 144 is NOT Martial Law
        3. After Independence ; There has never been implementation of martial laws
        4. Martial Law : Military Rule
          1. The Civil Administration System doesn't work
          2. The Justice, Administration & Whole Process of an area is given to an army officer
        5. When can Martial Law be Implemented - War, Invasion, Insurgency

      • Art 35 - Legislation to give affect to provisions of this part (3)

        • Who has Authority to make Laws for Part 3
        1. Article 35 Empowers the Parliament not the State Legislature
        2. To Make Laws to give the Affect
          1. Article 16(3)
          2. Article 32(3)
          3. Article 33
          4. Article 34
        3. Prescribe Punishment for those acts which are declared to be offences under this part
          1. Art 17 - Untouchability
          2. Art 23 - Begar
          3. Art 24 - Child Labour

    • Trinity - Goden Triangle - Art 14 19 21

      1. Before Menaka Gandhi Case, FR were considered different in water tight compartment
      2. After MG case only, FR were considered to be connected
      3. If Rt to Life & Liberty is Deprived - Art 21 If need to be, only by Due Process of Law
      4. Test of not only Art 21 but also test of Art 14 & Art 19 is also needed
      5. How is Art 14 19 21 related
        1. Restrictions should be - Reasonable Restriction Test of Reasonability
      6. Google Art 14 19 21

      • Liberal (7) - 44, 45, 48, 48A, 49, 50, 51

        • Art 44 - Uniform Civil Code (UCC) - Very Important ⭐️⭐️

          • How old is the case of UCC ?

            The Debate of Having a UCC is as old as the CA itself.

          • What is the Meaning of UCC ?

            Definiton

            A Set of Common Civil Code for all Indian Citizens in personal matters related to marriage, divorce, property, inheritance etc irrespective of religion which right now is not uniform

            1. Uniform - Set of Common Laws
            2. Civil Code
              1. Mundane Aspects
                1. Marriage
                2. Divorce
                3. Inheritance of Property
              2. For Civil / Personal Laws

          • Current Status of Laws ?

            1. 80% Hindu Buddhism Sikh Jain - Same Law
            2. 20% Muslims Christians Parsis & Others - Different Law

          • Why do we need UCC - In Favour ?

            1. It is the Moral Obligation of State
            2. UCC is example of Progressive Society
              1. Example : France & USA
            3. Uniform Laws help in reduction of Vote Bank Politics
            4. Deals with Remedy to Violation of Rights
              1. Example
                1. Grounds of Religion - Art 15
                2. Equality Before Law - Art 14
                3. Right to Life - Art 21
            5. Gender Injustice across religion
            6. Equality of Law Across Religion

          • Why do we NOT need UCC - Against the Motion ?

            1. Violative of Art 25 - Religious Rights
            2. If people don’t accept State Implements it by Force Unity & Integrity of the Nation will hampers because people will resent
            3. State Interference in Personal Matters

          • Examples of Laws Different Laws Currently ?

            1. Marriage
              1. Hindu Marriage Act, 1955
              2. Muslim Personal Law (Shariat)
              3. Incurable Leprosy
                1. Hindu & Muslim - Ground for Divorce
                2. Christian & Parsis - Not Ground for Divorce
              4. Adoption
                1. Adopted Child Right to Inherit Property - Hindu Yes
                2. Other Religion No
              5. Maintainence After Divorce
                1. Hindus - Compulsory
                2. Others - No
              6. Practise of Triple Talak & Polygamy
                1. Muslims - Allowed Until Now
                2. Hindus - Not Allowed

          • How does it violate FR ?

            1. Art 14 Art 19 Art 21

          • Who Suggested UCC in CA ?

          • Ex of Western Countries which have UCC ?

            France and USA

          • Case Laws wrt to UCC in SC & LC ?

            Largely SC was Neutral,

            • Shah Bano Case, 1985
              1. Case of Divorce b/w Muslim Husband & Muslim Wife at age 65
              2. Husband denied Maintainence
              3. Maintenance for only first 3 Months
              4. Under Muslim Personal Law, she cant get maintenance
              5. SC invoked 125 of CrPC - Criminal Law Its a Punishable Offence to not Provide Maintainence to Wife Even after Divorce she would continue to be Wife of husband if she has not married anyones else Husband will have to give Rs 500
              6. SC Recommended Implementation of UCC
            • Sarla Mudgal Case, 1995
              1. Sarla Mudgal - Hindu
              2. Husband - Hindu
              3. Marriage - Hindu Marriage Act
              4. Husband - Converted to Islam
              5. Husband - Married Muslim Women
              6. SC declared
                1. We don’t see your religious conversion not on belief but to take advantage of polygamy rule of Islam
              7. SC Suggested Govt to think about UCC
            • SC recommended matter to Law Commission

              1. Law Commission has very categorically said that UCC is

              2. Neither Feasible Nor Necessary

              3. Why LC said like this

                1. Tension Amongst Minorities
                2. Citizens don’t accept it

              4. LC suggested Incremental Approach but not a Sweeping Reform

              5. Example

                THE PROHIBITION OF CHILD MARRIAGE (AMENDMENT) BILL, 2021

                Like Making Age of Marriage 21

          2. Panchsheel - Non Interference in Internal Affairs of other Country
          3. No First Use Nuclear Policy

    • Does Constitution divide DPSP categories

      1. No it does not, we make these division for our understanding

    • Background

      1. Present in Part 4 of Constitution
      2. Consist of Art 36 to Art 51

    • What is a DPSP

      1. They are Certain Guidelines and they are very detailed guidelines

    • How is FD & DPSP related

      1. FD are Moral Obligation - On Citizens
      2. DPSP - Moral Obligation - On State

    • How does DPSP lead to Welfare State

      1. If State keeps implementing DPSP → will automatically lead to Welfare State

    • Why was DPSP Kept a Moral Obligation

      1. Initially india was a Nascent Democracy at the time of Independence

      2. Though Some Members of CA wanted it to be a Legal Obligation, Ambedkar Said it should only be a Moral Obligation because

        Resource Constraints

        1. DPSP is very aspirational, country like US cant even implement it fully now
        2. Legal Obligation will only increase the Litigation in Courts

        Power of Indian Democracy

        1. Even Moral Obligation can make Govt follow the DPSP, because India is a Democracy and they cant ingnore it

    • In what condition DPSP is Justiciable

      1. Unless Implemented It cant be made a Legal Obligation

      2. But if the Govt makes a Law and Confers Rights in the Citizen → Taking Inspiration from DPSP→ Then after the Law being made → If rights are violated, now it is justiciable

      3. Example

        MGNREGA - 100 Days of Employment If he is not given Employment, he can seek remedy

    • Is DPSP new to Constitution

      1. In Government of India Act 1935, there were elements of DPSP
      2. In GOI Act, 1935 it was called Instrument of Instruction

    • What is the Difference Between FR & DPSP ?

    • Examples of Questions Possible

      1. Justiciable or Moral Obligation
      2. Amendments of DPSP
      3. Difference from DPSP & FR
      1. Prelims Exam - Min 1 Max 2
      2. Mains - 1 Question - Majorly UCC

  • Part 4A (Art 51A) - Fundamental Duties

    • Background - Factual Information

      1. was not Part of Original Constitution
      2. Added by 42nd CAA 1976
      3. Added to Part 4 A
      4. Added Art 51 A
      5. Initially had 10 Duties
      6. 11th Duty added by 86th CAA, 2002

    • The Fundamental Duties Images

    • The Story Behind its coming

      1. Based on Recommendation of Swaran Singh Committee (Defence Minster of India then)
      2. He was Part of Kitchen Cabinet of Indira Gandhi
      3. 1975 - 1976 - Time of Third National Emergency
      4. People weren't happy ; Public Property was destroyed
      5. He Recommended Bringing in some Fundamental Duties
      6. This FD could be a Moral Obligation & NOT a Legal Obligation

    • Important Points to Note

      1. It is the Duty of the Citizens
      2. It is the Moral Obligation of Citizens
      3. There is NO moral obligation on Aliens

    • Can It be made a Legal Obligation

      1. Examples
        1. FD : To Respect National Emblems
        2. Law : Prevention of Insults to National Honours Act, 1971
      2. It become a Legal Obligation, Only if the Law is Made on it

    • Scope of Mains Questions

      FD is the Codification of task integral to Indian Way of Life

      with FD it was codified but the morals were already present in the lives of Indians

    • Detailed FD

      1. To Abide by the Constitution and Respect the Ideals of Constitution & to the National Flag and National Anthem
        1. Ideals are Justice Liberty Equality
        2. Institutions - Judiciary Parliament
      2. To Cherish and to follow the Noble Ideas which inspired Struggle for Freedoms
        1. Ideals - Self Respect, Non Violence, Satyagraha
      3. To Uphold Protect and Sovereignty & integrity of India
        1. Revolt against foreign rules
        2. Uprisings & Revolt of 1857
        3. after independence, Chinese war, civilians participated
      4. To Defend Country
        1. Same as above
      5. To Promote Harmony and the spirit of common brotherhood amongst all the people
        1. Vasudeva Kutumbhakam
      6. Renounce Practise Derogatory to Dignity of Women
        1. Social Reformer - Abolish Sati Widow Re Marriages
      7. Value & Preserve Rich Heritage & Composite Culture
        1. Indian Festivals
        2. Foreign Soft Power
      8. Scientific Temper
        1. Bose Aryabhatta
      9. Spirit of Reform
        1. RRMR
      10. Safeguard Public Property & Abjure Violence
        1. Satyagrah
        2. Non Violence in Religions
      11. Individual and Collective Activity
        1. DPSP
        2. Cooperative Movements
      12. Child Education
        1. Gurukul Culture
