        Only a Pvt Mem can be Member | | Election Method | Elected through System of Proportional Representation | Elected through System of Proportional Representation | Elected through System of Proportional Representation | | Functions | They Get the CAG Report from the President of India, submitted to him by CAG (Direct Finance of Govt)

        They will make noise, they may share the CAG report with public as well | They Get the CAG Report from the President of India, submitted to him by CAG (Indirect Finance of Govt)

        They will make noise, they may share the CAG report with public as well | They Recommend on the estimates prepared by Department of Economic Affairs for the Budget of that Year | | Anecdote / Remarks | Murli Manohar Joshi called PM Singh in 2014 as Chairperson of this Committee | | |

        Recommendations

        1. Cut the Allowances, and Use the Money in Hiring the Experts
        2. They Completely Function in Non Transparent Manner
        3. NCRWC Recommended a Panel of Full Time Experts and Researchers to help the Parliament

        Drawback

        1. Committee is Recommendatory & Non Binding
        2. Their Nature of Work is Post Martem except for Estimates Committee
        3. Allowances Given to them to hire the Help of experts are used for Personal Use and Seldom Experts are Hired unlike US

      • DRSC (24)

        • Details

          1. DRSC - Departmentally Related Standing Committee

          2. It was started as a practise in 1993

          3. 24 Total Committees → Each Cmt has 31 Members - 21 from LS & 10 from RS

            16 in LS - Headed by LS Member - Chairperson by LS Speaker

            8 in RS - Headed by RS Member - Chairperson by RS Chairman

          4. All Members are Nominated by Speaker and Chairperson Respectively

        • Functions
          1. Scrutinising the Bills
          2. Scrutinising the Demand for Grants
          3. Implementation of Various Policies & Schemes by Govt
            1. Example: DRSC on Health & Family Welfare will look into the Functioning of NHM - National Health Mission
            2. Example: DRSC on Urban Affairs will look into the Functioning of Smart City Mission
        • Issues

          1. When the Bills are Introduced it is not Mandatory to Refer the Bills to DRSC

            in 16th LS only 25% of Total Bills Introduced were referred to a DRSC

          2. The Recommendations of DRSC is only binding in Nature

          3. Members of DRSC are Not Experts in Legal or Field of DRSC. They do not have expert help as well

            NCRWC called it “Crippled” in its functioning

          4. No Transparency in its working

        • Advantages
          1. As there are members from different parties and houses, it acts as a better consensus building forums before the bill is introduced
          2. Members can freely raise their concern as a part of Ruling Party, even a member of ruling party can go against the bill in the PC without the fear of Anti Defection Law

      • Administrative Committees

        1. Helps Smooth & Better Administration of House

        2. Examples of Administrative Committees are :

          1. Rules Committee of LS and RS

            To Frame the Rules in the House

            Headed by Speaker in LS and Chairman in RS

          2. Ethics Committee

            Keeps a Note of Various Conflict of Interests a MP may have

            It is largely dysfunctional in both the houses

          3. Business Advisory Committee

            Advises the PO so as to how to carry out various businesses in the house

            Headed by Speaker in LS and Chairman in RS

          4. General Purpose Committee

            After All the Functions which are left out will be taken up by the General Purpose Committee

            Headed by the Presiding Officers Themselves

        3. Functions

          Helps Presiding Officer to maintain the discipline and decorum of the house

        4. It is there in both the house of parliament i.e LS & RS

      Temporary Committees

      • Ad Hoc Committees
        1. They are Temporary in Nature
        2. It is set up for a specific reason
        3. Example
          1. Joint Parliamentary Committee
            1. It will always have members from both the house
            2. can be formed for
              1. Look into Corruption Charges
              2. Scrutinise the Bill
          2. Select Committee
            1. In case of Select Committee it is only a member from a particular house
            2. Members are chosen for a expert reason
          3. Once Work is Done, They are Removed

    • Function of the Parliament

      1. Law Making
      2. Budget Formulation
      3. Budget Enactment
      4. Accountability of Govt/Executive
      5. Quasi Judicial Rule

    • Is the Quality of Working in Parliament Declining ?

      1. At Commencement of Constitutions, P used to sit for 122 Days, Now it sits for 70 Days Only
      2. Quality of Debates has to be increased, majority in the house doesn’t speak against it due to ADL
      3. Bills aren’t having good expert preview as only 25% of bills are being sent to DRSC
      4. No of Ordinances in parliament is increasing which signifies decline of functioning of parliament
      5. There has been New in most of the Budget, Most of the Budget are Incremental Budget
      1. Criminalisation of Politics, It is grave that Law Breakers are Becoming Law Makers
      2. No Gender Parity in the Parliament, Representation of Women is Less than 10%

    • Reforms in Functioning of Parliament

      1. NCRWC Suggested Minimum of 120 Days of Siting
      2. Check on Ordinance Making Power
      3. Reservation for Women in Lok Sabha
      4. Mandatory Referral to DRSC except for under Exceptional Charges
      5. Members with Criminal Background should be subjected to Fast Track Court
      6. Changes to Anto Defection Law

  • Judiciary - Union & State Judiciary

    • Introduction to Indian Judiciary

      • What do you mean by Independent and Integrated Judiciary?

        1. Independent from Executive and Legislature → Balances the Feature of Federalism
        2. Integrated with HC and DC → Where Order of Upper Court is Binding upon Lower Court
        3. Integrated Judiciary → All Courts can Look into All Laws, There is no Separate Hierarchy of Court for different Laws

      • Establishment & Constitution of Supreme Court

        1. Supreme Court of India - Highest Court of India
        2. Judges of Supreme Court shall be appointed by President
        3. However, at the commencement of constitution there shall be 7+1 Judges
        4. But Present Strength of Judges of Supreme Court is 33 Judges and 1 CJI of India

      • What does it take to Increase the Number of Judges?

        Supreme Court of No of Judges Act, 1956 → The Power to make this law has been given to the parliament

        It can be done by an ordinary law and the number of judges can be increased as per the demands

    • Supreme Court of India

      • What does the constitution say about the appointment of Judges of the Supreme Court?

        1. President may in consultation with
        2. Judges of Supreme Court
        3. Judges of High Court
        4. Chief Justice of India

        shall appoint Judges of High Court

      • What is Collegium System

        No other Democratic Country uses this System but India

        Its a System of Appointment of Judges to higher judiciary where the judges appoint the judges

        Here, President is a mere appointing Authority

        Collegium - Group of Judges

      • What is the Origin of Collegium System

        Its an Judicial Innovation by Supreme Court like that of Basic Structure Doctrine

      • What are the Case Laws Involved in Judicial Innovation of “Collegium System”

        • First Judges Case, 1981
          1. Meaning of Consultation was asked
          2. SC Said that Consultation does not mean Concurrence
          3. Hence, Primacy was given to the Executives
        • Second Judges Case, 1993
          1. Question
            1. This Issue was Raised that if the Ultimately the Primacy is of Executives, then it will affect the Integrity of Judges and Hence Supreme Court
            2. There Should be No Case of Committed Judiciary → Committed Meaning Committed to Govt
          2. SC Said,
            1. that Henceforth → Collegium System Evolved → CJI & 2 Senior Most Judges
            2. Here, Consultation would amount to Concurrence
          3. Question
            1. Whose Consultation would amount to Concurrence
          4. SC Said
            1. Consultation of Chief Justice of India would amount to Concurrence
        • Third Judges Case, 1998 (Presidential Reference u/a 143)
          1. Consultation with Plurality of Judges → SC Expanded the Collegium
          2. Collegium would now amount to → CJI + 4 Senior Most Judges
          3. Along with CJI At Least 3 Senior Most Judges should be in Concurrence

      • Other Countries System

        1. India - Collegium
        2. US - Judicial Commission

      • by 99th CAA, 2015 → Govt brought in NJAC

        1. NJAC → It was declared null and void by the SC on Grounds of violation of Basic Structure
        2. Here Independence of Judiciary was considered to be a part of Basic Structure

      • Comparison Between NJAC and Collegium System

      • Critical Analysis of Collegium System and NJAC

        1. Promotes Judicial Nepotism: At Least 50% of Judges are Kith and Kin of Past Judges
        2. Goes against concept of Separation of Power → It does away with the system of checks and balances
        3. The Process is Non Transparent and Non Questionable
        4. There is Lack of Accountability on the Part of Collegium
        5. But, the Compromisation of Judiciary is also a grave concern
      • Home Work: How to Improve Collegium System
      • Home Work: Example from where the Power of Judicial Review Flows?
        1. Art 13

      • Appointment of Supreme Court Judges

        1. President is appointed by a Warrant under his Hand and Seal
        2. Till they Attain 65 Years of Age
        3. Resignation by given to the President
        4. Can be Removed by Order of President →
          1. Only by Motion Passed in Parliament
          2. Addressed in Both the House
          3. In the Same Session
          4. By means of Special Majority
          5. On Grounds of Misbehaviour and incapacity
        5. Once Passed, the President has to Sign the Order
        6. Here, Step by Step Procedure is NOT Given in the Constitution

      • Procedure from Removal of a Judge of Supreme Court*

        Constitution has empowered the Parliament to give a detailed Process for impeachment of President → Judges Enquiry Act, 1968

        in 1988 → There was a Motion for CJ of Punjab of High Court

        Procedure for Removal of a Judge

        1. Motion has to be Introduced in Either Houses
        2. Signed by 100/50 Member of LS and RS
        3. Subject to Pre Approval of Speaker & Chairman in LS & RS
        4. Here, Even after the Support of 50 Members, the PO can deny the approval of the Introduction of the Bill
        5. PO shall appoint a Investigating Committee of Three Members
          1. Two Judges of SC
          2. One Retired Judge - Jurists
          3. Hence this is a Quasi Judicial Function of Parliament
        6. Incapacity
          1. Not so Severe Physical Disability
          2. Mental Incapacity like - Schizophrenia
        7. CMT will give the Report to Parliament
        8. Passed with Special Majority in the First House
        9. Passed to Next House and Motion will be put to vote in the Same Session of the Parliament
        10. If Passed with Special Majority in the Second House, Sent for Presidents Approval
        11. President will Sign and Judge Stands Impeached

      • Qualification to be a Supreme Court Judge

        1. Citizen of India (In Addition to this)

          Not Necessarily by Birth or Naturalised Citizen

        2. Either: Five Year Judge of High Court or Two or More Such Courts

        3. Either: Advocate of High Court or Two or More Such Courts in Succession for 10 Years

        4. Either: Someone who is a Distinguished Jurists in the Opinion of President (Opinion of Collegium)

        5. There is NO Minimum Age Criteria for the Appointment of SC Judges

        6. Note

          An Oath has to administered by President under his hand of warrant and seal

      • Difference Between Jurisdiction and Power
        1. Jurisdiction - Type of Cases they can entertain
        2. Power - Not Necessarily Jurisdiction but some powers that they can do
      • Jurisdiction & Powers of the Supreme Court
          1. Original Jurisdiction

          • Introduction

            1. Given by Power Article 131
            2. Cases that can be taken directly by the ONLY by the Supreme Court
            3. No Other Court in India can take these cases or look into it
            4. Example:
              1. Any Dispute which is Federal in Character → Federal Disputes
              2. Federal in Nature
                1. Union of India v/s State or States
                2. Union of India and State v/s State/States
                3. State v/s States ( Two or More States )
            5. Here Individuals are NOT Party to it, Only States or Centre

          • Special Case of UT

            Special Case: Dispute Between Union Government and UT - Is it a Federal Dispute ?

            Under Art 239 UT is controlled by Centre → Hence not being in a Federal Dispute they should go to High Court and then by Means of Appellate Dispute

            Special Case: Dispute Between UT and any other States is a → Federal Dispute

          • Who is considered to be in a Federal Dispute

            For Someone to be in a Federal Relationship - There Should be a Different Constitutional Head

            Hence, Including UT Of Delhi, JK & Puducherry is not in Federal Relationship

          1. Appellate Jurisdiction
          2. In Original You go to SC directly
          3. In Appellate by means of Hierarchy, they can make an appeal to the Supreme Court
          4. Nature of Case in which there can be Appellate Jurisdiction
            1. Appeal on Civil Law
            2. Criminal Matters in the Supreme Court
          5. Here Individuals and State both can be a Party to it

          1. Appeal by Special Leave

          2. Example of Inter State River Water Dispute is a Federal Dispute ?

            Yes

          3. Will it go to SC directly ?

            No

          4. What is the Exception then ?

            1. Exception is Art 262 which says, Whenever there is Case of River Water Disputes, the matter shall not go to Supreme Court but Shall in Lie in front of an Authority as made by the Law of the Parliament
            2. Inter State River Water Disputes Act, → Taken Up by an Ad Hoc Tribunal
            3. Example: Cauvery Water Dispute took 25 Years to give the Order, TN Govt Said they will not accept the Orders of Tribunal. Meanwhile they accepted the Interim Orders of Proportion of Water Sharing, but they didn't accept the Final Order of Tribunal
            4. According to constitution the order of Tribunal is Binding. But Now TN Govt appealed to SC by means of Special Leave Jurisdiction

          5. If any party in India, is not able to go to the Supreme Court by means of any other Jurisdiction, then they can appeal the Supreme Court by Special Leave Jurisdiction

          6. Exemption to Special Leave Jurisdiction : Orders of Armed Forces Tribunal

          7. It's a Extraordinary Power and Hence used by the SC very Sparsingly

          1. Writ Jurisdiction
          2. Power to Issue Writs for Fundamental Rights only
          3. Case Only
            1. Write Petition - W
            2. Original Petition - O
            3. Appellate - A
            4. Special Leave Petition - SLP
          4. There are 5 Types of Writs that can be Issued by the Supreme Court
          5. Can SC Issue Writs for Legal Rights
          6. Difference
            1. SC - Can take Matters only for Violation of FR only but Geographical Area is More
            2. HC - Can take Matters only for Violation of FR & Legal Rights but Geographical Area is Less
          7. Art 139 of the Constitution Parliament can empower the Supreme Court to issue writs for Legal Rights as well
          1. Review Powers
          2. SC Can review their own order and change it
          3. Hence You Cannot Punish the Judge if SC Changes its decision
          4. In Such Cases a, Review Petition is Filed in the Supreme Court
          5. Examples
            1. Kesavananda Bharati Case
          1. Curative Petition
          2. It is NOT Mentioned in Supreme Court
          3. SC under Art 137 of the Constitution did a Judicial Innovation and made a Curative Petition
          4. Once All Exhaustion of Options is Done and even Review Petitions is exhausted, then curative petition exists
          5. Then Even if Curative Petition is Exhausted then there is NO Option from the Court
          6. Only Option is Mercy Petition to the President
          1. Law Declared by the Supreme Court binding on all courts
          2. It is an example of implementation of Hierarchy of Courts
          3. Judgment from one HC to another HC is not binding but Persuasive
          4. Examples

            1. Judgment Already Passed by the Supreme Court has to be taken care by HC. Hence in Sedition Case Conviction Rate is only 3% as all sedition cases go to Supreme Court Firsts

            2. Example

              Kedarnath Case, 1962 → Clear and Immediate Incitement of Violence (Sedition)

          1. Rule Making Power - Article 145
          2. Rule Making Powers is given to SC by means of Art 145
          3. Supreme Court can make Rules with the Approval of Presidents, regulating general practice and procedure in the Court
          4. Approval of Presidents → It is Generally Given
          5. Rules Like
            1. Procedure of Filling of Cases
            2. Conduct if Judges
            3. Min No of Judges Needed for a Constitutional Bench

          1. Advisory Jurisdiction

          2. Art 143

            1. It's a Little Different from other Jurisdictions

          3. It can be for

            1. Question of Law
            2. Question of Fact

          4. Whenever President Seeks Advice of Supreme Court

            It is not binding upon the Supreme Court to take the Case

            It is not binding upon the Govt to accept the Decision of Supreme Court

          5. Example : Ayodhya Mandir Verdict President Referred it to SC in 1990, SC Rejected the Case for Advisory

            Now, another new case was filed in Supreme Court which couldn't be rejected by the Supreme Court

          1. Contempt Powers
          2. It is nowhere mentioned or defined in the Constitution
          3. Contempt - Showing any type of Disrespect to the Authority of the Court
          4. Art 129 - Supreme Court has the Power to Punish for the Contempt of the Supreme Court and other Courts
          5. Contempt can be defined based on the Law passed by the Parliament → Contempt of Courts Act, 1971

          • Summary - It is NOT Defined in the Constitution, It comes from the Contempt of Court Act

          • There are 2 Types of Contempt

            1. Civil Contempt

              Willful Disobedience

              Not Appearing in Front of the Court or Non-Compliance of Civil Judgement

              Generally, It's a Fine

            2. Criminal Contempt

              Disrespect to Judge or Institution of Judiciary

              Someone who prevents the functioning of the Judiciary

              Generally, It’s a Punishment in Jail

          1. Example: Adv Prashant Bhushan was filed for Criminal Contempt, but SC took a Lenient Way and Said
            1. Accept the Contempt
            2. Apologise
            3. Pay a Fine of 1 Rs
            4. or Go to Jail for 3 Months
          1. Extraordinary Powers of SC under Art 142

          Art 142: The Supreme Court of India can Exercise its powers by Laws made by the Parliament of India

          Example: Periyar Volan Case - Person involved in assassination of Former PM Rajiv Gandhi

          Mercy Petition → President → President Sat on it

          SC used Extraordinary Power → Commuted Hanging to Life Imprisonment

          Now he filed the Mercy Petition for Reducing Life Imprisonment to the Governor of TN

          TN CoM A&A that His Life Sentence Should be Reduced → Gov Sat on it

          SC used Art 142 → Until Now he had spent 30 Years in Jail

          Now, SC Released him from Jail

    • High Courts of India

      • Introduction

        1. Constitution Says that there shall be a High Court for Each State → Meaning Some HC can have Concurrent Jurisdiction

        2. Each High Court Shall Consist of a Chief Justice plus some Judges (as decided by the President)

          Note:

          In SC Number of Judges is Decided by an act of Parliament

          In HC Number of Judges is decided by the President

      • Process for Appointment of Judges of HC

        1. Appointment and Condition of Service of Judges of High Court (Art 217)
        2. Appointed by President by warrant under his Hand and Seal
        3. They can retain the Office until they ATTAIN the Age of 62 Years (In SC its 65 Years of Age)
        4. For the Appointment of Judges, President shall Consult
          1. CJI
          2. Governor of State
          3. CJ of the HC of the Concerned State where the appointment to other judges of the High Court is done
        5. Here in Third Judges Case, SC Held that for the HC Judges also Collegium Shall Consists of
          1. CJI + 2 Senior Most Judges of SC
          2. CJ of Concerned High Court
          3. Other Judges of High Court and Such Judges of SC Conversant with the Affairs of High Court Concerned

      • The Movement of Files in Appointment of HC Judge is

        1. Process of Apt started by CJ of HC
        2. Files go to CM
        3. from CM to Gov
        4. From Gov to Ministry of Law
        5. From Here to CJI
        6. From CJI to Ministry of Law
        7. From Ministry of Law to PM
        8. From PM to President

      • Procedure for Removal of HC

        Same Process as that of SC Judges

      • Qualification for being the Judge of a HC

        1. Citizen of India &&

        2. 10 Years of Judicial Office in the Territory of India

          Judicial Office: District Judge etc

        3. OR Advocate of HC for at least 10 Years

        4. Oath Administered by Governor

      • Transfer of Judges from one HC to Another

        1. President of India can Transfer the Judges from one HC to another HC
        2. But Right Now it is done by Collegium. President acts on the aid and advise of Collegium
        3. Collegium → CJI + 4 Senior Most Judges (Written Recommendation Mandatory)
          1. Chief Justice of Both High Court (from where being transferred to where being transferred)
          2. Such other Judges of Supreme Court who are conversant with the affairs of the High Courts Concerned
          3. Recommendation is Mandatory but the Ultimate Authority is that of Collegium
        4. Judges of the HC have to show compliance no matter what, only way they can defy is by means of Resigning it

      • Jurisdiction and Powers of HC

        • Introduction

          1. All HC in our country shall continue to exercise such powers as before the commencement of constitution subject to law made by parliament and state legislature
          2. Jurisdiction as existed at the commencement of the constitution subject to law made by parliament and state legislature

        • Original Jurisdiction

          Civil Matters: based on laws passed by the state legislature

          Criminal Matters: there is no original jurisdiction in case of criminal matters in case of HC

          Criminal Matters detailed investigation has to be done which is done by Lower Courts

        • Appellate Jurisdiction

          Applies to all the Civil and Criminal Matters

        • HC Power of Superintendence

          General Guidelines - Regulating Practise and Proceeding in the courts, prescribed forms in which accounts have to be kept

          Note : Armed Forces Tribunal Excluded from the Jurisdiction of High Court

        • Writ Powers of HC

          1. Art 226 of Constitution
          2. They Can Issue Writs for Legal Rights and FR, throughout the Territory of the Concerned State

    • Subordinate Courts

      1. All Courts Below and Including District Courts is Subordinate Courts

      2. Appointment Made by Governor in consultation with HC. No Procedure Defined

      3. Recruitment of Person Other than District Judges

        By Means of PSC of That State conducts State Judicial Service Examination. Appointment made by Governor

        Rules Made by the Governor in consultation with state public service commission and high court concerned

    • Homework: All India Judicial Services Pros and Cons

      One of the Reason that there is a Lot of Pendency in Courts is that most cases is appealed in the High Courts, that is due to poor quality of judgements in the Lower Court. Most Important courts of India are Session and District Courts

      Reform Suggested Are: All India Service under Art 312 → All India Judicial Services

      Pre Condition: Rajya Sabha has to give a consent by passing Resolution by means of Special Majority

      Currently Judges are selected from the same state. Hence State has a very limited set of Talent Pool

    • PIL - Public Interest Litigation

      • Concept of Locus Standi

        Concept of Locus Standi: How are you connected to the case, especially in case of Writ Jurisdiction

        People who have any problem can go and knock the doors of Judiciary, despite they are not connected to the case

        Any Person can bring it to the notice of court by means of bonafide intention and not some vested interests

      • What is PIL ?

        OIK is defined as Litigation in interest of general public without following the principles of Locus Standi

      • Who can file a PIL ?

        PIl is a proceeding in which an individual or group seeks relief in the interests of general public and not for his or her own relief. PIL Has modified and relaxed the Traditional Doctrine of Locus Standi.

        Examples

        1. ADR
        2. Common Cause

      • Since When has it gained Prominence in India ?

        From 1970’s

      • Defining Case: Husainara Khatoon v/s State of Bihar, 1970

        1. Considered to be known prominent in PIL
        2. Relates to PIL for Plight of Undertrials
        3. Undertrials - Poor People, No Bail, No Judicial Knowledge
        4. SC Said:
          1. Speedy Justice as FR under Constitution
          2. 14 to 17 People were released
          3. that year 40,000 People were released

      • Defining Case: SP Gupta v/s UOI, 1981

        1. PIL can be filed for
          1. Writ Jurisdiction - Largely used when FR is violated
        2. Who can file it
          1. Individual or Social Action Group

      • Are Guidelines by SC Law ?

        Guidelines by SC is Law until and unless law made by the parliament

      • Advantage of PIL

        1. Protection of Rights of Individuals

          Example: Husainara Khatoon v/s State of Bihar, 1979

          Example: Vishakha Guidelines preventing Sexual Harassment at Workplace, this was law until 2013. In 2013, they made law

        2. Policy Decisions to Address Major Lacuna in Governance

          Example: CNG in Delhi

          Example: Nota in Elections → ADR filed PIL → Has no Significance → But allows Expression of Discontent

          Example: Good Samaritan → In case of Helping Police

      • Disadvantages of PIL

        1. Private Interest Litigation / Publicity Interest Litigation / Political Interest Litigation

          Example: Kalyaneshwari v/s Union of India Case → Industries Polluting Environment, Petitioner was part of Competitive Industry

        2. Violation of Doctrine of Separation of Powers

          1. Judge Made Laws
          2. Judge Made Policies
          3. Judicial Innovations

        3. Differentiating between Judicial Activism or Judicial Overreach is very difficult

          Judicial Activism (Pro Active Role Played by Judiciary) Examples → Positive Things

          1. Procedure Estab by Law is Due Process of Law
          2. Vishakha Case

        4. Pro Activeness of Judges venturing into Legislature or Executive → Judicial Adventurism → Negative Things

          Example: Based on PIL, All Alcohols within 500 M Range of Highways has to Shut Down things

          Example: Censoring of some parts of Jolly LLB despite certification from Central Film Board

      • Solutions

        1. While Entertaining PIL, a Court should Follow the Established Guidelines
          1. Bonafide Intention should be confirmed
          2. No Judicial Adventurism / Overreach → Judicial Restraint by Judiciary itself

    • Tribunals

      • Origin of Tribunals

        1. It was Inserted into the constitution by 42nd CAA
        2. It’s a Quasi Judicial Body

      • Difference Between Quasi Judicial and Judicial Bodies

        Called as Quasi Because

        1. They have Specialised Jurisdiction whereas Judicial Bodies have General Jurisdiction
        2. Service Conditions & Independence
          1. Judges are appointed by Govt and Executives Hence Independence is not as strong as Judges
        3. There is a Lot of Conflict of Interest for the Judges in Tribunals
        4. Jurisdiction of Quasi Judicial Bodies is very Restricted
        5. Procedure they Follow → Judicial Bodies follow a Rigid Process but Tribunals follow easy procedures that they only have to follow Law of Natural Justice
        6. They have Speedy Justice
        7. For Example: Taxation Matters, Environment Matters, Admin Matters

        Called as Judicial Because

        1. They impart Justice

      • Origin

        1. First Recommended by First Law Commission (14th Report)

          Pros

          1. Speedy Justice
          2. Cost of Justice Reduced
          3. Easy Access to Courts
          4. Judicial Pendency will be reduced

          Cons

          1. Independence and Impartiality of Govt

        2. Justice Shah Committee, 1972 ( HC Arrears Committee)

          Recommended Creation of Tribunals

        3. Sixth Law Commission

          Not in Favour of Creation of Tribunals

          May Violate Jurisdiction of HC & SC

          But if after the Tribunal, One can go to HC & SC. Then there should be no provision for appealing after Tribunals

          But then it can hamper the Jurisdiction of SC & HC violating Article 226, Art 32 & Art 236

          If you have to go to SC & HC → Then what is the point of making tribunals

        4. Based on Recommendation of Swaran Singh Committee under PM ship of IG

        5. It is Not a Part of Original Constitution, it was brought in by 42nd CAA

          1. Article 323 A → Administrative Tribunals
            1. Parliament is the only authority to make law
            2. Laws made by Parliament → Administrative of Tribunals Act, 1985
              1. Centre - CAT → Centre Administrative Parliament
              2. State - SAT → State Administrative Parliament
          2. Article 323 B → Other Tribunals
            1. Both State Leg & Parliament can make Laws on
            2. Taxation Matters, Env Tribunals etc

      • SP Sampath Kumar v/s UOI, 1986

        1. Administrative Tribunals Act, 1985 was challenged in Supreme Court
        2. Tribunals would be final and binding, hence ATA was unconstitutional violative ofA
          1. Art 226
          2. Art 32
          3. Art 136
        3. When the Matter was Sub Judice, Govt made changes and allowed appeal to HC & SC
        4. Here Art 32 and Art 136 was protected & Art 226 was NOT Protected
        5. Govt Debated : Effective Alternative Institutional Mechanism Theory saying that people can go to SC but not HC
        6. Here SC somehow accepted it

      • L Chandra Kumar v/s UOI, 1997

        1. It argued that Judicial Review Flows from Art 226
        2. They Said if Art 226 is a part of Basic Structure Doctrine
        3. Here SC Said, You Can Make Tribunals but can’t substitute the role of HC
        4. Here SC Held, that you can make the Tribunals, but HC appeal can be made
        5. SC Said,
          1. Position of Judges in Tribunals is independent from Partiality
        6. SC Suggested
          1. Judges in Tribunals shall be appointed by a Committee headed by CJ of HC
        7. HomeWork: Rationalisation of Tribunals Act, 2021

  • Centre State Relations: Addition of Current Affairs is a Must

    • Framework

      Constitution Divides the Relationship between Centre and State in the Following Way

    • Example of Companies Having Federal Political System

      1. USA Canada Australia & India
      2. Indian Federalism is closely related to constitution of canada
      3. Each Countries Federal Structure is Different

    • Division of Powers (DOP) Between the Central and State Government

      • Legislative DOP - Between Union Legislature & State Legislature

        • Questions to be Asked in Centre State Relations of
          1. What are the items on which Parliament can make Laws ?
          2. What are the Items on which State can make Laws ?
        • It is related to which article and Schedule
          1. Art 246 Provides for Schedule 7
          2. Schedule 7 Provides for 3 Lists, Namely

        • What are the Lists of Schedule 7 Here ?

          1. Union List - Union Makes Laws (97-100)

            1. Laws Needing Uniformity have been Given to Union

            2. Laws Made by the Parliament will apply throughout the country until and unless exceptions have been defined

            3. Law made by Parliament can have Extra Territorial Jurisdiction meaning outside India

              Example: Defence, Foreign Affairs, Communication, Banking & Insurance

              Example: NIA Act after Mumbai Attacks of 26/11/2008

          2. State List - NORMALLY State Makes Laws (66 - 61)

            1. To Accomodate Regional Diversity
            2. Throughout Country of Concerned State
            3. Example: Health, Sanitation, Agriculture, Fishery

          3. Concurrent List - Both Parliament and State Makes Laws (52 -47)

            1. As of Regional Diversity Later Uniformity can be applied for
            2. Example: Forest, Wildlife, Population Control and Family PLanning, Education, Social and Economic Planning, Marriage & Divorce

        • Framework of Lists

        • What is the Case when the Law Made by State can prevail ? What is the Exemption ?

          If there is a conflict in Law made by States and centres, in all times law made by centre will prevail with only one exemption

          The Exemption being, For Example for Education State and Centre has made Laws, Assent is given by Governor Normally

          Now, If Gov Reserves the Bill, The Bill is reserved for President's Assent & if the President Assent is Given on the Bill of State. Then here the Law of State will Prevail

          For Example: NEET as Mandatory Exam for entry into Medical Courses

          State Govt of TN: Passed a Bill by all Legislators 100% Bill was accepted to exempt students of TN from NEET Examination

          Constitutionally Education is a Item in the Concurrent List

          This bill passed by TN Govt is only useful when the Governor reserves it for President and President has to give the assent

        • Is India a Truly Federal Country ?

          1. India is a Federal Supremacy not a Truly Federal One
          2. It’s a Federal Structure with Unitary Bias

        • What are the Instances where Union is Preferred over State

          1. More Items in Union List

          2. Shift in the Nature of Amendment that has happened to Schedule 7, has been shifting items from State List to Concurrent Lists

          3. In Case of Any Conflict between Centre and State

          4. Art 3, Only INherent Power of the Parliament to reorganise States

          5. Only Parliament can make Constitutional Amendments

          6. Through Governors Power, Centre can control law making powers of State

          7. In Case of Creation of Leg Ass, Even Ratification of that State isn’t binding upon centre

          8. Residuary Powers of Law Making is with Central Government

            ( In a True Federal Country, like US, States have Residuary Powers)

        • What is a Residuary Power ?

          1. Anything that does not fall under three lists
          2. Parliament has the Power
          3. GOA Act, 1945 - Residuary Powers to Viceroy
          4. USA - Residuary Powers to State

        • Doctrine of Pith & Substance: In Case of Conflict between State Law & Central Law, the Central Law will Prevail

          The Doctrine: where a question arises whether a particular law relates to a particular subject mentioned in one list or another, the court investigate the substances of the law.

          For Example: if the substance falls within the union list, then the incidental encroachment by the law on the state list does not make the law invalid.

          Innovated by SC through: State of Rajasthan v/s G Chawla, 1959

        • Example of Application of Doctrine of Pith & Substance:

          1. Agricultural Law: By Art 131 for Federal Dispute in Supreme Court, Many States applied in Law
          2. NIA Act, 2008: was challenged in court by some states, by empowering NIA, parliament is trying to encroachment upon the power of state of Law and Order of State List

        • What Does Supreme Court Do

          1. If Union has unintentionally encroached upon the powers of state.
          2. If, the reason, objective, subject matter is centred around administration of item in the Union List (only then) that Law cannot be considered as a unconstitutional Law
          3. Example: By NIA Act 2008

            1. State Said : Centre is Encroaching Upon the Powers of Police of Law and Order which is in State List

            2. Centre Said : It is not for encroachment in powers of state but for a centralised intelligence agency

              Centre said it has made law under National Security Item

            3. Here SC: Said that Centres Law is Correct

          4. Example: Agricultural Law
            1. There is Nothing Regarding Agriculture in the Union List
            2. In Concurrent List Also, there is no direct agriculture but only Agricultural Produce and Distribution.
            3. Hence, There is a INTENTIONAL Encroachment upon powers of state is seen
            4. Though the Law has been withdrawn by the Centre, SC would by this Doctrine, would have struck down this Law

        Power of Parliament to Legislate on State List

        • Art 249: National Interest

          1. Special Resolution by Rajya Sabha by a Majority of 2/3rd Members of Present & Voting

          2. When they have to suddenly make laws on something which is in state list

          3. For Example: Health is in State List, During Covid Time Govt wanted to bring in Law for Health

            Parliament can’t do it alone, it has to be done with the permission of Rajya Sabha as well

          4. Here this Law made is not a Permanent Law, it’s a Temporary One, this Resolution passed by Lok Sabha the Resolution will be enforced for a period of One Year

          5. The Law Ceases to Exist after 6 months

          6. The State can also make Laws on this item of concurrent list but there has to be consistency in it or the law of Parliament will prevail

        • Art 250: Power of Parliament to make laws during Proclamation of National Emergency
          1. National Emergency is under Art 352 but Power of Legislature to Make Laws during National Emergency is under Art 250
          2. During National Emergency Parliaments will have the power to legislate on any item in State List
          3. Whenever the National Emergency is Revoked, The Centre can make laws on matters in State List
          4. But only six months after the Revocation of National Emergency
          5. Note : Here Power of State Legislature is Not Deprived
        • Art 252: Legislate for Two or More State by Consent and Adoption of such legislations by any other State

          1. Here there is No National Emergency

          2. State here is requesting to make laws on State List

          3. Minimum of 2 States have to Make Such a Requests

          4. Done in cases where uniformity between 2 states is needed

          5. For Example: Wildlife was in State List until 1976

            It was later made part of Concurrent List

            Wildlife Protection Act 1972 was passed by the Parliament as the state requested the Parliament

          6. Two or More States Request the Parliament by = Passing Resolution by Simple Majority

          7. Now in the Future only Parliament will gave the Power to amend such law

          8. Also this will be a permanent Law

          9. The Law will apply to those states only

          10. Now that they want to adopt such a law they can adopt the Law

          11. But once adopted by a simple majority, it will be amended only by parliament

        • Art 253: Implementation of International Treaties
          1. Parliament Makes Laws to Implement International Treaties
          2. Here they don't even have to take permission from states for items under states list given that the condition is of Implementation of International Treaties
        • Art 356: State Emergency
          1. Here the Law made by the Parliament is a Permanent Law
          2. Parliament can makes laws on any items in the State List

      • What is the Procedure to Amend Schedule 7

        1. Special Majority - Absolute Majority + Not Less than 2/3rd of the Parliament
        2. State Ratification by Simple Majority of 50%+1 States

      • Executive DOP - Between Union Legislature & State Legislature

        Art 256: Obligation of States and Unions

        Executive Power of Every State Shall be exercises as to ensure with laws made by parliament and any existing law in that state

        Executive Power of Union Extends to Giving Such Direction to a state as may appear to the government of Indian to be necessary for the purpose

        Here States have to respect the Constitutional Direction of Union or else they can be given direction by centre to obey

        Here, Still they don’t obey, President can conclude, failure of constitutional machinery failure and can apply Art 365 for President's Rule

      • Financial DOP - Between Finances of Union & Finances of State

        • Introduction

          1. Each and Every Govt should have a major source of revenue - Prominently by Means of Taxation
          2. Art 265: No Tax can be collected in country except by an authority of Law
          3. Whatever Tax is collected by Union cannot be solely kept by Centre, But what a State Earns can keep it with itself and it will get something from Centre as well
          4. Body that will recommend the Govt the distribution of Resources within State and Centre is Finance Commission. It will also recommend distribution between states under Art 280
          5. NOTE : Concurrent List Items are made upon by Parliament but Implemented by States
          6. Resources Are :
            1. Taxation Revenue - Necessarily Need Law
            2. Non Taxation Revenues - May and May Not Need Law
              1. Auction of Natural Resources
              2. Fines
              3. Profit from PSU
            3. Borrowings
            4. Profit

        • Distribution of Powers to Levy Taxes on Union & State

        • Finance Commission - Finance Commission Act, 1951

          1. It decides the Distribution of Revenue between Centre and States
          2. FC consists of Chairman and 4 Members

          • Qualification to be a Chairman is

          • Latest Commission - 15th Finance Commission

            1. Any Other Matter Referred to Commission by the President: To Mobilise Funds for Local Govt by State Govt
            2. Centre - Divisible and Non Divisible Pool
            3. Centre Shares money with States from Divisible Pools

          • Issues with Grant in Aid

            1. FC is a Important Body in Fiscal Federalism
            2. Grant in Aid by the FC is being Given to States who are the Party from Centre and Not for those who are actually in Need
            3. Horizontal Distribution Parameters are Not Followed by FC Generally
            4. In the Latest 15th FC -
              1. 2011 Census - Used 2011 Census and More Weightage was given to 1971 Census
              2. Earlier 2011 Census Low and 1971 Census High Percentage
              3. 1971 Census Used High Percentage
              4. Central Govt Said, They Agree South may be affected by Census, but revenue sharing on the basis of Performance Indicators like
                1. MMR, IMR
                2. Digital Penetration

      1. Definition

      2. Example:

        1. Inter State Council

    • Competitive Federalism

      1. Competition between Federating Units
        1. Centre and State
        2. in Between States
      2. Its a Concept where units of Federation Compete with One Another
      3. Examples Are:
        1. Allocation of Funds by 15th Finance Commission on Performance Based Indicators
        2. Ease of Doing Business Report by World Bank, Helps in Foreign Investors
        3. Report by NITI Aayog: States Composite Water Management Index
        4. 15th FC Performance Parameters of States
        5. Competition Amongst States in Implementing Swachh Bharat Mission

    • Both Types of Federalism is by Relation of

      Cooperation : Is Largely Between Centre and State

    • Confrontationist Approach in Indian Federalism

      1. Indian Federalism has been lately example of it
      2. Examples

        1. GST Cess Settlement between Centre and States

        2. WB not Implementing Centre Funded Scheme

        3. Office of Governor being used as Carrier of Message of Centre

          Example: Tamil Nadu

        4. Usage of CBI on States by Centre

          States have withdrawn General Consent Between CBI

        5. Centrally Govt Changed the Rules of All India Services

      3. Issues Arise Especially when Centre and State are of Different Parties

  • Local Government in India (LGB - Local Government Bodies)

    • Three Tier Govt System added by 73rd & 74th CAA
      1. Urban Local - Municipality
      2. Rural Local - Panchayat
    • Background
      1. Given Constitutional Status by 73rd CAA, 1992 → Panchayat
      2. Given Constitutional Status by 74rd CAA, 1992 → Municipality
      3. They were there before 1992 but were not of constitutional status
      4. Municipalities are older than panchayats
      5. Lord Ripon is the Father of Local Government in India
      6. After 26 Jan 1950, Local Govt is Item No 5 in State List. Hence State can Legislate it. They used to decide to have a panchayat and municipality
      7. There was no uniformity in
        1. The Structure of M&P
        2. The Existence of M&P
        3. The Rules of M&P
    • What is its Significance & Importance
      1. Accountability
      2. De Centralisation of Power
      3. Gadhis View of Ram Rajya and Village Swaraj
      4. For Uniformity in Indian M&P
    • By the Narsimha Rao Govt. This amendments were brought

      1. States had to Establish M&P Madatory

      2. Strucural Uniofrmity was Brought in Panchayats

        Levels Were

        1. District Panchayat
        2. Intermediate Panchayat
        3. Village Pachayats

      3. Strcuture of Muncipality was Given

        Levels Were

        1. Transitional Municipality
        2. Municipality
        3. Municipal Corporation
    • Objective of Giving Constitutional Status
      1. Decentralising Power
      2. Bringing Democracy at Grassroot Level
      3. Ensures Accountability of Local Govt to Local People
      4. To Increase People Participation

    • 73rd CAA, 1992

      1. Introduced Part 9 as Panchayats from Art 243 to 243 O

      2. Inserted a New Schedule as Schedule 11 of the Constitution with 29 Functional Items

        Example: Fisheries, Roads, Animal Husbandry, Sanitation

      3. Art 243 G - Legislature ay empower the Panchayat to take care of any of the functional items

        1. They have no Legislatures
        2. Local Govt is an Item in State List
        3. State Govt has to make laws, from the 29 Items of Sch 11 for the Local Government to Make Laws on it

    • 74th CAA, 1992

      1. Inserted in Part 9A of of the Constitution

      2. Inserted Schedule 12 of the Constitution

      3. Functional Items for Municipalities is 18 in Number

        Example: Urban Planning, Urban Poverty, Fire Services, Registration of Births and Deaths

      4. Art 243 W: Legislatures can epower the Local Bodeis to Carry out any of the functions through an act of state legislature

    • Provision of Panchayats & Municipalities

      Note: In Case of Lok Sabha and State Leg Dissolved, there is a Re Election for the New 5 Year Term

      In Case of Village Panchayat, After Pre Mature Dissolution & Re Election, panchayat will be for Remainder of the Term.

      The Re Election will not happen only when the New Election Time is less than 6 Months

      Note: There is Difference Between Re Election and By Election

      | Panchayat | Municipalities | | --- | --- | | Art 243 Defitintion Rural Local Self Govt | Art 243 P Definition Urban Local Self Government | | Article 243 A Gram Sabha at Rural Level Compulsory Gram Sabha at Village Level

      Perform Such Function as the State Leg may determine by Law | | | Art 243 B: Constitution of Panchayats → Three Tier

      This Structure is a Must Exception: If Any State has Population Less than 20 Lac, it can directly have district panchayat without the use of Intermediate Panchayat

      Village: Intermediate: District: | Art 243 Q: Constittuion of Municipaltities

      Nagar Panchayat: Transitional Area Municipal Council: Smaller Urban Area Municipal Corporation: Larger Urban Area (Usually Population > 10 Lacs)

      Transitional Area from Rural to Urban, Like Agri Reduced & Industry Increased | | Art 243 C: Composition of Panchayats

      State Legislature May by Law Detrmine

      1. Composition
      2. Election of Members & Chairpersons

      Direct Elections(FPPS) for Members at all 3 Levels (Territorial Constitutencies)

      Chiarperson at Village Level (Sarpanch) (Depends on Law Passed by State Legislature)

      Chaiperson at Intermediate & District Level from the Among the Elected Members | Art 243 R: Composition of Municipalities

      State Legislature may by Law

      1. Composition

      4. SC / ST Reservation in Proportion to Population

      6. Women - 1/3rd Seats to be Reserved for Women in Case of Members & also Chairpersons

      7. OBC - Reservation of Seats & Chairperson as determined by Law of State Legislature

      Reservation for women and SC&ST can be Adjusted in the same seat | Art 243 T: Reservation of Seats

      1. SC / ST Reservation in Proportion to Population

      2. Chaieperson in Panchayat for SC / ST - decided by State Leg

      3. Women - 1/3rd Seats to be Reserved for Women in Case of Members & also Chairpersons

      4. OBC - Reservation of Seats & Chairperson as determined by Law of State Legislature | | Art 243 E: Duration of Panchayats

      5 Years, unless sooner dissolved, New Panchayat constituted for Pre Mature dissolution only for Reamining term

      New Panchayat to be constituted only when Remainder Period is at Least 6 Months | Art 243 U: Duration of Municipality

      5 Years, unless sooner dissolved, New Municipality constituted for Pre Mature dissolution only for Reamining term

      New Panchayat to be constituted only when Remainder Period is at Least 6 Months | | Art 243 F: Disqualification of Membership

      1. If he/she is disqualified under any law for election to state legislature concerned (Generally According to RPA 1951)

      2. If he is less than 21 Years

      3. If he is so disqulified by or under any law made the by the legislature of the state (may vary from state to state) → for Grievance an appropriate authority will be made

      4. Any Question of Disqualification may be referred to such authority or amy be determined by state leg by Law | Art 243 F: Disqualification of Membership

      5. If he/she is disqualified under any law for election to state legislature concerned (Generally According to RPA 1951)

      6. If he is less than 21 Years

      7. If he is so disqulified by or under any law made the by the legislature of the state (may vary from state to state) → for Grievance an appropriate authority will be made

      8. Any Question of Disqualification may be referred to such authority or amy be determined by state leg by Law | | Art 243 G: Powers Authroities and Responsibilities of Panchayats

      State Leg may by law provide such authorities to panchayat | Art 243 W: Powers Authroities and Responsibilities of Municipalities

      1. To Enable them to perform Institutions of Self Govt (Any or all items mentioned in 12th Schedule)

      2. State Leg by Law | | Art 243 H: Power to Imposes Taxes and Funds of Panchayat

      State Leg by Law Authorise the Panchayats

      1. Levy Collect & Appropriate Duties & Tolls

      2. Assign, Such Taxes, Duties

      3. Grant in Aid

      4. Constitution Fund (They can have ther constitution of funds by means of Law made by State Leg) | Art 243 X: Power to Imposes Taxes and Funds of Municipalities State Leg by Law Authorise the Panchayats

      5. Levy Collect & Appropriate Duties & Tolls

      6. Assign, Such Taxes, Duties

      7. Grant in Aid

      | | Art 243 K: Elections to Panchayat State Election Commission Appointed by Governor

      SEC Removal can be done in the same manner as the Judge of a High Court

      Conditions of Service cannot be caried to his disadvantage

      State Leg can make law for conduct of elections; Sub to Provisions of the Constitutions | Art 243 ZA: Elections to Municipality | | Art 243 L: Application to UT

    • Compulsory & Voluntary Provisions in 73rd and 74th CAA

  • Local Governance in Tribal Area

    • Provision of Fifth and Sixth Schedule of the Constitution
      1. President can declare an area to be Scheduled Area wrt Art 74
      2. A Tribal Advisory Council shall be established. On its aid and advice the Governor will be advised on how the administration of scheduled area has to be done
      3. Amendment to the Schedule 5 of the Constitution can be done by a simple majority or ordinary law. it is not an Amendment under Art 368
      4. Certain States Like Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura, Mizoram → Within State certain Tribal Districts are Protected under Schedule 6 of the Constitution
      5. Here in Tribal Districts there shall be a Autonomous District Council they can make their own Laws wrt Marriage and Other Social Aspects of Life
      6. Here Changes in Schedule 6 is not an amendment under Art 368 and it can be done by an act of simple majority
    • Will Panchayat System Extend upto Schedule 5 & 6 ?
      1. Parliament while making the Law did not apply Third Tier Government System to Sch 5 & 6
      2. Here PESA Act was Passed → Panchayat Extension to Schedule Areas Act, 1996
      3. Here Gram Sabha under PESA has more power than the Actual 73rd CAA Gram Sabha like
        1. Gram Sabha will decide Schemes and Policies
        2. Can Decided about Minor Forest Produce
        3. Can identify Beneficiaries
      4. PESA is applicable to only 5th Schedule Area → Major Objective is to Promote Self Governance
      5. Hence
        1. Normal Rural Area - 73rd CAA
        2. Sch 5 Area - PESA
        3. Sc 6 Area - Autonomous District Council

  • Problem with Regards to Implementation of Local Governance in India

    • Abbreviations Used

      SPV - Special Purpose Vehicles or Institution

      MPLAD - Member of Parliament Local Area Development Fund

      MLALAD - MLA Local Area Development Fund

      DPC - District Planning Committee

      MPC - Metropolitan Planning Committee

    For Implementation of Smart City Mission, MPC is needed. Here it is either Not Constituted or it is not empowered

    Here Rural Development Agency, is a SPV and it has bypassed Local Bodies

    Here Development may happen, but the Idea of Grassroot Democracy by Means of Elected Representatives at Village Level does not Occur

  • Model Case Studies for Local Governance

    1. Kerala Case Studies - For Panchayats
    2. Bangalore Metropolitan Local Governance Reforms - For Metropolitan Corporation

  • ARC Recommendation on LGB

    1. It recommended improvement in
      1. Finances - Funds
      2. Functions - to be empowered
      3. Functionaries - Skill
    2. It said that initially it might be time consuming but once developed it will be really useful

  • Constitutional Bodies

    • Meaning of Constitutional Bodies

      If something is mentioned in the constitution then it is not sure that it is a constitutional body

      Constitution may say that it is a Body that may be estab by means of Law

      Constitutional Body is a Body which is Directly Established by Constitution itself

    • Election Commission of India

      1. To Ensure Free and Fair Election

      2. Art 324 Says there shall be an Election Commission

      3. Responsibility is to Direct, Control and Supervise Election in our Country

      4. They have to carry out Elections of LS, RS, State Leg, State Leg Council, Election of President and Vice President

      5. State Election Commission → Election of Panchayat and Municipalities

      6. CEC & Election Commissioners as may be determined by the President time to time

        Here the Composition is NOT Mentioned in the Constitution

        By Conventional Practise, from 1989 we have on CEC and 2 EC

        Constitution only says that if there are other election commissioners, then the CEC may be made as Chairman of EC

      7. Privilege of CEC

        1. Based on Law made by Parliament → Position of CEC and Other EC is same
        2. He is the only Chairman
        3. The Decisions are taken only on the basis of Majority Votes

      8. Regional Commissioners

        1. President can appoint, if they want wrt Art 74
        2. for 4 to 5 states one regional commissioner

      9. CEO - Chief Electoral Officer

      10. Hierarchy

        1. CEC + 2 EC → Appointed by President
        2. Regional Commissioners → Appointed by President
        3. Chief Electoral Officer → Appointed by CEC

      11. Service Conditions are decided by Means of Parliamentary Law → Election Commission Act of 1991

      12. Removal of CEC

        He can be removed in the same manner as the Judge of a Supreme Court

        The Process has been made hard so as to make the position of CEC Independent so that he can conduct a Free and Fair Election

        Same Grounds of Misbehaviour and Mental Incapacity

      13. President with the Recommendation of CEC can remove the EC

        Hence EC have to maintain good relations with EC

      14. Case

        1. 1990 - President of India Appointed CEC and 2 EC
        2. Then President Removed EC
        3. Here there was no recommendation of CEC
        4. EC went to SC against President
        5. Here President was NOT Wrong according to SC
        6. as post was scraped and not they were removed

      15. Manpower

        1. They do NOT have Permanent Manpower
        2. It is the Responsibility of President and Governor to Provide Manpower
        3. Hence, Govt Teacher, State Police, IAS can be deputed by Pres and Gov→ During Deputation Election Commission can Punish them

      16. Detailed Process for the Elections has been made by Parliament by means of RPA 1951 and RPA 1950

      17. Criticism of Election Commission

        1. Election Commission of India wanted the Same Grounds for Removal of Election Commissioners like that of CEC

        2. as of Now Election Commission of India doesn't have permanent Secretariat

          Not even few permanent officials → There should be some minimal staff for the same

          There by Insulating other people from within the Govt in the Process of Election Commission

        3. Expenditure of Election Commission is MADE from the Consolidated Fund of India. It should be Charged Expenditure

          Even the CAG is Charged Expenditure

        4. There is No Cooling Off Period for the CEC or EC to get into some other posts

    • Comptroller and Auditor General of India

      • Introduction

        1. Art 148 of the Constitution Provides for Establishment of Office of CAG
        2. Appointed by the President by a Warrant under his hand and seal
        3. He makes the Government Accountable for each and every single penny that Government Spends. Hence Prevents Financial Arbitrariness of the Government

      • Why is it called so ?

        It is Merely a British Legacy

        He is NOT a Comptroller - Someone whose permission is needed to withdraw Fund

        In India he is merely a Auditor General → Who Audits

        In India Comptroller is → Parliament of India

      • Constitutional Safeguard

        1. Removal → Like CEC and Judge of a Supreme Court

        2. Salary and Service Conditions → By An Act of Parliament

          It is NOT be President

        3. His Positions cannot be and should not be varied to his Disadvantage

        4. Office Expenses, Salaries and Allowances of Members of the Officers, Charged on the CFI

      • Duties and Power of the CAG Art 149

        1. Decided by An Act of Parliament → CAG ( Duties Powers and Conditions of Service Act, 1971)
        2. Audit of Income and Revenue for Centre and State
        3. Audit of Receipts and Expenditure of Union and States
        4. Compile Accounts of Centre - CGA - Compiler General of Accounts
        5. He Performs Financial Audits but he can perform Audit of Wisdom as well - Where and How can it be spent better
        6. Audit of PSU and Government Companies and Corporation etc

      • Audit Reports Art 151

        1. Union Reports - President - Parliament
        2. State Reports - Governor - State Legislators

      • Criticism

        1. Language of the CAG - Very Technical
        2. Timely Report of the CAG
        3. There is NO Technical Qualification for the CAG. It's a very specialised Job for a Specialised Jobs
        4. There is NO Specific Body for the Appointment of CAG

      • CAG Is NOT Eligible for Further Appointment. He cannot be Re Appointed as well

    • PSC - Public Service Commission

      • Purpose

        1. It an Independent Body because it is entrusted with Responsibility of Most Important Officers
        2. There may be Nepotism & Not on Basis of Merit. If left to the Government

      • PSC for Union and State u/a Art 315

        1. Art 315 Says that there shall be a UPSC and SPSC - Constitutional Body

        2. Enables Parliament to Establish JSPC - Statutory Body if Established

          in 1996 there was of Haryana and Punjab

        3. On Request of Governor, With Approval of the President ; UPSC may serve any needs of the State

        4. Disciplinary Actions Opinion

        5. Recruitment to Central Government, All India Services and States

      • Appointment and Term of Office Members - Art 316

        1. Chairman and Members of UPSC and JPSC are appointed by President

        2. SPSC by Governor

        3. 1/2 of Members of Every Service Commission should have held ; at least 10 Years of Service ; either Under GoI or State at the Time of Appointment

        4. During Vacancy of Chairman, Pres may temporarily appoint another member as Chairman until new Chairman is Appointed in case of JPSC and UPSC

          Same for SPSC by Governor

        e. Term of Office

        1. 6 Years of 65 Years - UPSC
        2. 6 Years of 62 Years - SPSC & JPSC

      • Removal and Suspension

        1. They are Adjudicating Insolvent

        2. Engaged in Paid Employment outside the Office

        3. Physical Disability or State of Mind → Infirmity of State or Mind

        4. On Grounds of Misbehaviour, only after making reference to the Supreme Court

          Pending such enquiry by Supreme Court, the President (UPSC/JPSC) and Governor (SPSC) can suspend the Chairman or Members

          Governor can suspend but CANT Remove for SPSC Also

          SPSC Removal by President after SC tells to remove

      • Powers to Make Regulations and Conditions of Service (Art 318)

        UPSC and JPSC - President

        SPSC - Governor

    • Other Constitutional Bodies

      1. Art 338 - National Commission for Scheduled Caste
      2. Art 338 A - National Commission for Scheduled Tribes
      3. Art 338 B - National Commission for Backward Class
      4. Functions
        1. Policy Research
        2. Awareness About Rights
        3. Constitutional and Legal Rights Addressed
        4. Look into Violation of Rights
      5. Criticism
        1. They are Politicised in Function
        2. Their Recommendation are merely recommendatory

  • Statutory Bodies

    1. Basics to Know About
      1. By an Act of Parliament or State Legislature
      2. There has to be an Object or Objective
      3. What is the Composition of that Body - Not So Important
      4. Functions of the Body - Related to Objective
      5. Criticism are Generally Same
      6. Suggestions are Generally Same
    2. Important Statutory Bodies
      1. National Human Rights Commission, 1993
      2. Central Information Commission, 2005
      3. National Commission for Women, 1992
      4. National Commission for Minorities, 1992
      5. National Commission for Protection of Child Rights, 2005
      6. Lokpal 2013
    3. Criticism
      1. Politicisation of Appointment
      2. Toothless Tiger → Except for Lokpal & Central Information Commission → All are Recommendatory in Nature
      3. Irregularity of Fund & Lack of Financial Resources
    4. Suggestions
      1. Giving Constitutional Status can lessen the inefficiency of these commissions
      2. Merely Giving a Constitutional Status can't be the Solution
      3. For Politicisation Issue - There should be a Similar Select Committee
      4. Statutory Backing - For Govt to give it in writing that why any act has been done
      5. appropriate Backing has to be given by means of Political Will

  • Executive Bodies

    • Meaning

      1. Created by An Executive Order - Like by Cabinet Ministers
      2. by an Executive Order or Executive Resolution

    • NITI Aayog

      1. NITI Aayog Replaced erstwhile Planning Commission in India
      2. National Institution for Transforming India
      3. Its a Think Tank for the Government
      4. Gives no of Policy Proposal for Govt

    • CBI

      1. Premier Investigating Agency of Central Govt - Headed by Rank of IG of Police called Director CBI
      2. It is NOT Created by a Law but by Means of Executive Order
      3. Before Independence, During WW2 it was formed to investigate war corruption activities under ministry of defence
      4. It is under DoPT now
      5. They Derive Powers from Act called Delhi Special Police Establishment Act, 1946

      Home Work : Look at Reforms for CBI

    • ED - Enforcement Directorate

      1. Under Ministry of Finance
      2. Implements
        1. PMLA Act - Prevention of Money Laundering Act
        2. FEMA Act - Foreign Exchange Management Act

    • NCB - Narcotic Control Bureau

Representation of People Act

  • Introduction

    1. Art 324
    2. Art 327
    3. RPA Act was passed by Provisional Parliament as RPA, 1950 & RPA 1951
    4. Elections for Both Parliament and State Legislature

  • Why Two Laws were Needed ?

    1. Segregate so as to what the Election Commission has to do before the Election itself
    2. Two Sets of Laws for
      1. RPA 1950 - Before & After Conduct of Elections
      2. RPA 1951 - Actual Conduct of Election

  • RPA, 1950 - Not So Important

    1. Before the Actual Conduct of Election

    2. Consolidation of all order passed by Delimitation Commission for specifying Constituency

    3. To Prepare Electoral Role

    4. Allocation of Seats - House of People, Legislative Assembly and Council

    5. EC ensures how the seats have to be filled as a Single Member Constituency

    6. Preparation of Electoral Roles by CEO (usually senior IAS Officer) appointed by CEC

    7. CEO is assisted by DEO (District Electoral Officer - Generally DM / Collector)

    8. Eligible for Voting unde UAF subject to Disqualification Criteria

      1. You should be 18 Years of Age

      2. You Should be a Ordinary Resident of India

      3. You Should be a Citizen of India

      4. You Should be a Person of Sound Mind

      5. You Should be Eligible to vote under law and Not be debarred from Voting under Law for Time Being

        By Means of Active Case & In Jails

    9. Amendments to the Electoral Roll

      1. Earlier Cut of Date was only 1 i.e 1 January

      2. Now 4 Cut off Dates are there

        1. 1 January
        2. 1 April
        3. 1 July
        4. 1 October

      3. No Person should be Registered in More than One Constituencies

        Link Aadhar to Voter ID - to prevent duplication of votes

      4. Before EC was taking undertaking from the Person

      5. Home Work : Issue of Linking Aadhar and Voter ID

  • RPA, 1951 - Very Important - Questions Every Year

    1. Qualification of Membership of Council of States and House of People

      1. Elector in Parliamentary Constituency ; Not Necessarily from the Same Parliamentary Constituency

      2. From How Many Constituency can you contest an election from

        Earlier there was No Limit. With Amendment it was made to be 2 Only

    2. Member of SC / ST to contest elections from Reserved Constituency

    3. Qualification for Leg Assembly and Council

      Elector from any Parliamentary Constituency from that State

    4. Disqualification and Conviction for Certain Offences

      1. In Constitution

        1. Citizenship
        2. Insolvency
        3. Office of Profit
        4. Unsound Mind
        5. In case of Defection

      2. Shall be Disqualified on the Basis of

        1. Protection of Civil Rights Act - He Practised Untouchability
        2. Customs Act
        3. UAPA

      3. Only Fine - No Election for Next 6 Years

      4. With Punishment - No Election for Next 6 Years from the date of completion of punishment

        Hence if Punishment is of 1 Years then for 7 Years he cannot contest election

      5. Other than this Offences - Not Mentioned in RPA - Lily Thomas Case

        1. Imprisonment < 2 Years - No Disqualification
        2. Imprisonment Minimum of 2 Years → No Immediate Disqualification + Can Contest Election + Temporary Vacancy
        3. Imprisonment > 2 Years → Immediate Disqualification + No Election for the Next 6 Months

      6. Corrupt Practises - Raj Narayan Case

      7. Engaged in Govt Contracts leads to Conflict of Interest. Example Hemant Soren

      8. Disqualification - Dismissal for Corruption or Disloyalty - Cooling Off Period is 5 Years

        EC will recommend the President after the Quasi Judicial Process

        Govt Servants Disqualified for 5 Years

        Remedy within 30 or 40 Days to challenge disqualification in High Court

        Can go to SC

    5. Sections Dealt with are Section 8 & Section 9

    6. Election Notification by President for Council of States & House of People on recommendation of Election Commission

    7. Legislative Assembly and Legislative Council

    8. Duties and Powers of Administrative Officers

      1. CEO
      2. DEO
      3. Returning Officer - Nomination Paper Accepted Rejected
      4. Presiding Officer
      5. Election Agents of Political Parties

    9. Registration of Political Parties

      Cant do Deregistration

    10. Nomination Candidates

    11. Moral Code of Conduct for Elections

    12. Conduction of Pollings

    13. Counting of Votes

    14. Declaration of Results

    15. Vacation of Seats - Not to be a Member of Both the Houses of Simultaneously

      may specify rules; failing which seats in all legislatures become vacant

    17. Home Work : Amendments to RPA

    18. Home Work : State and National Party

    19. Home Work : Difference between Delisting and Deregistering by Election Commission

  • Pressure Groups

    • Introduction

    Types of Pressure Groups

    • Formal Pressure Groups

      1. Pressure Groups which have well established Institutional Structure are called Formal Pressure Groups
      2. Example:
        1. Industry : FICI, ASSOCHAM
        2. Trade Union : AITUC, CITU
        3. Professional Groups : IMA, Bar Council, Indian Bank Association

    • Informal Pressure Groups

      1. Pressure Groups that does not have well established Institutional Structure
      2. Example
        1. People Movement by Anna Hazare ( India Against Corruption) - led to Lokpal
        2. Chipko Movement
        3. Maratha Agitation
        4. Patidar
      3. Methods / Functioning of Pressure Groups - Add from PPT by Sir

        1. Electioneering

        2. Lobbying

        3. Propagandizing

          Ex: Anna Hazare India Against Corruption
